Reproducible from the concurrency rather than random - which is why capturing the output beats re-running and calling it a flake.

CONTACTS AND COMPANIES WERE CLEAN AND THE DIFFERENCE IS ONE HELPER. `/api/company-contacts` maps rows through `toContactResponse` and `/api/companies` through `toCamelAliases` (createdAt included), so every field their registry entries name arrives. `business-records` has a mapper too and it had simply grown field by field, which is how two timestamps stayed snake-only. When a page renders blank cells, check whether its endpoint has a row mapper and what that mapper actually lists - the spread at the top of one carries the raw column names and reads like coverage.

A HAND CHECK RECORDED IN PROSE IS A GUARD NOBODY RUNS (COP-M01, round 104). The line above is true, and it was established by reading both mappers ONCE - so it stopped being evidence the moment either was edited, which is exactly the liability this story found in the leads registry (four default columns the endpoint could never return, two of them marked editable). `crm-shell-nav-parity.test.ts` derives it for all three objects now, and **the two need different rules**: `company-contacts` maps through `toContactResponse` and spreads NOTHING, so every advertised column must be an explicit key, while `companies` spreads the RAW PostgREST row (`...company,`) and layers `toCamelAliases` plus an inline record literal on top - so a column is also covered when its stored name IS the camelCase key. That holds for `industry`, `website`, `phone` and `employees` and for nothing multi-word, which is the whole reason the alias map exists; expressing it as "no underscore in the column name, resolved against drizzle's `getTableConfig`" is what makes the rule fail on the next multi-word column somebody advertises rather than on the one that happens to be there today. **The explicit half of a contract can live in more than one place** - reading only the alias function would report a column the inline literal covers as missing, and a hard check that reports correct code is where a real finding hides, so the set is their union. **MUTATION TESTING FOUND THE DEFECT IN MY OWN ASSERTION**: the companies rule rests on the raw-row spread and I asserted `...toCamelAliases(company)`, a different construct three lines below it, so deleting the thing the rule depends on left the test green. Bind an assertion to the construct the PROPERTY needs, not to the nearest thing with a matching name. Same pass, same family: the filter check was `toContain('q.filters.' + key)`, which passes while the key is merely logged - it binds to the `.eq(` call now, and the one limit no source check can cover (a filter still textually applied inside `if (false)`) is written into the test instead of papered over.

`npm run lint` HAS NEVER PASSED AND IS IN NO WORKFLOW; `check:lint` IS THE GATE (round 104). Bare `eslint .` exits 1 on a **7,721-error repo-wide backlog**, so a story whose AC says "npm run lint passes" cannot be read literally - `scripts/check-lint.mjs` (PA-048) is the enforced ratchet and the thing CI runs, failing only when the TOTAL grows. Measuring it is where two traps meet: `npm run lint 2>&1 | tail -4; echo $?` printed **exit=0**, which is `tail`'s status, and this file already records that rule - redirect to /dev/null and read `$?`, every time. **AND THE RATCHET HAD DRIFTED 1,235 PROBLEMS BELOW ITS BASELINE**, last set at `595fdc4`, so many rounds of deletions had been improving it with nobody recording the gain - the mirror image of the regression it exists to catch, and it hides a real one exactly as well. The instinct this file gives for the typecheck ratchet transfers: **a big improvement is a parse failure until proven otherwise**, and proving it took two cheap checks rather than an assumption - eslint reported **2,125 files linted** (a full corpus, not a bail), and restoring the round's three deleted files from `HEAD~1` and linting just them accounted for **15** of the 1,235, so the drop was accumulated and not mine. Tighten a ratchet you find loose in the same pass; a baseline that forgives 1,235 problems is 1,235 problems of cover for the next one.

A NAV REPOINT NEEDS THE PARITY CHECK ITS STORY ASKS FOR, AND `check:unlinked-routes` IS THE EVIDENCE THE FEATURE WAS INVISIBLE. `/crm/leads` sat in `docs/unlinked-routes-baseline.json` while CRM-001..010's saved views, board toggle, advanced filters, column picker, bulk selection, inline editing and capped CSV export shipped behind it. The baseline entry clearing on the same commit that repoints the sidebar is what proves the link now exists; keep the legacy routes registered (COP-E03 retires them) and keep the hub's `matchPatterns` listing the old paths so deep links stay expanded.

TWO COLUMNS CAN MEAN THE SAME THING AND ONLY ONE OF THEM CAN BE TRUSTED (COP-B05). `meter_readings` carries the machine's lifetime counters (`bw_meter_reading`, `color_meter_reading`) AND a precomputed delta (`black_copies`, `color_copies`) against `previous_black_meter`. The delta columns DEFAULT TO 0, so a row nobody finished importing is indistinguishable from a month in which the machine printed nothing - and every volume, cost-per-page and TCO figure built on them inherits that. `shared/fleet-assessment.ts` derives volume from the counters and the days between two readings, falls back to the copy columns only when no counter is stored at all, and reports the `basis` on every line so a rep can say where the number came from. Four cases refuse to produce a number rather than guess: no readings, one reading (no period), two readings on one date, and A COUNTER THAT READS LOWER THAN IT DID (meter reset or machine swapped - a negative page count on a customer-facing document is worse than a blank). The same module's other rule generalises past copiers: A TOTAL BUILT OVER AN UNCOSTED ROW IS A FLOOR AND MUST SAY SO, because a saving measured from too low a baseline understates the saving and the customer can disprove it with their own invoice. Note also what was NOT reused: `print-cost-calculator-service.ts` is a BENCHMARK ESTIMATOR (industry averages, fleet-age multipliers, guessed supply and service costs) and belongs beside contracted arithmetic, labelled, never inside a total presented as what the customer actually pays.

A SELF-HEALING INSERT IS A SILENT DATA-LOSS MACHINE UNLESS IT TELLS THE CALLER (COP-B06). `insertLineItemRows` in the proposals edge fn retries with only `CORE_LINE_ITEM_COLUMNS` when an insert fails on a missing column (PGRST204), which is right - line items persisting beats the whole quote failing - but it logged the drop and answered 201 as if nothing happened. Every OPTIONAL column goes in that retry, so the day `replaces_equipment_id` shipped ahead of its migration, a rep could attach three serials, see a success toast, and have the links discarded with nothing on screen saying so. The response now carries `lineItemColumnsDropped` plus a warning naming what was lost. Generalise it: a fallback that quietly narrows what it writes needs to say what it dropped, or it converts a schema-drift error into invisible data loss. Two smaller facts from the same story. `equipment` has `monthly_payment` and `lease_expires_date` and NO buyout column, so remaining term times payment is the remaining PAYMENT STREAM, not a buyout - a lessor's buyout normally adds a residual, the two differ by thousands, and `shared/fleet-quote.ts` names the derived one `remainingPayments` and prefers `deals.lease_buyout_exposure` when a rep has recorded the quoted figure. And `proposal_line_items` is declared TWICE (shared/schema.ts and shared/quote-proposal-schema.ts, the WF-C-04 pair); a column added to only one of them reads as phantom, so add it to both and let `shared/drizzle-schema.ts` decide which one migrates.

AN UNAUTHENTICATED ENDPOINT THAT WRITES NEEDS A CONTROL WHOSE STRENGTH IS WRITTEN DOWN (COP-B14). `public-booking` has no JWT in front of it and it writes rows, sends email and puts events on a rep's calendar. `shared/public-throttle.ts` now guards it and its header states plainly what each piece is worth, because a control nobody graded gets trusted for more than it does: the RATE LIMIT is the real one (per source AND per page - the per-source limit is defeated by a handful of addresses, and a page floodable from a botnet is a calendar nobody can use), while the honeypot and the minimum fill time are CLIENT-SUPPLIED and stop commodity bots only. Four rules worth reusing on any public surface. IP IS HASHED, NEVER STORED - the bucket is a digest of address plus slug, because throttling needs "the same source again" and not who. AN UNKNOWN SOURCE IS THROTTLED, NOT EXEMPT: no address shares one bucket with every other unknown caller. A MISSING OR FUTURE CLIENT TIMESTAMP IS NOT SUSPICIOUS, since bfcache, an old client or a wrong clock would otherwise cost a real booking, and refusing a prospect is worse than accepting a bot. And store ONE ROW PER ATTEMPT counted over a window rather than a counter that is read-then-incremented - two concurrent invocations lose an update on a counter and let a burst through, appends cannot. Idempotent sweeps go the same way: `reminder_email_sent_at` is stamped in the SAME statement that selects unclaimed rows and that statement RETURNS what it won, so atomicity comes from the WHERE clause, one round trip covers the batch, and a concurrent sweep simply gets the rest. Two smaller things from the same pass: a cron-fired endpoint belongs on the AUTHENTICATED function when the public one routes its first path segment as a slug (`/booking/reminders` would be shadowed by a page named "reminders" - SUPA-024 with a slug instead of an id), and a test stub that ECHOES its input proves nothing about hiding, which is how "the bucket never contains the address" first failed on the stub rather than passing on the code.

A GUARD WHOSE COMMENT PROMISES MORE THAN ITS CODE IS WORSE THAN ONE THAT PROMISES LESS (COP-B01). `check:unlinked-routes`' header has said since the day I wrote it that "a redirect (LegacyRedirect) exists to be typed, not linked" - and nothing in the code implemented that, so retiring a path BY redirecting it reported the old path as an unlinked page. Implementing it resolved SIX baselined entries that were legitimate redirects all along (70 -> 64). The rule matches on the route's ELEMENT rather than a path list so it keeps working for the next one, and it carries a floor that FAILS rather than passes when the route table collapses to nothing - the vacuous-pass trap that guard's own NOT_A_LINK note already records. When you baseline something, read the code that produced it, not the header above it.

A CLIENT THAT ASKS FOR MORE THAN THE SERVER WILL GIVE BELIEVES IT GOT IT ALL (COP-I01). **IT WAS A CLASS, AND THE WORST INSTANCE CREATES DUPLICATE RECORDS.** Three more callers asked a 200-capped endpoint for 500: the deals board, `ProspectsPage`, and `Contacts.tsx`'s company picker - which holds the first 200, filters THOSE client-side, and when the typed name is not among them tells the user "New company X will be created as a lead". A company that already exists past the 200th is presented as new and gets duplicated. `npm run check:over-cap-limits` gates it, and TWO MUTATIONS shaped the rule: the first version assumed one cap for every list and reported `BlogCalendar` asking `blog-posts` for 500, which clamps at 500 and is correct, so the guard now READS which functions import `parseCrmListQuery`; and that narrowing then let the original defect through, because the board builds its URL from `config.apiEndpoint` and no literal path sits near the limit - a file resolving its endpoint through the CRM object registry counts too. **A KPI CARD COUNTED OVER ONE PAGE IS HARDER TO SPOT THAN AN INVENTED ONE**, because it is a real count of the wrong set: `Contacts.tsx` put the server's exact `total` beside "Active", "New This Month" and "Unassigned" computed in a `useMemo` over `contacts`, which is one page of 25. `GET /contacts/stats` answers all four as exact counts under the list's own ownership scope; match a free-text column like `lead_status` with `ilike` rather than `in()`, since 'Unqualified' and 'unqualified' are one status and an `in` list counts one and misses the other; and render an em dash while it loads, because a wrong KPI is worse than a missing one. `EnhancedPipelineBoard` requested `limit: 500`; every CRM list endpoint clamps to `MAX_CRM_PAGE_SIZE`, which is **200** (`_shared/crm-list-query.ts`), so the board rendered 200 rows, the per-stage badge on every column was wrong, the column money totals were computed over a subset, and nothing said so - at this story's own 5,000-record target the board showed 4% of the pipeline. The endpoint was never the problem: it returns an exact `count` filtered the same way the rows are, and the queryFn threw the envelope away (`result?.records ?? result?.data ?? []`), which is PA-040's shape again - the data to tell the truth was in the response and nobody read it. `shared/board-truncation.ts` holds the page size, LOCKED to the server's constant by a test that PARSES it rather than copying it, and `boardTruncation()` refuses to claim anything it cannot know: no count means no claim (guessing `loaded === limit ? 'probably more'` reports a truncation on a board holding exactly 200), `total < loaded` is the count and the rows disagreeing rather than a gap, and NaN renders nothing instead of "Showing NaN of". When a list IS truncated, say which numbers on the screen describe only the subset - "showing 200 of 1,431" alone leaves a rep reading the column badges as pipeline figures. Second defect on the same path, worth recognising anywhere a mutation is optimistic: the success toast fired in the drag handler right after `.mutate()`, so a failed move raised "Stage updated" and then "Failed to update stage", with the optimistic one arriving first and reading as the outcome. A success message belongs in `onSuccess`.

A BENCHMARK SEEDED WITH ONE TENANT MEASURES NOTHING ANYBODY EXPERIENCES (COP-I01). `npm run bench:crm` (`scripts/bench-crm-lists.mjs`) times the real CRM list and board query shapes against a seeded Postgres and fails over a budget. Its first cut put 5,000 rows in one tenant, an empty table otherwise, and reported 2-6ms with a SEQUENTIAL SCAN on every query - scanning 5,000 rows is cheap, so the number was true and useless. THE REAL SHAPE IS MULTI-TENANT: a tenant with 5,000 accounts sits inside a table holding every other tenant's, and without a tenant-scoped index the scan is charged for all of them. Re-seeded with 95,000 rows across 40 other tenants the same queries took 11.8-16.4ms, still all scans; with the indexes, 1.8-3.8ms and no scan. What matters is not the millisecond count but which quantity the cost scales with. THE FINDING IT PRODUCED: **`companies` had no index at all, not even on `tenant_id`**, and that is the table the primary CRM account list reads in production. `business_records` and `deals` had BARE indexes on `created_at`, `owner_id` and `expected_close_date` - a bare index orders or groups the WHOLE table, so it cannot serve "this tenant's newest hundred", which is the query every one of these screens issues. All are tenant-first composites now (migration `0095`), held by `server/tests/unit/crm-list-indexes.test.ts` against drizzle's own `getTableConfig`, which asserts the leading column IS the tenant - a composite that leads with anything else looks right and narrows nothing. Two operational notes: drizzle emits plain `CREATE INDEX`, which takes a write lock, so on a large production table run the `CONCURRENTLY` form by hand and baseline the migration instead; and a benchmark's header must state what it does NOT measure (here: PostgREST, network, serialisation, render) or a green run gets quoted as end-to-end proof.
**CORRECTED 2026-09-20 (COP-B01 round 68): THAT BENCHMARK COULD NOT RUN, so the millisecond figures above are not reproducible from the committed script.** `deals.created_by_id` is NOT NULL with no default and the seed omitted it, so `npm run bench:crm` died on its first INSERT - proven by replaying all 80 journalled migrations into a scratch Postgres 16 in this container (the COP-M07 recipe) and running it. A benchmark that cannot execute is the performance equivalent of a guard that passes vacuously, and this one was already being quoted as evidence, here and in COP-I01's own AC1. Fixed, extended with the six My Day query shapes, and re-measured at this repo's stated target of 5,000 tenant rows inside 100,000 total: the CRM list and board shapes run 2.9-24.4ms and the My Day shapes 1.2-2.6ms, with no sequential scans. At ten times the volume the My Day shapes are 9.7-24.7ms, scaling linearly the way a tenant index does. `server/tests/unit/bench-my-day-coverage.test.ts` is the guard that was missing: it derives every NOT NULL column with no default from drizzle's `getTableConfig` and asserts the seed carries it, so the next schema change that adds one fails a test rather than the script. THE GENERAL RULE: when a story's evidence is a number, re-run the thing that produced it before building on it - the same instruction this file already gives about story notes, applied to tooling. And a benchmark's scope line is load-bearing in both directions: 2.6ms against a 1,000ms bar proves the database is not what would break the page, and proves nothing about PostgREST, the network, serialisation or render, which is why COP-B01 AC5 and COP-I01 AC1 stay open against a deployed environment rather than against anything buildable in a container.

AN IMPORT IS NOT A GATE, AND A LIST WITHOUT REASONS READS THE SAME WHETHER IT WAS DECIDED OR OVERLOOKED (SEC-EDGE-001). Two lessons from one pass over `check:edge-rbac`. FIRST: it counted an IMPORT of `_shared/rbac.ts` as enforcement. That module also exports `getRoleLevel`/`resolveRoleLevel`, which ANSWER "what level is this caller" so a handler can shape a response - COP-B01's My Day layout uses exactly that - so importing it made a function read as restricted while rejecting nobody, and this story's own AC1 ("exactly three edge functions import it") was counting imports. The rule now needs an ENFORCING call OR a level comparison that leads to a rejection, because `device-monitoring` and `erp-integration` do `getRoleLevel(...)` then `if (roleLevel < 5) return 403`, which is enforcement without the helper. That found `dashboard-widgets`, which passes the role INTO an RPC as a parameter and rejects nobody - added to a shrink-only list with the correction recorded, since fixing a misclassification is not a regression. This is the same shape as the guard's own older note about `app_metadata?.permissions`: reading a claim is not enforcing it. SECOND: `docs/edge-rbac-triage.json` gives every baselined function a verdict and a reason, and the check REFUSES a baselined function with no entry. Six verdicts, three of them DERIVED from the tree rather than asserted (public = never calls auth.getUser; internal = pg_cron posts to it; headless = no caller in any client tree), and `unexamined` is a permitted verdict - that is the point, because "nobody has looked" said out loud beats it hiding inside a flat list. Any long baseline in this repo deserves the same treatment: `docs/nplus1-triage.json` did it first, and the value is separating the entries that cannot matter yet from the ones that are a worklist.

A VERDICT REASONED FROM A FUNCTION'S NAME IS THE SAME UNDIFFERENTIATED LIST, WEARING A CATEGORY (SEC-EDGE-001). One pass classified eleven edge functions as `needs-gate` from what they were called; the next pass read them and overturned or narrowed six. `ai-employees` has NO writes at all and is read-only behind an alwaysVisible page - "autonomous agent configuration" was the name talking. `mobile-logs` is a log INGEST endpoint that anticipates an anonymous caller and carries per-device rate limiting (CR-004), so a role gate breaks app logging. `voice-agent` does not place calls - the real-time stack is unbuilt - it ingests call-end summaries, so the call LOG and `purge-recordings` are what want gating. `social-media` generation is fine for a rep behind an alwaysVisible page; `POST /posts/:id/publish` is the privileged act, so the gate belongs on the BRANCH and gating the function would have broken the feature. The general rules: gate the branch that does the privileged thing rather than the file that contains it; a function whose page is `alwaysVisible` cannot be gated above that without breaking the page, so the nav rule is evidence about the right level (CLAUDE.md's "mirror navigation-permissions" cuts both ways); and when a triage entry is corrected, write CORRECTED and the evidence into it, so the next reader checks the reading instead of trusting the category.

THE BRANCH-LEVEL GATE IS THE NORMAL SHAPE, NOT THE EXCEPTION (SEC-EDGE-001). Three of the last four edge functions gated serve a READ a rep legitimately needs and a WRITE only a manager should have, so a file-level gate would have taken the read with it and broken the feature: `social-media` generates copy for any rep behind an alwaysVisible page but `POST /posts/:id/publish` posts on the tenant brand; `qbr` reads are a rep's own work on their own account while generating, editing and deleting speak for the dealer to a customer; `web-forms` reads stay open while a definition change decides what the outside world sees. `voice-agent`'s `POST /purge-recordings` is a fourth shape - its own header calls it a cron endpoint, so the gate is EITHER the scheduler's token (`isCronRequest`) OR a manager, while the call log stays open because it is a rep's record of their own conversations. Define `requireManager()`/`denyManager()` once after the tenant resolution and call them at the top of the privileged branches; `check:edge-rbac` counts the file as gated either way, so the ratchet does not push you toward the blunter instrument. One operational note that has now bitten three times in one session: a line-keyed baseline (`docs/nplus1-triage.json` keys loops as `file.ts:line`) DRIFTS whenever you insert code above the line, and the fix is to update the key rather than silence the guard.

A ROLE GATE DOES NOT CLOSE SELF-APPROVAL (SEC-EDGE-001). `POST /deal-desk/requests/:id/decision` wrote `final_decision_by = user.id`, set `status: 'approved'` and stamped the timestamp with NO check that the caller was an approver and none that they were not the REQUESTER - while `approval_requests.approvers` sat there as a jsonb list nothing consulted. The quote guardrails (QUOTE-006/016) route exactly the over-policy discounts into that queue, so a rep could raise the request the policy demanded and grant it themselves in the next call. The fix is TWO controls and the second is the one that matters: a MANAGER level check, AND the requester refused whatever their rank, because a manager approving their own discount is still self-approval. Place the refusal BEFORE the update - one that runs after the write is not a refusal - answer 403 rather than 400, and keep writing `final_decision_by` so the audit trail survives the fix. Wherever this repo has an approval, a review or a sign-off, ask the same question: is the actor allowed to be the subject? `server/tests/unit/deal-desk-self-approval.test.ts` locks it by reading the source, since nothing typechecks the edge tree, and asserts the ORDERING as part of the property. Related triage rule from the same pass: a function with ZERO writes is usually open-by-design, but `ai-gpt5` is the exception worth knowing - no writes, yet every call costs money at OpenAI and any tenant member can make one, so the control it wants is a spend cap or rate limit rather than a role.

A CALLER-SUPPLIED ID IS AN AUTHORIZATION DECISION WEARING A FILTER'S CLOTHES (SEC-EDGE-001). `?userId=`, `?customerId=`, `?repId=` and `body.userId` are all over the edge tree, and most of them are honest list filters. The ones that are not look identical: `const userId = url.searchParams.get('userId') ?? user.id` reads as a sensible default and is the whole access check. `daily-briefing` had it twice - the list served any colleague's briefing, and `POST /generate` accepted `body.userId`, which INSERTS a `daily_briefing_log` row against that person and EMAILS them, so the write side was the worse half and the more innocent-looking line. `crm`'s `/analytics/conversion` had it over `sales_metrics`, which is per-rep conversion and activity numbers. **THE FIX IS ORDER, NOT REFUSAL.** These overrides are real features - a manager reading a report's briefing - so resolve the caller's tier with `resolveScope` and require the requested id to be inside it (403 OUT_OF_SCOPE), or on a list apply `applyUserScope` FIRST and let the query parameters filter on top. A parameter applied before the scope is a filter the caller controls; applied after, it is a filter inside a box they do not. `commission` is the reference implementation (WF-R-05 scopes before its `employeeId` filter); `supabase/functions/daily-briefing/index.ts`'s `scopeAllows()` is the override-check shape. Put the check ABOVE the lookup of the target row - one that runs after has already leaked whether that id exists. Grep for the class with `searchParams.get('(userId|user_id|repId|ownerId|assignedTo|assigned_to)')` and read what the answer DECIDES, not what it filters.

A TRIAGE VERDICT IS ONLY AS GOOD AS THE PATHS YOU READ (SEC-EDGE-001). `daily-briefing` carried my own earlier `open-by-design` verdict in `docs/edge-rbac-triage.json`, reasoned from its preferences and list handlers. `handleGenerate` was never opened, and that is where the defect was. A verdict from reading some paths is indistinguishable in the file from one that read all of them, so an entry now records WHICH paths were read - the same discipline the triage file already applies to "unexamined", one level finer.

AN UNDECLARED TABLE MAKES `check:phantom-cols` SKIP EVERY COLUMN ON THAT CHAIN, SO A PHANTOM TABLE CONCEALS A PHANTOM COLUMN (SEC-EDGE-001 batch 13). `supabase/functions/today-dashboard/` is what the iOS Today screen calls (`APIEndpoint.swift:305`). WF-L-06 fixed ONE of its six reads and left five carrying the same defect, all of them discarding their error, so the screen showed a quiet day: `activities`, `leads` and `deal_desk_requests` are tables in no schema (a lead is a `business_records` row with `record_type = 'lead'`; deal-desk writes `approval_requests`, whose pending set is `['pending','in_review']` everywhere it asks), `users.full_name` is not a column and its embed took the whole activity query down, and `tasks.status = 'pending'` is **a real column on a real table holding a value no writer has ever stored** - the vocabulary is todo/in_progress/completed/cancelled. That last one is invisible to every guard here: a literal compared to a real column typechecks and passes `check:phantom-cols`, and only reading the writers finds it. `docs/phantom-tables-baseline.json` had listed three of the tables against this exact file the whole time, and nobody joined "this table does not exist" to "therefore the home screen is empty" - so **tighten a baseline in the same commit as the fix**, or the assertion is trusted rather than evaluated. Two smaller rules from the same pass. `.or()` IS A DISJUNCTION: `.or('due_date.gte.today,due_date.lt.tomorrow')` is true of every row with a due date at all, so it read as a day filter and was not one, and the impossible status filter was the only thing hiding it - fixing either alone would have put the entire backlog under "due today". And a count of a family whose read FAILED is null, not 0, with the failed families named in a `degraded` array: a 0 is a measurement saying the day is empty. Same class one function over: `parts-orders` embedded `vendor:vendor_id (id, name)` when `vendors` has `vendor_name` (500 on the list) and `ordered_by_user:ordered_by (...)` when COP-M01 had already recorded that `parts_orders` has no `ordered_by` column - and that branch answers 404, so opening a parts order reported the order does not exist.

A CONSOLIDATION'S PR 2 NEVER SHIPPED, AND ITS ROUTING PREMISE WAS NEVER BUILT (SEC-EDGE-001 batch 14). `supabase/functions/lead-assignment/` is the canonical dispatcher for lead routing and its own header lists NINE auxiliary edge functions to delete "once PR 2 ships"; `docs/lead-assignment-parity.md` has the endpoint matrix and the soak-then-delete plan. PR 1 landed and PR 2 did not, so seven duplicates sat beside it, every one naming columns its tables do not have - `rep_capacity` has `max_active_leads`/`current_active_leads` not `max_leads`/`current_leads`, `lead_assignment_history` has `assigned_to`/`assigned_by` not the `*_id` spellings its embeds used, `users` has `first_name`/`last_name` not the `full_name` six of them embedded (and **an embed that cannot resolve takes the whole query down, not one field**). **THE ROUTING NEVER EXISTED**: the parity doc says the router "fans out to the canonical edge function based on prefix" and nothing implemented it, so `server.ts` resolved `/api/rep-capacity` to the rep-capacity DIRECTORY and the canonical handler was unreachable under every name it claims. Deleting the seven on the header's word alone would have taken seven prefixes from a broken function to no function, so the `server.ts` alias ships in the same commit with `stripSegments = 0` - the leases and reports precedent, because `lead-assignment`'s `PREFIX_MAP` keys on segment 0 and stripping it 404s inside the dispatcher. **TWO OF THE NINE ARE KEPT AND CHECKING THAT WAS THE WHOLE RISK**: `sales-territories` and `auto-lead-routing` have live callers, and COP-B09 wired the first and fixed its seven phantom columns AFTER the parity audit was written. A migration plan describes the tree as it was; execute it against the tree as it is, or a working page gets aliased onto a handler with a different shape (EDGE-005f). What genuinely disappears is recorded: `DELETE /lead-assignment-queue/:id` has no canonical equivalent, and `GET /user-assignments/:userId` returned a combined leads+deals+tasks+history view where the canonical handler answers history only - though three of that view's four queries were phantom, so the loss is the intent, not a feature.

A REGISTERED ROUTER CAN BE INVISIBLE TO A GREP FOR ITS OWN FILENAME. `server/routes-lead-assignment.ts` is a THIRD implementation of these 21 endpoints and it IS mounted - `registerLeadAssignmentRoutes(app)` at `routes-registry:846`, with the identifier imported through `server/domains/sales.ts`, so grepping the registry for `routes-lead-assignment` returns nothing and the file reads as dead. `check:shadowed-express` sees it only because QUALITY-002 taught it the self-mounting `register*(app)` shape. **Grep for the EXPORTED FUNCTION NAME, not the module path, before calling an Express router unregistered.** Dev therefore serves these prefixes from Express while prod now serves them from the canonical edge function, with different response shapes - a parity job, not a deletion.

A DEV-ONLY BREAKAGE IS HARDER TO NOTICE THAN A PROD-ONLY ONE, AND `/api/auto-lead-routing` WAS BOTH KINDS AT ONCE (SEC-EDGE-001 batch 15). `AutoLeadRoutingDashboard.tsx` is routed and calls `/dashboard`, `/config` and `/rules`. Express served the first two and **had no `/rules` handler at all**, so listing, creating and deleting a routing rule 404'd on every developer machine while working in production - the usual split running backwards, which nobody files as an incident because production is fine. Its `/config` PUT was worse than absent: it logged the body and answered success without storing anything, so toggling routing settings said "saved successfully" and lost them on reload (the edge function stores them under `tenants.metadata.autoLeadRouting`). **The fix is a `crmProxies` entry, not new Express handlers** - dev then runs the handler production runs - and the two preconditions were checked rather than assumed: the prefix IS migrated (the edge fn covers `/rules` x4, `/dashboard`, `/config` GET+PUT, `POST /route[/:leadId]`), and the `/dashboard` response keys were compared one by one against what the page reads, because a proxy entry changes what dev answers and a wrong shape beats a 404 for damage. What a proxy drops gets named: `POST /route-batch` and `GET /preview/:leadId`, neither with a caller. `server/routes-auto-lead-routing.ts` and `server/routes-lead-assignment.ts` (16 handlers over six uncalled `edge-too` prefixes, 15 of 16 covered by the canonical function) are both deleted.

A PHANTOM COLUMN CAN MAKE A RECOMMENDATION ARBITRARY WITHOUT MAKING IT FAIL (SEC-EDGE-001 batch 16). `renewal-playbooks` picked a playbook with `(playbooks || []).find((p) => p.risk_levels?.includes(renewal.risk_level)) || playbooks?.[0]`. `risk_levels` is not a column - the table has `trigger_conditions` - so the optional chain was undefined on every row, `find()` never matched, and the fallback returned THE FIRST PLAYBOOK IN THE LIST under the key `recommendedPlaybook`, as though it had been matched against the renewal's risk. No error, no empty state, just arbitrary selection wearing matching logic's clothes. **When a `find()` sits in front of a `||` fallback, check that the predicate can ever be true**; the fallback is what converts a dead predicate into a plausible answer. It matches `trigger_conditions.riskLevels` now, prefers an `is_default` row when nothing matches, answers null when neither fits, and returns `recommendationBasis` saying which of the three happened.

WHEN A PHANTOM-COLUMN CLUSTER IS ONE FEATURE, ASK WHICH HALF RUNS (SEC-EDGE-001 batch 16). Sixteen of the 41 entries in `docs/phantom-columns-baseline.json` belonged to the four renewal-management edge functions, and AUDIT-026 had already triaged the feature: a complete renewal model with its own schema, never wired to a screen, beside a different renewal model that was, with connecting-or-retiring filed as a product call. **That call was never the defect.** The Express half (`server/routes-renewal-management.ts`) writes real columns through Drizzle; the edge half, which production resolves these prefixes to, could not execute a single statement - `renewal_activities` stores `activity_subject`/`activity_description`/`performed_by`/`activity_date`, `expansion_opportunities` has no name/potential_value/probability/products/won_at/lost_at, `renewal_playbooks` has `playbook_name`. Correcting columns inside an undecided feature is cheap and keeps the code honest whichever way the product call goes; deleting it would decide the question by accident. Two rules the fixes encode and both generalise: a table with NO TITLE COLUMN refuses a `name` rather than filing it under a field that means something else (COP-B06 - a write that quietly narrows what it stores turns a schema mismatch into invisible data loss), and a numeric `probability` is COERCED into the `low|medium|high` vocabulary by band and reported, never dropped (COP-B00). The other half of that cluster went the opposite way: `projects-enhanced` was a GET+POST subset of the reachable full-CRUD `projects` function over the same table, and `roles` plus `role-management` both filtered the GLOBAL `roles` table on `tenant_id`, **a column it does not have**, so both were 42703 in every branch while `rbac` already served the reads off `enhanced_roles`, which does have one. All three deleted; phantom columns 41 -> 15.

A PRESENCE CHECK IS A COUNT WEARING A PROPERTY'S CLOTHES, AND A MUTANT WILL PATCH YOUR COMMENT FIRST. Two survivors in one pass, both instructive. `expect(SRC).toContain('playbook_name')` was satisfied by the `ORDER BY` alone, so reverting the INSERT to `name:` passed - the same shape as counting `applyUserScope` calls instead of walking the sites, now the third time this session. And a mutation replacing `performed_by:` hit its first occurrence, which was inside the fix's own header comment quoting the broken embed, so the code was never mutated at all. **Bind a write assertion to the `.insert(`/`.update(` chain, and check WHERE a mutation landed before believing a mutant survived.** Two further failures were substring overlap in the absence assertions (`subject:` inside `activity_subject:`, `products:` inside `proposed_products:`) - anchor those on `(^|[^a-z_])`, or the guard reports the fix as the defect.

**DELETING A ROUTER IS NOT DELETING ITS SERVICE.** `server/services/auto-lead-routing-service.ts` outlived both routers because `web-form-processor.ts` imports it, and that is a live production workflow seam (`form.submitted`). Check the service's own importers before assuming it went with the handler. And when you unmount a router, comment the mount site and the domain re-export with the reason rather than deleting the lines silently: an exported `register*(app)` mount is invisible to a grep for the module path, so the next reader needs the identifier named where the call used to be, and a test asserting the retirement must match on the IDENTIFIER too.

SEVEN MORE EDGE FUNCTIONS CARRY THE `users.full_name` EMBED and all of them are in `docs/unreferenced-edge-fns-baseline.json`: role-management, expansion-opportunities, projects-enhanced, renewal-activities, lead-assignment-history, fleet, work-orders. Nothing is broken while nobody calls them, which is COP-B03's exact setup - run `check:phantom-cols` against the function before believing a page you just wired works. `rep-capacity` is the one worth knowing about beyond the embed: its error branch answers hardcoded capacity (`max_leads: 50`, `is_available: true`) at status 200, which is EDGE-002g's fabricated fallback wearing an OBJECT rather than an array-of-objects, so `check:phantom-tables` does not match it; and its writes have no role gate, on rows that decide where inbound leads route.

`check:duplicate-tables` IS AT ZERO, AND THE LAST ONE HAD A LIVE CALLER (AUDIT-037). `server/storage.ts` imported `location_history` twice - once canonically, once aliased `gpsLocationHistory` from a `gps-tracking-schema.ts` copy carrying eight columns the table has never had - and `getGpsLocationHistory` did `db.select()` over the alias. **Drizzle selects every DECLARED column**, so the SQL named eight that do not exist: 42703 on every call, proven (`column "altitude" does not exist`). That is the nightly mileage job, whose catch returned `{ totalMiles: 0 }`, so it recorded that every technician drove nowhere every night since it shipped - a failure indistinguishable from a quiet day, on a number somebody gets reimbursed for. Fixed rather than deleted, because the data supports it: `shared/gps-track.ts` sums haversine over consecutive fixes, and its four rules generalise to any track - SORT before summing (the query returns newest-first and a drift filter divides by elapsed time, negative on a descending list); DISCARD a segment implying an impossible speed (two fixes either side of a signal gap are not a drive); an EMPTY track is null miles, not zero (zero says they did not move, null says nothing was recorded, and the caller skips a null); and a fix with NO COORDINATE is dropped rather than read as (0,0), which is 600km off Ghana. What is not derivable is named: `stops` counted a `ticketId` column that does not exist so it was always 0 ("visited nobody"), and `trips` was `trips > 0 ? trips : 1`, inventing a journey. **Correcting a declaration is how a runtime 42703 becomes a compile error** - tsc reported every one of these the moment the duplicate went, including `routes-templates.ts` writing `dependencies: task.dependencies || []`, always the empty branch, so every generated template asserted its tasks were independent.

A GUARD IS ONLY AS GOOD AS THE DECLARATION IT CHECKS AGAINST (AUDIT-037). `check:phantom-cols` validates edge code against `shared/schema.ts`, so a phantom column IN that declaration makes the guard vouch for the bug. `proposals.customer_feedback` was added on the reading that migration 0000 creates it - it does, and 0002 DROPS it - and the `proposals` function's public accept/decline handler wrote it on both branches. Both were a PGRST204 (proven on a replayed chain, not inferred) and neither checked its result, so DECLINE silently did nothing and ACCEPT fell through to `upsertDealForProposal(forceWon: true)` plus `createContractFromProposal`: a customer accepting a quote produced a won deal and a contract for a proposal still sitting in 'sent'. **The evidence that let it in is the lesson**: the test asserted `read('0000_fuzzy_blizzard.sql').toContain('"customer_feedback"')`, and SEVEN other tables in that file have that column, so the assertion was never about `proposals`. Grepping a migration FILE for a column name proves nothing about a table - read that table's CREATE and replay every later DROP. Retired with it: WF-C-04's rule that a twice-declared table should have "both declarations carry it". It cannot work - `shared/quote-proposal-schema.ts` had drifted 15 columns from `schema.ts` while the comment telling people to keep them in step sat inside one of them, and all seven of its tables described the pre-0002 world. That file is re-exports now, `check:duplicate-tables` is 10 -> 3, and the three that remain (`tasks`, `projects`, `location_history`) are the same question unasked. One correction worth carrying: the duplicate had NOT blinded `check:phantom-cols`, which has resolved ambiguity against `shared/drizzle-schema.ts` since AUDIT-035 - the guard read the right declaration and the declaration was wrong, which is a different defect with a different fix.

A DUPLICATE SCHEMA DECLARATION WITH NO IMPORTERS STILL COSTS YOU THE GUARD (CRM-LAYOUT-001). `shared/reporting-schema.ts` declared `dashboard_layouts` as migration 0000 created it - `layout`, `category`, `is_public`, `allowed_roles`, `allowed_users`, `display_order`, `is_active`, `created_by`, all eight DROPPED by 0002 - while `shared/schema-dashboard.ts` carried the real shape. Nothing imported the stale one, so nothing was broken, and that is what made it expensive: `check:phantom-cols` SKIPS a table declared twice with different shapes rather than guess, so every column literal any edge function aimed at that table went unchecked for as long as both existed. Deleting it put the table back in scope; `docs/duplicate-tables-baseline.json` still holds ten more of the same question. **AND CHECK THE MIGRATION CHAIN, NOT THE FIRST CREATE TABLE**: reading 0000 alone said `layout` and `created_by` were NOT NULL and neither writer supplied them, which reads as "saving a layout has never worked for anyone" - a whole false incident, corrected by one `information_schema` query against a replayed chain. **A DISCRIMINATOR A USER CAN TYPE IS NOT A DISCRIMINATOR**: two live surfaces both stored "the one custom layout per user" filtered on `(tenant_id, user_id, is_user_custom)` alone, and the obvious fix was the existing NOT NULL `name`, which differs between the writers - except one surface lets the USER name their dashboard, so typing "Custom Dashboard" collides with the row being separated. A new `surface` column, with the backfill spelled out: `ADD COLUMN ... DEFAULT` labels EVERY existing row with the default, so the correcting UPDATE has to follow in the same migration or the column starts wrong for exactly the rows it exists to separate.

VISIBILITY IS A PREFERENCE, ELIGIBILITY IS A PERMISSION, AND THE ORDER MATTERS (COP-B01). `shared/my-day-layout.ts` applies the saved layout FIRST and the role filter SECOND: the reverse lets a stale layout from a demotion keep rendering a manager card. The role gate is re-evaluated on every READ against the live role level rather than baked into what is stored, so a promotion adds cards immediately and a demotion withholds them immediately, and a withheld card is REPORTED so the user can find out why it vanished. One subtle rule with its own test: a serializer must KEEP hidden items in the saved list - dropping them makes "hidden" indistinguishable from "never seen", the next resolve appends the card again, and the user hides the same card forever. Also: `dashboard_layouts` is ALREADY contested - `dashboard-widgets`' /user-layout and `dashboard/handlers/layouts.ts` both claim "the one custom layout per user" and both read `(tenant_id, user_id, is_user_custom)` with no surface filter, one with no ORDER BY - so a layout saved on one surface can be returned to the other. Do not add a third writer; that is an open defect, not a pattern to follow.

THE EXPRESS SIDE OF THAT HOLE IS NOW COVERED TOO, and five guards missed it for five different reasons. CRM-008 (priority 1, MARKED PASSING) shipped `record_layout_configs` and `/api/record-layout-config` on Express. `client/src/components/crm/RecordPageLayout.tsx` - its central deliverable, named in AC2 and AC10 - WAS NEVER WRITTEN, nothing calls the endpoint, there is no edge function and the prefix is not proxied, so the route 404s in production and runs for nobody in dev. `check:orphan-files` walks pages and components and a component that was never written is invisible; `check:server-orphans` walks FILE reachability and the router IS imported by routes-registry; `check:shadowed-express` needs a proxy to shadow it; `check:unreferenced-edge-fns` needs an edge function to exist; and `check:routes` classifies FRONTEND-REFERENCED routes BY DESIGN, so **"missingEdge: 0" means no CALLED route lacks an edge function, not that none exists** - that line reads like a stronger guarantee than it is. `npm run check:uncalled-express` closes it: 88 Express domains no client tree calls, 72 of them with no edge function either (dead on both hosts). Two classes because they are different problems, and the baseline is a WORKLIST - some entries are features nobody wired (PROD-008c's shape), some are deletable, and the guard does not guess. `csp-report` is excluded BY RULE with its caller named (the browser, via the CSP `report-uri` in server/index.ts) rather than baselined.

A ROUTE CAN RESOLVE AND STILL BE UNREACHABLE (COP-B10 / COP-B13). `check:nav` asks whether a nav TARGET resolves to a route. Nothing asked the reverse, and the reverse is a real failure: `/competitors` and `/playbooks` shipped with Route lines, navigation-permissions entries and NO sidebar entry, so the only way in was typing the URL. Two finished features invisible to every user. `check:orphan-files` was green too, because App.tsx lazily imports them so as FILES they are reachable - that is CRMX-016's loss ("a whole feature can ship behind three missing Route lines") one layer up. `npm run check:unlinked-routes` is the mirror: 70 of 293 parameterless routes have nothing in client source pointing at them. Only PARAMETERLESS routes count (a detail route is reached from its list, a redirect exists to be typed, a public no-shell page arrives from an email) and "pointed at" is deliberately generous - a sidebar entry, a Link, a navigate, a widget - because the question is whether a user can GET there, not whether it is in the sidebar. MUTATION-TESTED, and the first version was VACUOUS: removing both sidebar entries left it green, because `navigation-permissions.ts` names every gated path and that read as a link. Excluding the path registries is the difference between the guard working and passing while matching the wrong thing.

A FREE-TEXT COLUMN PLUS A STRUCTURED TABLE IS A RESOLUTION PROBLEM, NOT A MIGRATION (COP-B10, COP-B09). Twice now the same shape: `business_records.competitor_name` beside `competitor_battlecards`, and `business_records.territory` beside `sales_territories`. The obvious move - normalize the column onto a foreign key - means a migration deciding, UNREVIEWED, that 'Xerox Corp' and 'xerox' (or 'North' and 'N. Region') are the same thing, and destroying the original text if it decides wrong. Instead the structured row's name/code is matched against the stored string AT READ TIME through one shared normalizer (`_shared/competitor.ts`, `_shared/territory.ts`), so defining a battlecard or a territory immediately claims every row naming it and nothing is overwritten. An unrecognised spelling surfaces in a COVERAGE report the admin can act on with one alias - a smaller and far more visible failure than a silently mis-merged account. Two rules that came out of it: the normalizer must NOT strip distinguishing words ('North' and 'North Region' may be two real territories), and every roll-up keeps an EXPLICIT UNASSIGNED bucket, because omitting those rows is how a grouped view quietly stops adding up to the totals shown beside it.

A STORY'S OWN NOTES GO STALE, AND A RATCHET READS AS MORE AUTHORITATIVE THAN IT IS. Six times in one session a stated fact was wrong or out of date: five COP stories carried "[GATED: blocked on COP-M00]" after COP-M00, M04 and M07 had all passed; COP-B02 listed tasks and equipment as open after WF-P-08 and COP-M05 closed them; COP-B10's evidence line cited `business_records.mainCompetitors`, a column that is on the deprecated `opportunities` table; COP-M05 was complete and unmarked; COP-B09's BLOCKER note describes the PLATFORM territory model while every one of its ACs is about the TENANT one; and CRM-008 was marked passing with its main deliverable absent. CHECK THE TREE BEFORE BUILDING ON A NOTE - `ls` the file it names, grep the column, run the guard. It cost minutes each time and would have cost hours of building on a false premise.

`npm run check:stale-gates`: A GATE NAMING A BLOCKER THAT HAS ALREADY LANDED (round 57). Stories record what they wait on in prose, nothing revisits the line, and the next reader is turned away from work that became startable weeks ago. CLAUDE.md has carried this as a lesson since the session that found "five COP stories carried [GATED: blocked on COP-M00] after COP-M00, M04 and M07 had all passed" - and that was still true today: **six OPEN priority-1 stories were carrying it**, so a triage of the backlog reported twenty-four blocked P1s when a quarter of them were free. All seven findings (COP-B04, B05, B06, B09, B14, I06, SEC-EDGE-001) are cleared with the tree evidence written into each story, and clearing a gate is NOT a claim the story is done - the ACs were not re-verified, only the false "do not start this".

**THE TWO NARROWINGS ARE THE WHOLE GUARD** and the first run proved it: unnarrowed it reported 62 findings, nearly all noise. A PASSING story saying "depends on QUOTE-001" is recorded history and the dependency was satisfied; reporting it is where a live finding hides. And "depends on" / "prerequisite:" describe an ORDERING while "blocked on" / "gated on" / "waiting on" describe a story nobody can start - only the second kind costs anything. It is a hard gate at zero rather than a ratchet, because unlike a phantom column there is no correct version of this. It says nothing about the blockers it cannot settle: "needs the deployed database", "needs a telephony provider account", "needs a human decision" name no story id and are left alone, which is why the genuinely blocked P1s still read as blocked.

MUTATION TESTING FOUND DEAD CODE IN MY OWN GUARD. A skip for "a story citing itself" survived every mutant, because only OPEN stories reach that loop and an open story fails the preceding `passing.get(blocker)` test - the branch could never fire. Deleted with a comment saying so rather than left looking load-bearing, which is the same call the opportunity radar's `measured === 0` guard got. A surviving mutant is a missing test or redundant code, and saying which is the point. One test-side note worth carrying: the fixtures first used ids like `A-1`, which the pattern (2+ leading letters) never matches, so two cases failed against a correct guard - **make a fixture look like the thing it stands for, or it tests nothing the file will ever see.**

MUTATION-TEST EVERY NEW GUARD AND EVERY NEW SCORER, and treat a SURVIVING mutant as information rather than noise. Across this session's eight new pure modules, four surviving mutants each said something different: `healthy()` in deal-score.test.ts already scored 100, so every comparison test passed whichever way the factor pointed (fixed with a `middling()` fixture that carries a comment saying why); the competitor normalizer's punctuation-to-SPACE only matters when punctuation abuts a suffix ('Xerox,Inc.'), which no test covered (test added); `check:unlinked-routes` passed while matching the wrong corpus (registry files excluded); and the radar's `measured === 0` guard turned out to be genuinely REDUNDANT, since zero readings already yield zero revenue - labelled belt-and-braces in the code rather than left looking load-bearing. A surviving mutant is either a missing test or redundant code, and saying which is the point.

The blog platform's later stages are the largest instance (AUDIT-025): 37 `blog-*` edge functions exist, the admin UI calls 18, and 19 are reachable by nothing - no client tree, no alias, no cron, and no cross-function fetch, since `blog-jobs` runs its handlers IN-PROCESS and names no other function. All 86 US-BLOG stories are nonetheless marked passing, so the closure criteria never included "something invokes this". Check that before closing a story that ships an edge function.

MATCH URL SEGMENTS EXACTLY, never with `\b`. `-` is a word boundary, so `/api/pipeline\b` matches `/api/pipeline-config` and reports phantom callers - it gave 34 for one function while I was spot-checking. Stop the pattern at any character outside `[a-z0-9-]`.

A ROUTE REFERENCING A NAME IS NOT EVIDENCE ABOUT A FILE (AUDIT-018). `App.tsx:921` routes `/reports` to a component called `Reports`, which looked like proof that `pages/Reports.tsx` was live - but `App.tsx:135` is `const Reports = React.lazy(() => import('@/pages/EnhancedReportsHub'))`, and the 4-line `pages/Reports.tsx` was a redundant shim. The same trap hit `ExecutiveDashboard`, where three apparent importers all pointed at a different file of the same basename. Resolve the lazy import before concluding a page is reachable; `check:orphans` walks the real import graph and is the authority.

Consolidated pages supersede several predecessors each, and the successor's ENDPOINTS are how you tell (AUDIT-018): `ProductHubUnified` calls a strict superset of `ProductCatalog` + `ProductHub` + `ProductManagementHub`; `EquipmentLifecycleHub` matches `EquipmentLifecycleManagement` exactly; `IntegrationHub` supersets `IntegrationsManagement`; `EnhancedProductAccessories` supersets `ProductAccessories`; `EnhancedReportsHub` replaces `ReportsHub`; `Homepage` replaced `landing`. All are deleted. Compare endpoint sets, not names, before calling anything superseded.

Role dashboards are widget layouts now, not pages (AUDIT-018). `client/src/pages/dashboard.tsx` renders `RoleBasedDashboard`, which resolves an effective role code, loads the saved layout from `/api/dashboard/user-layout` and builds the page from `lib/dashboard-widget-registry.ts` (`DEFAULT_ROLE_LAYOUTS` covers COMPANY_ADMIN, EXECUTIVE, FINANCE_MANAGER, LOCATION_MANAGER, PLATFORM_ADMIN, REGIONAL_MANAGER, SALES_MANAGER, SALES_REP, SALES_SUPERVISOR, SERVICE_MANAGER, SERVICE_SUPERVISOR, TECHNICIAN, plus DEFAULT). The 11 hardcoded `pages/dashboards/*Dashboard.tsx` were its predecessor and are deleted. To change what a role sees, edit the registry - do not add a page. Note DIRECTOR and TEAM_LEAD are NOT seeded role codes (the catalogue has SALES_DIRECTOR, SERVICE_DIRECTOR, OPERATIONS_DIRECTOR, HR_DIRECTOR and LEAD_TECH); `rbac-route-helper.ts`'s `TEAM_LEAD: 2` is a level table entry, not a role row.

`check:shadowed-express` is now a hard gate at 0 backlog (PROD-008b), with 57 handlers RETAINED and individually annotated - triaged, not deleted. 48 of them are the two unconnected features (PROD-008c billing, PROD-008d documents); the other 9 are deliberate keeps, and `server/routes-universal-search.ts` is the one to be careful with: it is the proxy's NETWORK-ERROR FALLBACK, recorded at `edge-function-proxy.ts:307-311`, not a duplicate. Read the annotation before deleting anything this ratchet lists. The headline number always overstated debt: the small tail people assume is cleanup turned out to be entirely deliberate retentions and real features with no edge counterpart.

WHEN A STORY CORRECTS A RATCHET ANNOTATION, CORRECT THE ANNOTATION (CRMX-011a). `docs/shadowed-express-baseline.json` described `POST /api/business-records/import` as a "LIVE BROKEN FEATURE... the only implementation". Both halves were false: its only caller, `LeadsImport.tsx`, had no importers and was already listed in `docs/orphan-files-baseline.json`, and CSV import ships elsewhere - `/import` is routed to `CSVImportWizard` over `supabase/functions/import/`, which handles `business_records` and leads directly. CRMX-011a had established all of that two days earlier and recorded it; nobody updated the baseline, so the ratchet kept asserting the opposite, and a ratchet reads as more authoritative than a story note. Two ratchets also held complementary halves of the same fact - "live broken feature" and "nobody can reach this file" - with neither entry pointing at the other.

NOT EVERY SHADOWED ROUTER IS DELETABLE (PROD-008c, PROD-008d). Both of the large blocks in `check:shadowed-express` turned out to be complete features nobody wired, not debt - `/api/billing` (39 handlers) and `/api/documents` (9). `server/routes/advanced-billing-routes.ts` is 39 of the 61 handlers left in `check:shadowed-express` - meter anomalies, disputes, credit memos, billing schedules, invoice-generation logs - and unlike every other shadowed router retired so far it is neither a mock nor a duplicate: real tables in `shared/advanced-billing-schema.ts`, ~20 real storage methods, 1020 lines of Zod-validated handlers. It cannot run for three independent reasons (the proxy claims `/api/billing` first; the billing edge fn has no handlers for those five domains; and nothing in `client/src` references a dispute, credit memo, anomaly or schedule), so nothing is broken and nobody has lost anything - the feature was simply never connected. Porting it means building a UI too. Retiring it means dropping tables. That is a decision, not cleanup, and the file carries a header saying so. Before deleting a shadowed router, check whether its edge function actually covers the same endpoints; when it does, the deletion is safe, and when it does not you have found unconnected work rather than debt.

A prefix that is NOT proxied still 404s in prod when the edge function reads `parts[0]` as an id (SUPA-024). `DocumentManagement.tsx` is routed and calls `/api/document-management/library`, `/workflows` and `/search`. Dev runs Express, which serves them - twice over, from both `routes-document-management.ts` and `routes-sample-data.ts`. Production hits the edge function, where `const documentId = parts[0]` turns `/library` into a lookup for a document whose id is the string "library", and `.single()` returns 404. `docs/edge-path-coverage-baseline.json` already lists those three segments as gaps and its own note names this hazard ("a request that falls through to a generic :id branch"); nothing had connected the baseline entry to the page. Converging such a domain means ADDING the branches to the edge function - a bare `crmProxies` entry would make dev match prod by breaking dev too.

A SHADOWED ROUTER LOOKS EXACTLY LIKE A BROKEN LIVE ONE (QUALITY-002). `routes-security-dashboard.ts` had eight type errors from querying `audit_logs` on `createdAt`/`resourceType`/`details`, and `/security-dashboard` is a routed page calling three of its four endpoints - so it read as a live broken dashboard. It was not: `/api/security` is proxied, the proxy registers at `routes-registry:297` and that router mounted at `:848`, so it never ran, and `supabase/functions/security/` had already fixed the same column bugs and says so in its header. Before fixing a broken Express handler, check whether its prefix is in `crmProxies`; if it is, the file is dead and the fix is deletion. `check:shadowed-express` is the tool for this, but it had two blind spots now closed: a module that mounts ITSELF via an exported `register*(app)` function (the app.use is not in the registry at all), and a mount from a DYNAMIC `await import` (only static imports fed its ident-to-spec map). Those hid `routes-security-dashboard`, `routes-customer-numbers` and `routes-company-ids` - all three deleted, all three fully covered by their edge functions.

WRITE PRD NOTES FROM A FILE, NEVER INLINE IN A SHELL STRING. Backticks and parentheses inside a `node -e` note get eaten by bash command substitution: one note lost its code sample and read "a mount from a DYNAMIC import, , since only static imports...". Write the note to a scratch file and have node read it.

CHECK WHAT A HANDLER DOES BEFORE MAKING IT COMPILE (QUALITY-002). `routes-admin-workflows.ts` had nine type errors and `POST /api/admin/execute-action` returned a canned success for every action - "Database backup initiated", "Application cache cleared successfully", a security scan with invented findings - while doing nothing. In practice it 500'd, because its audit insert into `activity_reports` threw first, so the admin page showed "Failed to execute action". Fixing the column names would have turned that honest error into a green "completed successfully" toast on Suspend Tenant, Reset Passwords and Generate Backup. A typecheck batch that only makes types line up can ship a regression that way. The router was deleted instead; the twelve UI cards it served are AUDIT-022.

Public no-shell pages are NOT `<Route>` elements (AUDIT-014): `/p/:token` (shared proposal), `/f/:token` (hosted form) and `/book/:slug` + `/book/manage/:token` (CRMX-016 booking) are `pathname.startsWith(...)` early returns ABOVE the auth gate in App.tsx, because they must render with no app shell and no login. Each page parses its own parameter out of `window.location` rather than taking a wouter param. `check-nav-targets.mjs` now collects those guards as registered prefixes - before that it read the route DEFINITIONS as broken links, which is why `/p/` and `/f/` sat in its baseline as "known broken" while working fine. If you add another such page, add the guard and nothing else; a `<Route>` would put it inside the shell.

A whole feature can ship behind three missing Route lines. CRMX-016's booking surface had all three pages (41KB), both edge functions and both proxy entries, and was unreachable because `App.tsx` lazily imported the components and never routed them - eslint reported it only as "assigned a value but never used", which reads like dead code. When eslint flags an unused `React.lazy` import in App.tsx, check whether the page is meant to be routed before deleting the import.

Fabricated measurements are now a fourth guard (AUDIT-020): `check:no-random-metrics` flags an object PROPERTY whose value derives from `Math.random()` - `completionRate: 92 + Math.random() * 8`, `diskUsage: 45 + Math.random() * 20` - across `server/` and `supabase/functions/`. This is worse than the AUDIT-019 literals it complements: a literal is stable and eventually reads as a placeholder, while a random one changes on every request, which is exactly what real telemetry does, so refreshing appears to confirm it. Ids (`Math.random().toString(36)`), names built by template interpolation and jitter inside an `if` are excluded by rule rather than baselined, because a baseline holding known false positives lets a real offender hide. Baseline 34 across 8 files, keyed by file + property name; **the baseline is a todo list, not settled debt** (it is AUDIT-021) - `routes/performance-routes.ts` invents diskUsage/errorRate/throughput, and `routes-service-dispatch.ts` invents customerSatisfaction, onTimeArrival and the lat/lng it places technicians at on the dispatch map. Everything left in it is an Express file, so it is dev-only under the getApiUrl architecture; the two entries production COULD reach are already fixed - `websocket-service.ts` (not dev-only: ENABLE_WEBSOCKET is implied by `NODE_ENV === 'production'`) and the `meetings` edge fn's `/analytics`, which now counts `calendar_events` instead of returning 127 meetings and a 0.65 fatigue index. Blind spot, stated in the script header: the rule is line-anchored, so a single-line object literal escapes it.

CHECK REACHABILITY BEFORE FILING OR FIXING. AUDIT-020 was filed at priority 1 on the claim that server/integrations/dashboard-service.ts (since DELETED, so do not go looking for it) was live, from seeing that both it and `integrations/routes.ts` import `IntegrationService`. The arrow points the other way: NOTHING imported dashboard-service, and EDGE-005f had already replaced it in the `integrations` edge function months earlier, saying so in a comment there. The whole file was a 669-line orphan. `grep -rn '<basename>' server/ client/ supabase/ scripts/` before believing any severity claim about a server file - the same lesson PROD-014 taught when `routes-erp-integration.ts` turned out to be an unregistered duplicate of a live fixture.

`eq(col, null)` is always false (QUALITY-002): drizzle renders it as `col = NULL`, which no row satisfies. It appeared three times in the RBAC layer as `or(eq(effectiveUntil, null), effectiveUntil > NOW())`, so the OR collapsed to its second half and a grant with NO expiry - the normal shape for a permanent one - matched nothing. Use `isNull`. Two more in the same layer: `permission_overrides` has no `approvalStatus` column (approval is `approved_by` + `approval_date`), so an APPROVED override never granted anything; and `(await import('../db')).users` is undefined, because `server/db` exports the client, not the schema. All three fail closed, which is why nothing was ever reported. `server/tests/unit/rbac-temporal-and-approval.test.ts` locks them.

`storage.getUserWithRole` returns `role` as a Role ROW, not a string (QUALITY-002) - its type is `Omit<User, 'role'> & { role?: Role }`. `mfa-enforcement.ts` passed it to `parseRoleLevel`, which calls `.toLowerCase()`, so every request through `enforceMfaForAdmins` threw into its catch and answered 500 (harmless today only because the middleware is exported and never mounted). Read `role?.code` for the name and `role?.level` for the level - `roles` has a numeric `level` column, which beats parsing a name. Separately, `twoFactorEnabled` is on `user_settings`, NOT `users`.

CLEAR THE TSC CACHE AFTER EDITING A `.d.ts`. `tsconfig.json` sets `incremental: true` with `tsBuildInfoFile` under `node_modules/typescript`, and a `.d.ts` edit does NOT reliably invalidate it: four freshly-declared `SessionData` fields kept reporting TS2339 until `rm -f node_modules/typescript/tsbuildinfo`, at which point the count dropped 393 -> 383 with no code change. The failure mode is believing an augmentation is being shadowed when it is simply cached.

RUN `check:edge-rbac` WHEN YOU ADD AN EDGE FUNCTION. A new function with no role restriction is open to every authenticated tenant member, and this guard is the only thing that says so - it is easy to run a dozen other guards and miss it. Gate with a LEVEL check (`requireRoleLevel` from `_shared/rbac.ts`), mirroring whatever `navigation-permissions.ts` already applies to the page, NOT a permission code: per SEC-EDGE-002 the codes the Express gates name are not the codes any seeder creates, so a copied permission gate denies everyone below platform admin. GATE THE BRANCH, NOT THE FUNCTION, wherever a function mixes a surface every rep uses with a few writes that decide something tenant-wide (SEC-EDGE-001). A function-wide `req.method !== 'GET'` check is two lines and closes pages that are minLevel 1 on purpose - `chatbot`'s console, `contract-renewal`'s book, `company-ids`' lookup are all things a rep reads. The shape is a `requireManager()`/`denyManager()` pair declared once after the tenant resolve and `try { requireManager(); } catch (err) { return denyManager(err); }` at the top of each write branch, before the body reads a payload. `denyManager` answers 403 INSUFFICIENT_ROLE for an `RbacError` and RETHROWS everything else, because a catch that 403s on any failure turns a database outage into "your role is too low". TWO THINGS THE TRIAGE GOT WRONG FROM THE NAME, and reading the handlers is what said so: `address-books` was filed as reaching hardware on a customer site and does not - export hands back bytes a technician uploads, its page is minLevel 1, and a manager gate would have locked out its only users; `company-ids` kept a gate on three of six POST branches, because preview-slug, validate and next-code answer "what WOULD this be" while backfill rewrites display ids across every business record in one call. `docs/edge-rbac-triage.json` carries a verdict and a reason per baselined function so "open to all roles" stops reading the same whether it is deliberate or unexamined; CORRECTED 2026-09-20: `ai-gpt5` is NO LONGER on that worklist - it now carries the per-tenant rate limit it always wanted, taken from `ai-employee`, which had the pattern all along. The two entries left are `contract-tiered-rates` and `manufacturer-orders`, both deliberately ungated for the same reason: the honest gate level is their own page's, and their pages are open.

`system_integrations` has NO `config` column (QUALITY-002): it is `configuration` (API keys, endpoints) and `credentials` (OAuth tokens). Both `server/integrations/integration-service.ts` (LIVE - registered at routes-registry:401, prefix not proxied) and `server/services/oauth-token-refresh.ts` read and wrote `integration.config` throughout, which meant the OAuth callback's insert dropped four phantom keys and omitted NOT NULL `type` (so storing a connection always threw), every token read was undefined, and the refresh path's `.set({config})` reduced to `last_sync + status` while logging success - the refreshed token was never persisted. Its status vocabulary is `active | inactive | error | pending`; both files wrote `'connected'`, including in a WHERE clause, so the batch refresh matched nothing and the PROD-014a dashboard counted a fresh connection as neither active nor errored. `server/tests/unit/integration-service-columns.test.ts` locks the mapper against drizzle's own `getTableColumns`. THE GENERAL RULE: for server-side Drizzle code, tsc is the ONLY guard against a phantom column - `check:phantom-cols` reads edge functions only, and `check:spread-insert-keys` resolves array-literal spreads only. So a TS2339 cluster on a table type inside the ratchet is worth far more than its error count; it is usually a feature that cannot work at all.

Absence assertions must strip comments first. A test asserting `db.sql` or `/api/pipeline-config/templates` is gone will match the comment explaining why it was removed, and report its own explanation as the defect. This fired twice in one session (COP-E02, QUALITY-002) and is the same lesson `check:edge-coverage` already carries in its header.

Two stage vocabularies, one word (COP-E02): `pipeline_stages.id` is a `gen_random_uuid()` varchar belonging to the DEALS board (CrmDealsPage, `/api/pipeline-config`). `business_records.status` is a lifecycle string ('lead', 'contacted', 'closed_won') belonging to the ACCOUNT boards (ProspectsPage, SalesPipelineWorkflow, both of which board `record_type='lead'`, not deals). Comparing them silently yields -1, and `findIndex(...) + 1` turns that into index 0, so SalesPipelineWorkflow's "move to next stage" advanced every record to the first stage and PATCHed a UUID into `business_records.status` - accepted because the schema was `stage: z.string()`. The vocabulary now lives in `supabase/functions/sales-pipeline/` as `PIPELINE_STAGES`, is served by `GET /sales-pipeline/stages` so the page has one source of truth, and the write path validates with `z.enum` derived from that same list; `server/tests/unit/sales-pipeline-stage-vocabulary.test.ts` locks all of it. A status outside the vocabulary belongs to no column, so the board lists those rows explicitly rather than dropping them. Note `/api/sales-pipeline` cannot take a bare proxy entry: `routes-sales-forecasting.ts` owns `PUT /api/sales-pipeline/:id` over `forecast_pipeline_items`, a FOURTH stage model behind the same prefix, so the entries are scoped per path.

COP-B00 IS NOT A MIGRATION NOTE, IT IS TWO SCREENS DISAGREEING, AND IT BLOCKS COP-E02 (round 55). `/crm/leads` calls `/api/business-records`, whose own header calls it "a backwards-compatible wrapper that delegates to the companies table". `/sales-pipeline` calls `/api/sales-pipeline`, which reads `business_records` and never touches `companies`. **Both tables have live writers**, so the split cuts both ways: an account created through the CRM list lands in `companies` and never appears on the pipeline board, and **a prospect self-scheduling through `public-booking` is INSERTed into `business_records` with `record_type: 'lead'`** and never appears on the canonical Leads list (`leads/`, `customers/`, `apollo/`, `auto-lead-routing/` and `sales-rep-assignments/` write there too). So the board COP-E02 calls non-canonical is the only surface where some real leads appear, and deleting it per that story's AC5 would hide them - which is what its own AC3 forbids. `server/tests/unit/crm-table-split.test.ts` locks this and is designed to FAIL once the tables are reconciled, so the story unblocks itself loudly. **Before retiring any CRM surface, check which of the two tables it reads**; the endpoint name does not tell you, and `/api/business-records` reading `companies` is the exact trap.

A REDIRECT NEEDS A TARGET THAT CAN SHOW THE SAME ROWS. COP-E02's AC4 asks for `/prospects` to redirect to the canonical board with a preset saved view. There is nowhere to send it: the page boards `companies` filtered to `business_record_type='Prospect'`, the leads config in `crm-object-registry.ts` is pinned to `recordType: 'lead'`, and the companies config has `hasBoardView: false` with no record-type parameter on its endpoint (it takes `includeLeads`/`includeCustomers`). A canonical prospects view is the prerequisite, not the redirect. Same shape as EDGE-005f's rule one level up: sending a live surface somewhere that answers a different row set is worse than leaving it where it is.

WHEN A PRIORITY-1 STORY TURNS OUT TO BE BLOCKED, THE DELIVERABLE IS THE EVIDENCE AND A TEST THAT EXPIRES IT. Annotate the files somebody will open (the pages, not just the story - the next person to read "delete these pages" opens the page), state which story has to land first, and write the assertion so it FAILS when the blocker clears. A note alone goes stale; this repo has recorded six such staleness incidents in one session. The AUDIT-034 treatment - a test designed to fail the day someone fixes the thing - is the pattern for every "correct but not yet actionable" finding.

ERP integrations (PROD-014 / PROD-014a): `/api/erp-integration` is served by `supabase/functions/erp-integration/`, which reads `system_integrations` + `integration_metrics`. It is deliberately NOT folded into the `integrations` fn: that one serves the integration hub from `platform_integrations` / `integration_sync_logs` / `integration_webhooks`, the platform's connector catalogue, which is a different row set behind a similar name. The Express handler this replaced was a 670-line fixture (SAP Business One, 18 integrations, 98.7% sync rate) that existed TWICE, live in `routes-sample-data.ts` and as an unregistered duplicate in `routes-erp-integration.ts`; both are deleted, because porting a mock handler is the fix PROD-011 already ruled out. All aggregation is in the pure `_shared/erp-integration-dashboard.ts` (PostgREST has no GROUP BY, SUM or AVG), tested by `server/tests/unit/erp-integration-dashboard.test.ts`. Three rules it encodes: NULL IS NOT ZERO (no traffic in the window returns null, not a 0% success rate that reads as an outage); LATENCY IS WEIGHTED BY CALL COUNT (`avg_latency_ms` is a per-period average, so a mean of means turns 1 call at 1000ms plus 999 at 10ms into 505ms instead of 11ms - mutation-tested); and metrics rows outlive the integration they measured, so the tenant roll-up counts only systems still in the registry. Unanswerable fields are named in an `unbacked` array on the response and rendered by the page, rather than zeroed.

Fixture twins of working pages (AUDIT-019): before wiring a static-fixture page to a backend, grep for a SIBLING page on the same domain. Two of the four this story covered were duplicates of pages that already worked: `/ai-service-intelligence` against `/service/predictions` (the real predictive-failure engine), and `/predictive-contract-profitability` against `/contracts/profitability` (the contract-pnl engine). Both twins were deleted and their URLs redirected through the `LegacyRedirect` component already in App.tsx, because `client/src/data/emailCampaigns.ts` links at the old paths. When you retire a path this way, KEEP its `navigation-permissions.ts` entry and copy the same gate onto the redirect TARGET - a target with no entry of its own would otherwise be reachable on looser terms than the path that forwards to it. This is the BATCH 10 duplicate-pages lesson at route level rather than column level.

Meeting transcription (AUDIT-019 / LEGAL-009): the five `meeting_*` tables are DRIFT TABLES in no Drizzle schema, created by the hand-run `drizzle/rls/meeting-transcription-tables.sql`, so `check:phantom-cols` is blind to them and that DDL is the only source of truth for column names. `supabase/functions/meeting-transcription/` was complete and had ZERO callers, which is how a dispatcher that never routed `/recordings/:id/consent` survived: the handler implemented it for LEGAL-009, the `case 'recordings'` switch forwarded only `transcription` and `process`, and the one endpoint answering "did this person agree to be recorded" 404'd. Reads are otherwise meeting-scoped; `GET /recordings` (tenant-wide, capped at 200) was added because a console has no meeting id to start from. `POST /upload` FAILS CLOSED without `consentConfirmed`/`consentMethod`/`consentParticipants`, and it is multipart, so it goes through `apiFormRequest` and not `apiRequest`, which JSON-stringifies its body and cannot send FormData. The consent notice exists twice (server default, client prefill) and is locked by `server/tests/unit/recording-consent-notice.test.ts`. CORRECTED 2026-09-09: the Express counterpart this line used to warn about is GONE. `server/routes/meeting-transcription-routes.ts` (851 lines) and `server/services/meeting-transcription-service.ts` (828) were both MOCKS - four of the nine handlers returned a hardcoded "Q4 Planning Session Recording" with invented transcripts, notes and highlights, and the service generated transcript segments itself and stamped `tenantId: 'mock-tenant'` on what it built - so both were deleted, the edge function covering all nine endpoints one for one plus the consent branch. `server/tests/unit/meeting-transcription-express-retired.test.ts` fails if either returns. CORRECTED 2026-09-21 (MEETINGS-READS-001): the sibling this line used to describe is gone too. It said the `meeting-scheduling-routes` router held the `/api` ROOT paths and that "its schedule-request, analytics and optimize-schedule handlers really do call a real db-backed service" - the service imported `db` and never queried anything, so that half was a mock as well. Router, service and the dead migration runner beside them are deleted; see the MEETINGS-READS-001 note below.

"IS IT WIRED" IS THE WRONG TEST FOR A DEAD ROUTER; "WOULD IT DO ANYTHING IF IT WERE" IS THE RIGHT ONE (QUALITY-002).
