PROD-008c KEPT `advanced-billing-routes.ts` - 39 handlers, real tables, real storage methods - because it was unwired work that WORKED, and deleting it would have thrown a feature away. `routes-contract-alerts.ts` was the opposite: registered, called by no client tree, behind a prefix no edge function serves (404 in production), and naming seven columns that do not exist, so it would have 500'd in dev had anyone called it - along with its only consumer, `services/contract-renewal-workflow.ts`, which named two more. Both deleted, 266 -> 255. **The deletion is only safe because the replacement was checked**: the alert bell's contract-expiration entries come from `_shared/operational-alerts.ts` via the `performance` function off the REAL columns, and the retirement test asserts both that this derivation exists and that it does NOT name the phantom columns the deleted router invented. **What genuinely disappears gets written down** - milestone automation at 180/90/60/30 days, which nothing else implements - because deleting a broken attempt silently retires the idea with it. **And a security test that named the deleted file was WIDENED, not removed**: `sql-injection-prevention.test.ts` checked `INTERVAL '${sql.raw(...)}'` on that one file BY NAME, which is the only place the property had ever been enforced. It scans every `.ts` under `server/` now, and catches a second form the original missed (`INTERVAL '${n} days'` - the same hole wearing template syntax). A property asserted about one file by name stops being enforced the day that file is renamed.

A TIMING ASSERTION OVER A FIXTURE IS NOT A PERFORMANCE TEST, IT IS A FLAKE GENERATOR (QUALITY-002). `server/tests/api-endpoints.test.ts` had a "Performance Tests" block doing ten concurrent GETs and one timed GET against `/api/tasks`, asserting `expect(responseTime).toBeLessThan(1000)` - against a router that returned a hardcoded array, so it measured that an in-memory literal is fast. Being a wall-clock budget on a shared container running the whole suite in parallel, it was also the only assertion in the tree that could fail a full run at random, and it did: once in seven, with a single unattributed failure that never reproduced in eleven further runs. A budget belongs where `scripts/bench-crm-lists.mjs` put it - real queries against a seeded database, with a header saying what it does not measure. **The router underneath it is the more general lesson**: 452 lines of "Mock tasks data", registered nowhere, with the TEST SUITE as its only importer - which is enough to make `check:server-orphans` call it reachable, and enough to make dead code look covered while every assertion pinned the mock's shape to itself. When a type-error cluster sits in a file whose only importer is a test, check what that test asserts before fixing the types; deleting it here took the ratchet 272 -> 266 and removed the flake with it.

WHEN THE TYPECHECK RATCHET IMPROVES A LOT, SUSPECT A SYNTAX ERROR: a broken JSX tag made `scripts/check-types.mjs` report 415 -> 20 and exit 0. tsc had bailed at the parse error and never typechecked the rest. The script guards against an EMPTY node_modules, not against a file it cannot parse. Confirm a drop by looking at which files the remaining errors are in (`npx tsc --noEmit --pretty false | grep ': error TS'`), not at the count.

A LINK THAT WORKS ON THE MACHINE THAT COMPOSED IT IS NOT A LINK (COP-B14). `BookingPages.tsx` builds its copy button from `${window.location.origin}/book/${slug}` and is right to: the rep reads it on screen and pastes it straight away. The sequence composer is the opposite case - the URL is STORED in an email body template and sent weeks later to somebody outside the company - so a rep composing against localhost would have stored `http://localhost:5173/book/dana-30min` and every prospect who clicked it would have got nothing, while the sequence kept reporting sends. `client/src/lib/booking-link.ts` is the one answer to "what URL spells this slug": the current origin when a stranger could open it, the canonical `https://printyx.net` otherwise, and `usedCanonicalOrigin: true` returned so the UI SAYS the substitution happened - a silent swap is its own defect, one host over. PRIVATE RANGES COUNT AS LOCAL (192.168 is reachable from the next desk and from nowhere a prospect will ever be), and the octet tests are exact rather than prefix-ish: 172.32 is outside 172.16-31 and 1.10.x is not 10.x, both of which a lazier regex refuses in the direction of breaking a real deployment. A page with a blank slug returns null rather than a link to `/book/`, and `BookingLinkPicker` lists only `is_active` pages, so nothing on this path can put a 404 in an email; a tenant with no page gets a sentence and a create button rather than an empty list that reads as a failed load. The picker serves the record page and the composer from one component because the hard parts are shared (offerable pages, the caller's own first, the origin rule); `onInsert` is the only difference and it inserts AT THE CARET, because a sequence body has its call to action mid-paragraph and an append means the rep moves the URL by hand every time. Whenever a URL you build in the client will be STORED and read by someone else later, the origin is a decision, not a convenience.

A WRITE PATH AND A READ PATH CAN DRIFT APART WITH NOTHING BETWEEN THEM (CRM-008, round 65). `GET /companies/:id/activities` was a bare `select('*')` while `ActivityTimeline` - the timeline on BOTH LeadDetail and CustomerDetail - reads eleven camelCase keys off each row. Every one resolved to undefined, and the symptom is not an error: the type Badge renders empty, the icon falls through to the generic one, the call and email detail blocks never open, and the component's own missing-date branch stamps every entry "Unknown time". A timeline of blanks. The POST beside it writes an explicit snake_case column map and always worked, so activities were STORED correctly and DISPLAYED as nothing - which is why nobody filed it. Camelise with `toCamelShallow`, never `toCamel`, wherever a row carries jsonb of arbitrary shape (`related_records`, `attachments`): a deep convert rewrites the customer's own keys inside the blob. THE CLASS TO GREP FOR is an endpoint whose handler has no row mapper next to a component that reads camelCase - CLAUDE.md already records the same shape on `business-records` (two timestamps snake-only) and MeterReadings (a normalizer inventing keys); this is the version where the endpoint has no mapper at all.

A SHARED TIMELINE COMPONENT NEEDS ONE FILTER VOCABULARY, AND A BUCKET CAN HOLD SEVERAL TYPES (CRM-008 AC5). `client/src/lib/activity-timeline-filters.ts` is that vocabulary for `business_record_activities`. NOTHING IS EVER DROPPED: `activity_type` is a free varchar whose writers store at least eleven values, so an unrecognised or missing type lands in "Other" rather than vanishing, because an activity that silently disappears from a timeline is worse than one wearing an unfamiliar label. "Changes" is why a bucket maps to a LIST rather than one value - `stage_change` comes from `sales-pipeline` and `record_created`/`record_updated` from `customers`, three values written by two functions that AC5 asks to show under one tab. The named tabs stay visible at zero (an empty Calls tab is a real answer about this record) while "Other" appears only when something is in it. Third time this story has produced the same finding: A CAPABILITY BUILT ON ONE OF TWO RECORD PAGES READS AS DONE FROM EITHER ONE - the stage picker (round 56), the type filter and the editable record name (this round). When a story names two surfaces, open both.

TWO SMALLER RULES FROM THE SAME PASS. A column that exists is not a column something writes to: `call_outcome` was missing from the activities PATCH field map, so the edit dialog folded a call's outcome into `outcome` and the next line overwrote it - two form fields, one column, and the call outcome always lost. And before making a title, a name or any field inline-editable, CHECK THE WRITE PATH ACCEPTS IT: `company_name` is in `_shared/business-record-write.ts`'s whitelist and `title` is in the deals PATCH map, both verified rather than assumed, because this story's own history is five layout-editable fields answering 200 having changed nothing.

A COUNT MUST BE SCOPED THE WAY THE LIST BESIDE IT IS SCOPED (CRM-008 AC6). The counts on a record page's associated-record tabs are not four `count: exact` calls over tenant plus the association column, because THREE OF THE FOUR LISTS BEHIND THOSE TABS APPLY `applyUserScope` ON THEIR OWN COLUMNS - deals on `owner_id`/`created_by_id`, proposals on `assigned_to`/`created_by`, quotes on `created_by` (WF-R-05, a quote carries margin). Counting without that tells a rep "Deals 7" above a list of three, which is COP-I01's shape: a real count of the wrong set, harder to spot than an invented number because both figures are true of something. Contacts stay unscoped to match `/companies/:id/contacts`, which filters on tenant and company alone. `GET /crm/record-counts?recordId=` is the one request that answers all four as exact PostgREST head counts, each replicating its list's scope, reporting `scopeTier` and `coversWholeTenant` beside the numbers (COP-I06: a narrowed total that does not say it was narrowed is a wrong number, not a safe one). Whenever you put a number next to a list, find the list's query and copy its filters - including the ones that are not in the URL.

RADIX UNMOUNTS AN INACTIVE TAB, WHICH DECIDES WHERE A TAB COUNT COMES FROM. Lifting each count out of its child component (the `onCountChange` shape COP-B06 used for the fleet exposure) looks cheaper and does not work here: `TabsContent` is unmounted until selected, so the count appears only after a rep has clicked through all four tabs, and a number that shows up after you look is worth less than none. One parent-level request beats four children reporting up.

A 200 WITH AN `unbacked` LINE IS NOT AN EMPTY LIST (CRM-008 AC6). `/api/deals/:id/quotes` answers `data: []` at 200 when migration 0088 is missing, because `proposals.deal_id` does not exist on that database - so a badge counting the array would read "Quotes 0", which says the deal has no quotes rather than that the feature cannot run here. Withhold the number when `unbacked` is non-empty. Same family as NULL IS NOT ZERO, one layer out: the response is well formed, the array is real, and the count it supports is still a false claim.

ESLINT CATCHES A CONDITIONAL HOOK AND NOTHING ELSE DOES (CRM-008, round 66). A `useQuery` added beside the JSX it feeds landed BELOW LeadDetail's `if (isLoading)` and `if (!lead)` early returns. tsc passed, 6,991 tests passed, the build passed; `react-hooks/rules-of-hooks` was the only thing that reported it, and a hook after an early return crashes on the render where the record resolves. Run eslint on a page after adding a hook to it, not only tsc and the suite.

A CARD IN A CATALOGUE IS NOT A CARD ON A SCREEN (COP-B01 AC6). `team-pipeline` and `team-activity` sat in `shared/my-day-layout.ts` from the day the My Day workspace shipped - declared, role-gated at MANAGER, orderable in the customizer, filtered correctly by role level - and rendered NOTHING, because no endpoint answered them and `TodayDashboard`'s `cardSlots` had no entry for either id. `MyDayCardBoundary` showed an empty card rather than an error, so the feature was absent in the politest possible way: a manager could add the card, see a blank, and read it as a quiet week. **The check is mechanical and worth running on any registry-driven UI: every id in the catalogue must have a slot, and `server/tests/unit/team-rollup.test.ts` now asserts exactly that for the team cards.** This is COP-M01's "a configured field the record does not carry" one level up - a configurable surface fails silently in both directions, and only the catalogue-versus-slots comparison sees it.

AND IT SEES IT AGAIN EVERY TIME SOMEBODY RUNS IT (COP-B01, round 105). The same comparison, written as a test this round, immediately found **`overdue` in the catalogue with no slot** - role-gated, orderable in the customizer, and rendered by a HARDCODED BANNER above the grid, so hiding it did nothing and reordering it did nothing. The inverse of the team-card finding and invisible the same way: the card was on screen, so nobody asked whether the catalogue controlled it. Its "View all N overdue tasks" control was also a `<Button variant="link">` with no handler at all, on the page's most prominent alert. **Assert the comparison in BOTH directions** - every id has a slot AND every slot has an id - because the second half is what catches a slot rendering something its id does not name, which is how `meetings-followup` spent its life rendering `upcoming` under the heading "Coming Up" while the card AC1 names had never been built.

A PAGE THAT SAYS "YOUR DAY" AND READS THE TENANT'S IS THE HARDEST WRONG NUMBER TO SEE (COP-B01 AC6, round 105). Every read in the `dashboards` /today branch filtered on `tenant_id` alone, so a rep's My Day listed every other rep's overdue tasks and meetings and counted the whole tenant's completed tasks on a personal stat card - COP-I01's real-count-of-the-wrong-set, where every row is true of somebody. **AC6 read as satisfied because the TEAM roll-up cards were scoped from the start**, so opening those and finding them correct says nothing about the personal cards beside them. **SIX READS, AND THE SIXTH IS WHY YOU WALK THEM**: three list queries a scan finds at once, the new meetings read, one deliberately unscoped (a follow-up logged by a colleague covering the territory closes the loop just as well), and a `count: 'exact', head: true` nested two levels deeper in a second `Promise.all` that no scan for list shapes reaches. Scope on the column the table HAS - `business_record_activities` carries `created_by` NOT NULL and no `assigned_to` - and report `scopeTier`/`degradedFrom` so a narrowed list says it was narrowed (COP-I06).

A MUTANT THAT LOOKS LIKE REDUNDANT CODE IS USUALLY A MISSING FIXTURE, AND THIS ONE HID A REAL BUG (round 105). Deleting `a.id !== meeting.id` from a follow-up predicate survived, and read as genuinely redundant beside the strict `>` - a meeting cannot be later than itself. It is not: an activity is stamped at `completed_date ?? scheduled_date`, so a meeting held Monday and TICKED OFF Tuesday has its own entry later than its own `scheduled_date` and closes itself out, hiding exactly the meetings a diligent rep marks complete. The fixture had no ticked-off meeting in it. **And the scope assertion took THREE attempts**, each a shape this file already records: bound to the CALL rather than the ARGUMENT (`{ ...scope, userIds: null }` passes a check for `applyUserScope(` plus `'created_by'`); then a 900-character window that ran into the NEXT chain, whose `scope,` satisfied it for a read that had none; then a closing-paren search, which fails because these six chains sit at THREE different indents. The bound that works is the next `.from('<table>')` - a construct, not a distance.

THE TEAM IS `resolveScope`'s ANSWER, AND A CALLER WITH NO TEAM GETS 403, NOT A ROLL-UP OF THEMSELVES (COP-B01 AC6). `GET /crm/team-rollup` never takes a user list from the client. A scope that resolves to 'own' is refused with INSUFFICIENT_SCOPE, because a one-row team card is not a smaller version of the feature - it is a rep reading a manager surface - and the card treats that 403 as "not a manager any more", rendering nothing with `retry: false` so a demoted user does not re-ask on every focus. `shared/team-rollup.ts` does the grouping PostgREST cannot, and its rules are the ones this repo keeps re-learning: an EXPLICIT UNASSIGNED bucket that the card SHOWS (COP-B10 - a per-rep list that hides ownerless rows stops adding up to the total printed above it); a member with nothing appearing AT ZERO, which is the inverse of NULL-IS-NOT-ZERO and the difference is whether the row was looked for; a total over an uncosted row declared a FLOOR (COP-B05); and an activity with no type counted under `unspecified` rather than discarded. Each half is computed independently and a failure answers **null**, so the card renders nothing - "0 activities this week" about a team that is working is worse than an empty space, and the server already knows which it is.

A `?mine=true` FILTER IS NOT AN ACCESS CHECK, AND THE ITEM ROUTE IS THE WORSE HALF (COP-B04 AC4). `GET /opportunity-radar` filtered on tenant, status and - only when the caller asked - `?mine=true`. Dropping one query parameter returned up to 500 plays across the whole tenant, each naming an account, its machines, the trigger and an estimated dollar value, so every rep could read every other rep's book. No guard saw it: the function IS gated at MANAGER on the two branches that change tenant-wide settings, and its read is open on purpose because a rep has plays - what was missing is that a rep has only SOME plays. The fix is COP-I06's, which is ORDER rather than refusal: `resolveScope` first, `applyUserScope(query, 'owner_id', scope)` on the query BEFORE `mine` and `playType`, so a parameter can only narrow what the tier already allows, with `scopeTier` reported so a shorter list is explicable. **AND THE SAME SCOPE MUST REACH THE ITEM ROUTE**: `POST /:id/dismiss` and `POST /:id/convert` loaded the play by id and tenant alone, and dismissing another rep's play takes it off their board while converting one creates a deal in their name. A uuid is not the control - SEC-TENANT-005 already records that ids travel in URLs, exports and support tickets. Scoping a list and leaving its items open is a half-measure; grep for a handler that filters a list on a caller-supplied `mine`/`ownerId`/`userId` and then serves `/:id` on tenant alone. An UNOWNED row stays actionable for anyone who can see it, which is what `applyUserScope`'s own default already encodes.

`check:phantom-cols` CANNOT SEE A PAYLOAD BUILT BY A HELPER, SO CHECK IT AGAINST `getTableConfig` INSTEAD (COP-B04 AC3). That guard's documented blind spot is a payload assembled as a named variable, and CLAUDE.md cites the deals insert as the example - `dealFromPlay` in `_shared/opportunity-radar.ts` is precisely that shape, so the one-click convert was unverified by anything. `server/tests/unit/radar-list-scope.test.ts` resolves drizzle's `getTableConfig` and asserts two properties over the mapper's emitted keys: every key is a real column, and every NOT NULL column with no default is supplied. That is worth copying wherever an edge function inserts through a helper rather than an inline literal, and it is cheap - the table config is already imported by half a dozen tests here. The same test closes the other half of a one-click convert nobody usually checks: the radar writes its equipment links under `relation: 'replaces'` and the deals function reads `.eq('relation', 'replaces')`, so a link written under any other label would exist and surface on no screen.

THREE COLUMNS THAT LOOK LIKE ONE RELATIONSHIP (COP-B09 AC3). `sales_territories` carries `owner_id` (the primary rep), `team_members` (the others working it) and `manager_id` (the person it REPORTS TO), and only the first two are somebody's book. `shared/territory-membership.ts` keeps them apart because conflating them produces a defect that reads as working: **if `manager_id` counted toward a rep's default view, a sales manager over four territories would open the radar filtered to those four and read it as their own pipeline, while a manager over none saw everything** - the narrower answer arriving for the more senior person. Managing is what a roll-up is for, so it feeds `allTerritoryIds` and never `defaultTerritoryIds`. Three more rules travel with it: an EMPTY DEFAULT MEANS DO NOT FILTER rather than filter to nothing (a rep whose territory nobody recorded must not open an empty board and conclude they have no work - NULL-IS-NOT-ZERO applied to a filter); an INACTIVE TERRITORY BINDS NOBODY, or a closed territory keeps pinning its old owner; and an EXPLICIT REQUEST ALWAYS WINS, `all` included, so nobody is trapped in a default. `team_members` was missing from `TERRITORY_COLUMNS` the whole time, so every reader could only ever answer the primary owner - check the SELECT list before concluding a relationship is unrepresentable.

READ-TIME MATCHING IS WHAT MAKES AN AUDIT NECESSARY, NOT WHAT MAKES IT UNNECESSARY (COP-B09 AC6, round 106). That story's own notes concluded there was nothing to audit, because territory membership is resolved at read time and nothing is rewritten - so records cannot be orphaned. The first half is right and the conclusion inverts it: a rename takes effect **instantly and silently**, releasing every account whose free-text `territory` column says "North" and claiming every one saying "North Region", with no migration, no diff and nothing on any screen. A DELETE is worse, because those accounts land in the coverage report's UNASSIGNED bucket, which reads as "nobody has assigned these yet". **Wherever this repo resolves a relationship at read time (`_shared/territory.ts`, `_shared/competitor.ts`), the lookup row's NAME is a schema change wearing an edit's clothes.** Wiring it exposed two defects in branches that reported success: PUT and DELETE never read the row, so no diff was possible even in principle, and **PostgREST's delete matches nothing and returns NO ERROR**, so deleting a stale id answered `success: true`. That is a fabricated WRITE outcome, which all three fabrication guards miss because they watch reads. **AND THE COUNT IS DELIBERATELY ABSENT**: answering "how many accounts moved" needs the coverage scan over every business_record, far too expensive on a write path, and an exact-name approximation would miss the case-insensitive and code matches the real resolver makes - a number that is close but not right is worse than none, because the auditor cannot tell which they have. A test asserts the absence so nobody adds a plausible count later.

`supabase/functions/_shared/audit-log.ts` IS THE ONE WRITER NOW, AND FOUR FUNCTIONS ARE LEFT ALONE ON PURPOSE (round 106). Five edge functions write `audit_logs` (admin, user, audit-logs, root-admin, security) and each carries its own near-copy of the insert; the shared module exists so the next caller is not a sixth, and converting the five would be a refactor wearing a feature's clothes - they are one import away whenever somebody is in them anyway. Three rules it encodes: every NOT NULL column is supplied and the TEST derives that list from drizzle's `getTableConfig` rather than copying a sibling; `x-forwarded-for` is a LIST, so take the FIRST entry or the column an auditor reads as "who" fills with proxy addresses; and **it never throws**, because a rep who cannot save when the log is down is worse than a gap in the log - and the gap is visible while the refusal is not. **The "never throws" assertion is where the seventh presence-check trap fired**: reading the source for `} catch (err) {` plus a `return { written: false` passed while the catch body was replaced with `throw err`, because the OTHER such return, in the `if (error)` branch, was still there. The module takes a narrow client interface, so the repair is to CALL it against a stub that throws and assert it RESOLVES - and three more behavioural cases came free.

A TEST THAT PINS ADJACENCY BREAKS WHEN SOMETHING CORRECT IS INSERTED BETWEEN (COP-B09 round 70). Two existing guards failed on a change that was right, and both were repaired rather than relaxed. One asserted a select list matched `/manager_id, monthly_quota,/` when its property was "monthly_quota is returned" - it reads the column list and checks membership now. The other scanned every object-literal key indented eight spaces or more to find write payloads, which was accidentally correct only while the payloads were the sole literals in that region; a new branch's RESPONSE object was immediately reported as a phantom column of a table it never claimed to belong to. Bind such a scan to `.insert(`/`.upsert(` chains and the `set('col', ...)` helper - what it always claimed to read - and keep a floor so a regex that stops matching cannot pass in silence. **A guard failing on correct work is information about the guard**, and the repair is usually to assert the property rather than the layout.

MUTATION TESTING EARNS ITS KEEP ON THE SURVIVORS, NOT THE KILLS. Ten mutants on one module, eight died immediately, and the two that lived were worth more than the eight. The first was the comment trap for the FIFTH time in this session: an assertion that the select list contains `team_members` matched the COMMENT saying `team_members` had been absent, so it passed with the column deleted - strip comments from both sides, always, and this file has now recorded that lesson five times because it keeps being learned in a new costume. The second looked like redundant code and was a MISSING FIXTURE: a blank-caller guard appeared untested because no territory in the fixture was UNOWNED, and an empty `owner_id` compares equal to an empty caller id, so an unowned row is exactly what makes that guard load-bearing. When a mutant survives, check whether the fixture can reach the branch before calling the code redundant.

A BLOCKER INVESTIGATION IS A NOTE TOO, AND IT GOES STALE THE SAME WAY (COP-B12). That story was filed BLOCKED "investigated in full" on three compounding prerequisites, and re-reading the tree found one of them wrong in a way that had kept a whole criterion untouched for weeks. It said "THERE IS NO `meetings` TABLE... so there is nothing to associate a deal TO" - the table NAME is right and the conclusion is wrong, because **`calendar_events` is the meeting entity this product actually uses**. It is created by `drizzle/rls/meetings-tables.sql`, one of FOUR meeting-related files in that directory and the one the investigation did not open; it carries `related_entity_id`/`related_entity_type`, which is the association; and five edge paths write it, including the public booking surface. `meeting_recordings.meeting_id` is a bare uuid with no foreign key, caller-supplied at upload, so it can point at a `calendar_events.id` today. **When a blocker names a missing thing, grep for what plays its ROLE, not for its name** - and `ls` the whole directory the investigation cites, because a four-file directory read as one file is how this survived.

AN "AI FEATURE" USUALLY HAS ONE CRITERION THAT IS JUST A QUERY (COP-B12 AC4). Three of that story's criteria extract from a transcript and are genuinely gated on a speech-to-text provider. The fourth - "a pre-meeting briefing card assembles the account's recent activity, open deals, installed base, and service state" - reads four tables a dealer already fills and never touches a transcript. Before accepting a story as blocked, read its ACs individually against the blocker: the gate is rarely uniform, and the ungated criterion is often the one a rep would notice first. THE AUTHORIZATION CHOICE IS WORTH COPYING: the briefing is gated on the EVENT being the caller's (`calendar_events` filtered by tenant AND `user_id`), not on the account being in their book, because scoping it to owned accounts breaks it in exactly the case it matters - a manager joining a rep's call, a colleague covering a territory. Not-found and not-yours answer the same 404, since distinguishing them tells a caller whether an id exists on somebody else's calendar. And `shared/meeting-briefing.ts` carries the rule a briefing gets wrong invisibly: A SECTION THAT COULD NOT BE READ IS NULL, NOT EMPTY, because a briefing is read for thirty seconds and then acted on, so "no open deals" and "we could not look" must not render the same. Its inverse matters just as much - ZERO IS A MEASUREMENT WHEN THE ROWS WERE LOOKED FOR, and `machineCount: 0` is exactly what a rep selling a first machine needs.

`check:phantom-cols` IS A HARD GATE AT ZERO NOW (AUDIT-037, round 72). The baseline is empty. The last fifteen all sat in edge functions nothing calls, which is COP-B03's setup - harmless until somebody wires a caller - so they were rebound rather than left as traps, and most were bigger than "a column name is wrong". **AN UNKNOWN COLUMN FAILS THE WHOLE STATEMENT, NOT THE ONE FIELD**, which is the part that keeps surprising: `remote-monitoring`'s heartbeat wrote `status: 'online'` beside `last_seen`, so `last_seen` was never stamped and every device showed as last seen whenever it was registered, on a list that ORDERS BY last_seen; `business-records-search` put a phantom `email` inside a `.or()`, so searching by ANY term was a 42703 rather than just missing the email match; `chrome-extension`'s key insert had three bad keys, so generating an extension key has never worked once. **AND A REBIND CAN BE WORSE THAN THE BUG**: `tenants.settings` is really `tenants.metadata`, a SHARED blob auto-lead-routing also writes, so rebinding without merging would have turned a guaranteed 42703 into silent erasure of another feature's configuration - the WhiteLabelDashboard defect one table over. Check what else writes a jsonb column before you write to it.

WHEN A COLUMN DOES NOT EXIST, THE FIX IS SOMETIMES DELETION AND SOMETIMES A REFUSAL (AUDIT-037). Two of the fifteen were not rebindable and neither wanted a migration. `tasks.dependencies`: the tempting fix is to drop the column and answer `edges: []`, and that is the WORSE outcome, because an empty dependency graph is not "we do not know" - it is the claim that every task in the project is independent and can start now, which is the same fabrication this story found being written from the other end. Edges are null with a stated reason; the task list stays real. `sso_sessions`: the insert named three columns the table lacks AND omitted two it requires (`user_id` NOT NULL with an FK, `session_id` NOT NULL UNIQUE), and never destructured its result - so the endpoint answered `success: true, note: 'Session stored'` while storing nothing. **Adding the three columns would not have made it work**, because the row wants a user that JIT provisioning has not created yet, so the insert is gone and the response says `sessionPersisted: false`. Before writing a migration to satisfy an insert, check the columns it OMITS as well as the ones it invents.

A TEST THAT FAILS ON THE SUCCESS STATE IS USUALLY TELLING YOU TO FOLLOW ITS OWN COMMENT (AUDIT-037). `phantom-cols-reachable-zero` carried a floor asserting the baseline was NOT empty, with a note saying that if it ever reached zero the reachability assertion would pass vacuously and should be replaced by a hard gate at zero. Emptying the baseline made that floor fail. The fix is the instruction already written there, not a relaxation - and the reachability split is kept, because it is what will make a future regression legible rather than just red. The same round, `nplus1-triage` drifted because its keys are `file:line` and a fix inserted ten lines above one; CLAUDE.md already says to move the key rather than silence the guard, and that is the third line-key drift this session.

`check:swallowed-writes` WAS RED IN CI AND NOBODY HAD NOTICED (AUDIT-037, round 72). Nine edge write sites discarded their PostgREST result - which answers 200 while saving nothing - on a guard wired at `.github/workflows/ci.yml:91`. Six were two byte-identical copies of one delivery-date sync helper, each wrapped in a try/catch that can only fire on a NETWORK fault, which is precisely what the guard's own message warns about. The one that mattered most: the opportunity radar creates the deal BEFORE marking the play converted, so a discarded error there leaves a play still reading `open` pointing at a real deal, and the next rep converts it into a SECOND deal for the same trigger - COP-B04 AC7's idempotency lives in that one row. **Run the full guard set, not the ones your change touches**: this was found by running `check:swallowed-writes` on a whim during unrelated work, and it had been failing long enough that nobody could say when it started.

ASK THE GUARD HOW IT CLASSIFIES SOMETHING BEFORE CONCLUDING FROM A GREP (SEC-EDGE-001, round 73). Checking whether AC5's named targets were gated, a grep for `requireRoleLevel|requirePermission|ROLE_LEVEL` across `vendors`, `product-models` and `purchase-orders` returned nothing, which reads as "still open" - and all three are gated, through `denyWithoutPermission`, a fourth helper the pattern did not name. `node scripts/check-edge-rbac.mjs --list` answers it in one line per function, because the guard knows all four signals and a hand-written pattern knows the ones you remembered. The same command settles the platform-admin question: every `platform-*` function reports `shared-rbac, platform-admin, role-rejection`.

AN UNLISTED PATH IS VISIBLE BY DEFAULT, WHICH CHANGES WHAT "MIRROR THE NAV" MEANS (SEC-EDGE-001). `checkNavigationAccess` says it outright - "No rule means visible by default" - so a route with no `navigation-permissions.ts` entry is open to every role, exactly like `alwaysVisible`. `/service/toner-replenish` is one. That does NOT make its writes open by design: the rule this repo keeps arriving at is to gate the BRANCH that does the privileged thing, and here the seam has a name worth reusing - **THE SPEND LIMIT IS A MANAGER DECISION; SPENDING INSIDE IT IS NOT**. Toner-replenish's settings carry the cost ceiling below which a replenishment order auto-ships, so raising it authorises the system to spend more without asking; that is gated, while shipping, cancelling and running the pipeline stay open for the coordinator whose job they are. `renewal-autoquote` is the same shape with a subtler second branch: a SUPPRESSION means a customer never receives a renewal quote again, and nothing on the drafts board shows the absence - a renewal that silently stops being offered is indistinguishable from one nobody got round to - so both adding and lifting one need a manager, while marking a draft sent stays a rep's own work.

A FILE THAT ASSERTS ITS OWN SUMMARY KEEPS ITSELF HONEST (SEC-EDGE-001). `docs/edge-rbac-triage.json` carries a `counts` block, and a sibling test asserts it against the entries - so editing verdicts without recomputing the counts fails the suite. That is worth copying to any hand-maintained inventory: the summary is the thing people quote, and a summary nobody checks drifts from the list it summarises within two edits.

A SIBLING HAVING A REDACTOR IS NOT EVIDENCE THAT THIS ONE DOES (SEC-EDGE-001, round 74). CLAUDE.md records that `manufacturer_connections` and `signatures` run every credential read through a redactor, and that note is true and is about DIFFERENT TABLES. `manufacturer_integrations` - the dealer's HP, Canon and Xerox keys - had none: every read was a bare `select('*')` handed to the caller, so `GET /manufacturer-integrations` listed `api_key`, `api_secret`, `client_secret`, `access_token` and `refresh_token` to any authenticated member of the tenant, while `/manufacturer-integration` sat at `minLevel: 4` in the nav. TWO CONTROLS AND THE ORDER MATTERS: redaction is structural, because a response should not carry a secret whoever asked for it, and the role gate is second rather than a substitute. Each secret becomes a `*_set: boolean` marker so a settings page can still show what is configured. **THE EMBED IS THE ONE A CARELESS FIX MISSES**: the audit branch selects `integration:manufacturer_integrations(*)`, so every log row carried the full credential set through a path that does not look like a credential read - grep for `<table>(*)` embeds, not just for `.from('<table>')`, when auditing what a function can leak. And the INTERNAL resolver keeps `select('*')` on purpose, because the adapter needs the real key to call the manufacturer; what changes is what leaves the function, and a mutation that redacts the internal read fails the suite so the distinction cannot be flattened later.

WHEN YOU GATE ONE OF A FAMILY, CHECK THE REST OF THE FAMILY IN THE SAME PASS (SEC-EDGE-001). `product-models` and `software-products` are the same catalogue shape with the same `navigation-permissions.ts` rule (`operations.inventory.view`, minLevel 2). The first has gated its writes with `operations.inventory.manage` since this story's opening batch; the second was open to any tenant member - CSV import, bulk delete and dedupe included - for sixteen batches afterwards. The fix is two lines copied from the twin; finding it took reading the nav table and noticing two entries with identical rules and different outcomes.

A WRITE CAN BE SCOPED BY THE READ IT DEPENDS ON (SEC-EDGE-001). Three of `mobile-field`'s nine writes looked unscoped to an automated scan for `user.id` near the write: the `time_tracking_entries` inserts and a session update carry no caller filter of their own, because they hang off a session already read with `technician_id = user.id`. Transitive scoping is the normal shape in a handler that loads a row and then writes to it, so walk back to the read before filing a write as unprotected - and, in the other direction, before trusting one that merely sits near a filtered query.

ONE FUNCTION, TWO PAGES, TWO DIFFERENT NAV GATES - READ BOTH (SEC-EDGE-001, round 75). `/preventive-maintenance` is a view page with no minLevel and `/preventive-maintenance-automation` is minLevel 3 with `service.schedule.manage`, and they reach the same `maintenance` edge function. Gating the whole function at 3 locks out the lower page (the `webhooks` mistake this story already paid for); leaving it open lets any tenant member create the schedules that dispatch a technician to a customer site. **The lower page sets the READ level and the higher sets the WRITE level**, and neither rule alone gives the right answer - so when a function has more than one caller, grep the nav table for every path that reaches it before picking a level.

A COMMENT SAYING "REQUIRES AUTH" IS NOT AN AUTH CHECK (SEC-EDGE-001). `printer-monitoring`'s two `oid-presets` branches were `if (jwt) { verify } else if (apiKey) { verify }` with NO FINAL BRANCH, so a caller carrying neither credential skipped both checks and was served the catalogue - directly under a comment promising otherwise. The stakes were low (global SNMP reference data, no tenant filter) and the shape is not: an `if/else if` chain that authenticates is only as good as its last branch, and a missing `else` reads as covered to anyone skimming. Worth grepping for wherever auth is expressed as a chain rather than a guard clause. Note the rest of that function is correctly open-by-design: device submissions authenticate with `monitoring_clients.api_key`, a DEVICE credential, and **a role check on an agent submission is a category error** - the same reason `mobile-logs` was overturned earlier in this story.

A TEST THAT BREAKS WHEN YOU ADD A GATE IS EVIDENCE THE GATE WORKS (SEC-EDGE-001). Gating `maintenance` turned three cases of `preventive-maintenance-real` red, because its stub user carried no role claim. The fix is to give the fixture the level a real caller holds (`roleLevel: 3` for a scheduler) - but the question to answer FIRST is whether the caller it models should hold that level, because the alternative answer is that the gate is wrong. Then re-run with the claim removed and confirm the three go red again, or the fixture has quietly become the thing under test.

A GLOBAL TABLE IS A CROSS-TENANT WRITE, AND NO ROLE GATE CLOSES IT (SEC-EDGE-001, round 76). `oid_mappings` decides which SNMP OIDs printer monitoring polls for toner level and meter counts, and it has **no `tenant_id`** - so a custom mapping authored at one dealer is editable and deletable by anyone at any other, and a wrong or deleted one does not raise an error, the machine just stops reporting. `is_custom` protects the shipped presets and separates nobody from anybody; `created_by` is declared `integer` against a uuid `users.id` (AUDIT-032's shape), so it cannot even record an author, which is why the insert writes it null rather than coercing. The MANAGER gate (mirroring `/oid-management`, minLevel 4) is what IS available and it is not the fix - the fix is a column - so the gap is asserted STRUCTURALLY from `getTableConfig` in a test designed to FAIL the day `tenant_id` lands. **Check a table for a tenant column before writing up a role gate as the answer**; `workflow_templates` is the same shape read-only, and checking rather than assuming found its only writer anywhere is a seeder, which makes reading it tenant-wide correct. **AND EXEMPT THE POSTS THAT DO NOT WRITE**: `POST /export` here is a READ the page sends as a POST, so a blanket `method !== 'GET'` gate refuses a read on the strength of its verb.

A ROLE GATE MUST NOT BECOME THE ONLY CONTROL (SEC-EDGE-001). `predictive-failure` gained a SUPERVISOR gate on all non-GET, mirroring `/service/predictions` (minLevel 3, its only caller): `POST /score` creates draft service tickets, `/predictions/:id/approve` dispatches one to a customer site, and `PUT /settings` carries the agent kill switch and the confidence threshold - both tenant-wide, and raising the threshold empties the prediction board for everybody, which is indistinguishable from a fleet in good health (churn-risk's shape). The kill switch stays in front of scoring and the test asserts it, because a supervisor scoring a paused agent would dispatch technicians the tenant deliberately stopped.

`docs/edge-rbac-triage.json` STORES NON-ASCII AS `\u` ESCAPES, so writing it back with a plain `JSON.stringify` re-emits every one raw and turns a six-entry change into a 124-line diff - the `prd.json` reformatting trap in a different file. Escape chars above 0x7F on write. Same round, the line-keyed `docs/nplus1-triage.json` drifted twice more (oid-mappings 364 -> 428, predictive-failure 431 -> 485) because the gate inserts lines above the loops: **fourth and fifth occurrence this session**, and the fix is always to move the key rather than silence the guard.

AN ACCESS MODEL CAN BE IN THE SCHEMA AND READ BY NOTHING (SEC-EDGE-001, round 77). `meeting_recordings` declares `uploaded_by NOT NULL`, `is_public boolean DEFAULT false` and `access_permissions jsonb DEFAULT '[]'` - an owner, a share flag and a grant list - and every handler in `meeting-transcription` filtered on `tenant_id` alone, so a recording created private behaved as public while the column said the opposite. What that exposed is content, not a row count: the tenant-wide list, any meeting's recordings, any transcript IN FULL, the AI notes and highlights, plus `POST /content/search`, which `ilike`s across every transcript in the tenant and returns a snippet around the match - a word in a search box read back what was said in another rep's customer call. The page is `alwaysVisible`, so a role gate would take the feature from the people it is for; `supabase/functions/meeting-transcription/_access.ts` narrows ROWS (COP-I06) and lands on the ITEM routes as well as the lists, because scoping a list and leaving `/:id` open is COP-B04's half-measure. **Before writing a role gate, read the table's own columns**: the intent was already recorded and unimplemented, which is COP-B03's shape one layer in.

FOUR RULES FROM THAT MODULE, ALL REUSABLE. **Two `.or()` calls on one PostgREST query are ANDed**, so an extra disjunct (here `is_public.is.true`) cannot be a second call - `applyUserScope` emits exactly one `or()`, so a predicate that mixes scope with anything else has to build the clause list by hand. **An access resolver FAILS CLOSED**: returning "everything" when the database errors turns an outage into the exposure the resolver exists to close. **An `.in.()` with no values is rejected by PostgREST**, so an empty accessible set short-circuits with an empty answer rather than querying. And **a read with no writer is not a feature**: `access_permissions` is deliberately not filtered on, because nothing writes it, so honouring it would add a per-recording sharing control no UI can grant - named in the response's `unbacked` instead of half-built.

`QueryState`'s `defaultIsEmpty` ONLY KNOWS ABOUT ARRAYS. Turning a bare-array endpoint into an envelope (needed here so the page can say the list was narrowed) silently retires the empty state, because `isEmpty` stops matching and the "nothing here yet" copy never renders again. Pass an explicit `isEmpty={(data) => data.rows.length === 0}` in the same change, or the page shows an empty table where it used to explain itself.

A SUCCESS TOAST OVER A SWALLOWED LOOP IS A DESTRUCTIVE ACTION LYING (SEC-EDGE-001, round 78). `ManagedServices.tsx` bulk-deleted by looping `DELETE /api/managed-services/:id` with each call wrapped in `catch {}`, then toasted `Deleted ${ids.length}` regardless - and the edge function **had no `/:id` branch at all**, so in production every one of those calls 404'd while Express served working PATCH and DELETE handlers on every developer machine (`/api/managed-services` is not in `crmProxies`). A rep could select twenty products, be told all twenty were gone, and lose none. **Whenever a page loops a mutation and reports a count, check that the count is what happened rather than what was attempted**; keep the failures selected so a retry does not mean finding them again, and say "Nothing deleted" when that is the truth. Three guards watch fabricated READS and none of them watches a fabricated WRITE outcome.

A DEAD BUTTON WEARS DEAD STATE'S CLOTHES. Every row's Edit control called `setSelectedService(service)` and nothing read it, so the button did nothing - and eslint reported it only as "'selectedService' is assigned a value but never used", which reads like a tidy-up rather than a missing feature. Same disguise CRMX-016's unrouted pages wore (`React.lazy` import "assigned a value but never used"). **When eslint flags unused state on a page, find what sets it before deleting it.** Wiring it needs one rule written into the code: the Add button must CLEAR the edit target, or opening the dialog after an edit keeps the id set and submit patches the previous row while the form looks right, because `reset()` is what empties it.

AN ERROR DISCARDED OVER A PHANTOM TABLE IS A DASHBOARD OF ZEROES, NOT AN OUTAGE. Four of `managed-services`' five tables exist in no schema and no migration, and all four were already baselined against that file. The contract branches answered 500 ("Failed to fetch contracts"), which reads as an outage rather than a feature nobody built; `GET /usage` and `GET /dashboard` were worse, discarding their errors entirely so usage returned `200 []` and the dashboard returned `totalContracts: 0, totalDevices: 0, monthlyRevenue: 0` - a screen telling a dealer their managed-services business does not exist. All of them answer **503 RELATION_MISSING** now, `alertCount` is null rather than a hardcoded 0, and the gap is named in `unbacked`. `?month=` on that same branch was pulled off the query string and never applied, so one month returned the most recent hundred readings from any month - **and it was visible the whole time as a baselined `no-unused-vars` finding**, which is COP-B04's tell: a local computed and never read is usually a body that was removed or never written.

TWO MUTANTS SURVIVED FIRST TIME AND BOTH ARE SHAPES THIS FILE ALREADY RECORDS. A presence check for each reserved path segment matched the branch conditions that use the same words, so deleting `'contracts'` from `RESERVED_SEGMENTS` left the test green - and that mutant is a real routing break, because the `/:id` branches sit above the contract ones. And a fixed 200-character window after `const startCreate` ran past it into `closeDialog`, which also clears the edit target, so the mutant removing the line from `startCreate` survived. **Bind to the literal and to the function body: a window is not a scope, and a presence check is not a membership check.**

A COLUMN LITERAL APPLIED INSIDE A HELPER GETS ATTRIBUTED TO THE WRONG TABLE (SEC-EDGE-001, round 79). `check:phantom-cols` resolves a column against the table its call chain is on, and a shared `scopeToCustomer(query, claimed)` whose body does `.eq('customer_id', ...)` has no chain of its own - so the guard attributed the literal to whatever `.from()` it had last seen and reported `portal_service_classifications.customer_id` as phantom. The guard was wrong and the code was right. **A false positive inside a HARD GATE is worth restructuring around rather than baselining**, because a baseline holding a known non-defect is where a real one hides: the name became a constant off the chain, and the check that actually matters is a test asserting it against drizzle's `getTableConfig` - stronger than the guard could manage, and the treatment this file already prescribes for a payload built by a helper.

**CAPTURE THE OUTPUT BEFORE CALLING SOMETHING A FLAKE.** That finding surfaced as ONE failing test in a 7,000-test suite - `phantom-cols-resolves-ambiguity.test.ts` shells out to the guard, so a new guard finding appears as a single unrelated-looking failure with the real message buried in its stdout. It matched the shape of an intermittent failure recorded twice earlier in the session, so the instinct was to re-run and move on; it was deterministic, and it was a defect introduced ninety seconds before. Re-running is how you confirm a flake, not how you diagnose one: save the run and read the banner.

A CUSTOMER SURFACE THAT TRUSTS A CALLER-SUPPLIED RESOURCE ID (SEC-EDGE-001). `portal-service` is routed at `/portal` and its page's own header opens "the customer describes what's wrong". All four endpoints took a `requestId` and looked it up on `tenant_id` alone - and a portal customer is in the tenant - so one customer could read another's timeline, read the free text they typed, and rate their request. `POST /classify` is the worst and is a WRITE: it opens a service ticket on that customer's account, moves their request to `assigned`, and appends status history signed "AI Dispatch". Same fix as `customer-portal` one function over - `app_metadata` only, a claim narrows every lookup, no claim means staff and keeps the tenant view - with all five queries through one helper and 404 rather than 403 so an id on another account is never confirmed. **The generalisable question is not "is this gated" but "who addresses the row":** an endpoint that takes an id from the caller needs the same predicate on every branch, including the one that only writes.

ONE BRANCH WRITING TABLE A WHILE ANOTHER READS TABLE B IS NOT A NAMING QUIBBLE (SEC-EDGE-001, round 80). Every caller of `/api/professional-services` wants the product CATALOGUE - the page lists and creates catalogue rows, the quote builder fills its "Installation, training, consulting" picker from it, and Express serves `professional_services`. The edge function served PROJECTS, off `professional_services_projects` and `project_tasks`, neither of which exists in any schema. The prefix is not proxied, so dev worked off Express and production did not, and **the list branch swallowed the missing-table error and answered `[]` at 200** - so it read as a dealer who had never configured any professional services, and a rep could not add installation to a quote with nothing saying why. The tell that settles it: `POST /import` already wrote the REAL catalogue table through the shared spec, so one function imported a CSV successfully and then listed nothing. **When a prefix has two plausible domains, check which table each BRANCH touches, not just which one the function is named after.**

A DEFECT THAT CAME FROM COPYING TRAVELS IN FOURS. Round 78 fixed `ManagedServices`' swallowed bulk-delete loop inline and moved on; `grep -n "catch {}" client/src/pages/*.tsx` - which that round's own note says to run - finds `ProfessionalServices`, `Supplies` and `EnhancedProductAccessories` carrying the identical loop and the identical `Deleted ${ids.length}` toast. The fix is one module now, `client/src/lib/bulk-delete.ts`, with the rules where the fifth page inherits them: count successes not attempts, keep the failures selected, carry on past a failure rather than aborting, never render a partial failure as a success. **Fixing one instance inline is how a quartet becomes a quintet.**

WHICH BROKE THE PREVIOUS ROUND'S TEST, CORRECTLY. It pinned the inline shape (`let deleted = 0`), so converging on a shared helper failed it - a test failing on a change that is strictly better is information about the test. Rebound to the property (the page counts what happened and keeps the failures selected, wherever the counting lives), with the helper's behaviour exercised against real inputs rather than read as text. **A test that pins an implementation blocks the better version of it**, and this repo has now recorded the same lesson for adjacency (`/manager_id, monthly_quota,/`) and for indentation-scanned object keys.

`isMissingTableError` COUNTED AT `>= 7` WHEN THERE ARE EIGHT let a mutant through: deleting the check on the one branch whose swallowed `[]` was the original defect still passed. **A TOTAL IS NOT A PROPERTY** - fourth occurrence in this session, after `applyUserScope` calls, `fetchAllRows` calls and `RESERVED_SEGMENTS` words. Walk the sites.

A GUARD'S MATCHING UNIT IS PART OF ITS CONTRACT, AND THIS ONE NEVER SAID (SEC-EDGE-001, round 81). `check:raw-body-writes` scanned LINE BY LINE, so `.update({ ...body` had to be on one line - and prettier breaks that literal onto two the moment it has a second key, which it always does because `updated_at` is always there. The guard faithfully reported the single-line spelling and was blind to the normal one: widening it to scan the joined source took the count **12 -> 23**, eleven sites invisible since the day it shipped (branding-profiles, enrichment, leases, maintenance, pipeline-config x2, platform-crm, platform-deals, proposals x2, social-media, supplies). The header described what it looked FOR and never said it looked at one line at a time, which is the fact that decided what it could find. `check:no-random-metrics` carries the same caveat and does say so. **When a baseline doubles because a guard improved, write that into the baseline's own note** - a count that jumps reads as a regression otherwise, and the longer list implies the new entries are newer than the old ones.

THE CREATE-OR-EDIT DIALOG WAS WRONG ON FOUR PAGES IN THREE WAYS, ALL FROM COPYING. Three (`ManagedServices`, `ProfessionalServices`, `Supplies`) had an Edit button whose `setSelected(row)` nothing read - a dead control that eslint reports only as "assigned a value but never used". `EnhancedProductAccessories` DID read it and its **Add button did not clear it**, so opening Add after a cancelled Edit left the form populated with the previous row and submitted a PATCH against it under a dialog headed "Add New Accessory". That is the worst of the three, because it does not look broken: it looks like a prefilled duplicate and silently edits. `client/src/hooks/use-record-dialog.ts` resets the mode and the form TOGETHER from one `apply` site for every action; `resetWith: undefined` meaning "empty it" is the distinction that matters, since not resetting at all is what left the stale values on screen. **The decision is a pure function** so the property is testable without a React renderer - this repo has no `@testing-library/react`, and adding one to test three state transitions is the wrong trade.

STATE THE PROPERTY OVER EVERY ACTION RATHER THAN CHECKING THREE CASES: "editing is non-null exactly when the form was reset with a row" is one assertion that covers create, edit and close, and it keeps covering a fourth action nobody has written yet.

A GENERATED FILE THAT THROWS AWAY THE PROSE IN IT IS A FILE NOBODY CAN ANNOTATE (SEC-EDGE-001, round 82). `check:raw-body-writes --update-baseline` regenerated its default `note` on every run, so the paragraph explaining why the count had jumped 12 -> 23 - the one thing stopping a reader taking it for a regression - was silently discarded by the next tighten. I rewrote it, tightened, and lost it again inside the same round, and only the test asserting the note caught it. The writer preserves an existing note now. **Every ratchet in this repo is annotated** (`docs/nplus1-triage.json`, `docs/edge-rbac-triage.json`, the phantom-tables baseline) and that annotation is the difference between a worklist and an undifferentiated list - so any baseline WRITER that regenerates a header is worth checking for the same behaviour.

CHECK WHETHER A PAGE IS ASKING FOR SOMETHING IMPOSSIBLE BEFORE DELETING WHAT IT ASKS FOR. `DataEnrichment.tsx` reads `contacts.bySource`, `contacts.byStatus`, `contacts.byLevel` and `companies.byIndustry` as ARRAYS of `{key, count}`; `GET /enrichment/analytics` sent a flat object with two of the four, as maps - so the whole tab rendered "No data available" and three headline cards showed 0, on a 200, with nothing logged. The tempting fix is to delete the two "extra" sections; both turned out to be REAL columns (`enriched_contacts.management_level`, `enriched_companies.primary_industry`), so all four are derived. `management_level` is one no importer fills, which is named in `unbacked` rather than charted as though it meant something - and the test verifies that claim against the mapper instead of taking the comment's word for it.

AN UNPAGED `.select()` BEHIND AN AGGREGATE IS WORSE THAN BEHIND A LIST. The same endpoint tallied every enriched contact with a bare select, which stops at PostgREST's default page size - so a tenant past it got a tally of the first page presented as a total. A truncated LIST at least looks short; a truncated COUNT looks like a fact. Use `fetchAllRows` wherever a handler counts rows in memory.

`toContain('<key>')` IS A SUBSTRING MATCH, SO RENAMING THE KEY PASSES. A mutant that renamed the response key `unbacked` to `unbackedX` survived an assertion that the branch `toContain('unbacked')`. Anchor on the key as emitted (`/\bunbacked: \[/`) - the same overlap trap as `subject:` inside `activity_subject:` and `products:` inside `proposed_products:`.

WHEN A LESSON SAYS "WORTH CHECKING ELSEWHERE", CHECK IT PROPERLY RATHER THAN BY INFERENCE (round 83). Round 82 found `check:raw-body-writes` regenerating its `note` on every `--update-baseline` and recorded that other writers were worth a look. Grepping for the shape gave 36 suspects and was wrong about most of them; the answer came from RUNNING each writer against a backup of its own baseline, diffing the note, and restoring. 33 writers, exactly **five** destroying a hand-written note (`check-fk-id-types`, `check-month-arithmetic`, `check-no-random-metrics`, `check-insert-tenant-id`, `check-unwritten-tables`), two of them owning worklists this file names by hand. The rest regenerate text identical to what is in the file - not broken, one annotation away from it. **An empirical sweep needs its own vacuity check**: a writer that fails to run also leaves the note unchanged and reads as safe, so record whether each invocation actually rewrote the file. All 33 did.

A RATCHET'S STALE-ENTRY ASSERTION IS THE ONE THAT MATTERS. `server/tests/unit/baseline-notes-preserved.test.ts` records the 28 non-preserving writers as shrink-only, and its second test requires every name on the list to still be a writer AND still emit a note - because a stale entry is exactly where a real regression hides behind a green run. Six of the first thirty-seven names were wrong that way: the list had been built from "writes a file" rather than "writes a note".

`check:unwritten-tables` WAS RED ON `origin/main`, verified by checking out main's copies and running it - not a local break. `manager_insights` and `sales_metrics` are read by `supabase/functions/crm/index.ts` and written by nothing, both with live callers (CrmGoalsDashboard's insights panel guards on `length > 0` and so renders nothing at all; its conversion analysis reads the second). **The answer to AUDIT-028's question belongs at the READ SITE, not only in the JSON list**, because that is where the next reader is: `manager_insights` wants a PRODUCER, `sales_metrics` wants a DERIVATION over tables that already hold every input. Run the guards CI runs, on main, occasionally - a hard gate can sit red for a long time without anyone's change being the cause.

CORRECTION: the AUDIT-020 note above says `check:no-random-metrics` is "baseline 34 across 8 files". AUDIT-021 drove it to **zero** and it is a hard gate at 0 now; the baseline file's own note says so. That is the ninth stale claim corrected in this file by reading the tree.

A DEFAULT OF `[]` CAN UNLOCK A BUTTON (CR-033, round 84). `DeliveryAcceptance.tsx` read its checklist as `data ?? []`, derived `blockers` from it, and gated the Accept button on `blockers.length === 0` - so a FAILED fetch produced zero blockers and enabled the button. A customer could sign for a delivery whose checklist never loaded, and the record that page writes IS the evidence that a machine was installed and inspected. CR-033 is usually about a failure reading as "no data yet"; here it reads as "nothing left to check", which is the same lie pointed at a signature. Wherever an empty collection feeds a PERMISSION rather than a list, the gate needs the query's `isError` and `isLoading` as well as its length. THE REASON THESE ACCUMULATE is the size of the available error state: the full-page `ErrorState` is twelve rems of centred illustration, wrong in a twenty-pixel panel slot, so every panel that hit this returned `null` instead - and a null panel reads as "no competitor recorded on this deal", which is the one thing a rep most wants to be true. `client/src/components/ui/inline-query-error.tsx` is the card-sized answer: one line, the destructive colour, an optional retry, and it says the data could not be LOADED, never that there is none. A DELIBERATE FALLBACK MUST SAY IT IS ONE - `RecordPageLayout` keeping the shipped layout and `MyDayLayout` keeping the default card set are both correct behaviour and both used to be silent, so an admin whose saved layout failed to load saw a different page with no explanation; `usingDefaultLayout` is rendered now.

A QUERY KEY THAT IS ONE ELEMENT CANNOT BE INVALIDATED BY ITS PREFIX (CR-033, round 84). `OpportunityRadar` invalidated `[key.split('?')[0]]` against `['/api/opportunity-radar?status=open']`. TanStack matches query keys ELEMENT-WISE, so a prefix of the first element matches nothing - the board never refreshed after a scan, a dismiss or a convert, and the page looked frozen while being entirely correct. `invalidateApiPath` is the helper; `check:invalidation-keys` is the gate, and it was red on main. Same family as the CLAUDE.md rule that a query key is a URL: only put path segments in a key, and invalidate by an element you actually have.

A COMMENT CAN DESCRIBE THE FIX SOMEBODY MEANT TO MAKE (CR-033, round 84). `supabase/functions/crm/index.ts` carried "Capped by fetchAllRows' paging" directly above a bare `.select()` on won deals, so the tenant revenue total was silently one PostgREST page of deals. `today-dashboard`'s `newLeads` had the other half of the shape - it fetched every lead created today only to read `.length`, and the number on the screen is the one thing that cannot show it was capped. `check:row-caps` catches both; it too was red on main. **RUN ALL 88 GUARDS, NOT THE ONES YOUR CHANGE TOUCHES.** Three were red on `origin/main` this round and nobody could say when any of them started - the second time in three rounds that a whim run of the full set found a live defect. Seven cannot run in this container and that is worth knowing before reading a red as a regression: `check:drift`, `check:schema`, `check:schema:docker`, `check:schema:ssh` and `check:stage-resolution` need `DATABASE_URL`, `check:security` is `npm audit`, and `check:error-shape` is a pre-existing ratchet regression in `routes-renewal-management.ts`.

A GUARD THAT LOOKS FOR ONE NAME REPORTS THE OTHER SPELLING AS A DEFECT (CR-033, round 84). `check:query-states` asked only for `isError`, so `SystemMonitoring`'s `{error && ...}` - a component that destructures `error` and renders it - was a false positive. It now counts a bound `error` that appears somewhere BEYOND the destructure that bound it, which keeps the real property (binding it and ignoring it still fails) while dropping the false one. A baseline holding a known non-defect is where a real one hides, so widening the rule was part of the fix rather than an aside. Baseline 202 -> 180.

BIND AN ASSERTION TO THE CONSTRUCT, AND CHECK THE CODE'S OWN PUNCTUATION CANNOT CROSS IT (round 84). One mutant survived TWICE before the assertion held, and both repairs generalise. Slicing a function body to `'\n  }'` found a closing brace that does not exist at that indentation, so `indexOf` returned -1, `slice(0, -1)` ran to the end of the file, and a mutant returning 0 instead of null on an error path survived. Narrowing the match with `[^}]*` then hit the `}` inside the branch's OWN template literal - the log line interpolates the family name, so the closing brace of that interpolation ends the match - and reported a correct file as wrong. The version that works takes the first `return` after the error branch opens and compares it. Fourth round running that a fixed or sloppily-bounded window let a mutant through; a fixed character count, a brace at a guessed indentation and a character class are all the same mistake.

TWO TESTS BROKE ON CORRECT WORK AND BOTH WERE REBOUND, NOT RELAXED (round 84). `acceptance-capture` pinned `canSubmit` as one exact source string, which was true only while the expression fitted on one line - so the fix that strictly STRENGTHENED that gate failed it. It asserts the three conjuncts and the absence of `||` now. `today-dashboard-real-tables` pinned `newLeads: countOf(newLeads)` when the property is "a family whose read failed answers null, not 0"; converting that read to a head count broke the pin and not the property. A guard failing on correct work is information about the guard, and the repair is to assert the property rather than the layout.

A COMMENT CAN ASSERT A CONTROL FROM A FILE THAT DOES NOT CONTAIN IT (SEC-EDGE-001, round 85). `navigation-permissions.ts` opens `/territories` to anyone who can see an opportunity and explains why that is safe: "Creating one is a management act and is gated in the edge function." `supabase/functions/sales-territories/` had no role check of any kind - any authenticated tenant member could create a territory, rename one, take its `owner_id`, change its `monthly_quota` or DELETE it. **The false claim and the missing check were in different files, so neither read as wrong on its own**: the nav rule looks deliberate because it is, and the handler looks like every other ungated CRUD function. `printer-monitoring`'s "requires auth" comment at least sat above the branch it lied about. When a comment points at another file for the control, open that file. AND A TERRITORY IS NOT A LOOKUP ROW - three readers resolve a rep's book through it: `_shared/territory.ts` matches the free-text `business_records.territory` against a name or code AT READ TIME (COP-B10), so defining or renaming one claims or releases every account naming it with nothing rewritten and nothing to review; `shared/territory-membership.ts` makes `owner_id`/`team_members` the opportunity radar's default filter (COP-B09); and the forecast reports commit against `monthly_quota`. DELETE hides best: the accounts naming the deleted territory land in the coverage report's UNASSIGNED bucket, which reads as "nobody has assigned these yet" rather than "somebody removed the territory".

DERIVE A FAMILY FROM WHAT ITS MEMBERS IMPORT, NOT FROM WHICH NAME THEY RESEMBLE (SEC-EDGE-001, round 85). Seven edge functions import `_shared/catalog-import-runner.ts` and serve one catalogue shape: product-models, managed-services, product-accessories, professional-services, supplies, software-products and `service-products`. Six gated their writes on `operations.inventory.manage` and the seventh gated nothing, bulk CSV import included. `software-products` carries a header calling itself "the sibling nobody did", so this family has been swept TWICE by comparing a function to the one it most resembles, and a member survived both passes. CLAUDE.md's existing rule ("when you gate one of a family, check the rest of the family in the same pass") is right and was applied to a family defined by resemblance. `server/tests/unit/catalogue-and-territory-gates.test.ts` derives the member list by reading `supabase/functions/` and keeping whatever imports the runner, with a floor so a walk that stops matching fails rather than passing in silence - an eighth member added ungated now fails a test rather than waiting for a third sweep.

A WRITE BRANCH WITH NO CALLER IS A DOOR NOBODY HAS WALKED THROUGH YET (SEC-EDGE-001, round 85). Three of this round's five gates cost nothing to install because nothing calls the branches: `enabled-products`' POST/PUT/DELETE (ProductHubUnified only GETs that prefix and invalidates it - every enable, bulk-enable, import and pricing edit goes through `/api/catalog`, already gated), and both settings endpoints below. Checking the callers is what turns "this might break the page" into "this is free", and it is the same check that stops a gate breaking a feature. Grep the client trees for the prefix AND the verb before deciding either way.

TWO TENANT-WIDE SETTINGS FOUND BY LOOKING FOR A SETTINGS TABLE (SEC-EDGE-001, round 85). Rather than reading thirteen remaining functions in order, list which ones write a `*_settings` row - that is where a tenant-wide decision lives. `PUT /service/knowledge-search/settings` writes `federated_opt_in`, which sends this dealer's service knowledge into a pool shared with OTHER TENANTS, and the row stores `consentedAt` plus `consentedByUserId`: **it is built to record who agreed while letting anyone be that person**, and a consent record naming whoever clicked is worse than none, because it reads as evidence. `PUT /deal-desk-copilot/settings` writes `gp_floor_pct`, the gross-profit floor deciding which quotes need the deal desk - QUOTE-006's guardrail edited by the party it checks. Both gated at MANAGER on the BRANCH: the copilot renders inside QuoteBuilderPage and knowledge search is a technician's daily work, so a file-level gate would have taken both features from their users.

TWO OPEN-BY-DESIGN VERDICTS WORTH THE REASONING (SEC-EDGE-001, round 85). `email-sequences` writes an `email_unsubscribes` row, which IS a suppression - the shape gated in `renewal-autoquote` this same story - but one that only ever STOPS mail rather than starting it, so the failure direction is safe and gating it would let a rep keep emailing someone who asked them to stop. **Ask which way a control fails before copying a verdict from a matching shape.** And `predictive-dispatch` looks identical to `predictive-failure`, gated at SUPERVISOR earlier in this story, until you look for what that one has and this one does not: a settings row, a confidence threshold and an agent kill switch. Same shape, different verdict, because the privileged thing was never the write - it was the threshold beside it.

RUNNABLE IS NOT RUN (CR-023, round 86). `check:error-shape` had a 1,392-entry baseline, a `--update-baseline` writer, an entry in package.json - and appeared in NO GitHub workflow. So a correct security fix (WF-S-11 authenticating `routes-renewal-management.ts`, answering its new refusals with `res.status(401).json({ error })`) took the count 36 -> 46 and nothing reported it. **Check `.github/workflows/ci.yml` as well as package.json before treating a guard as enforced**; `grep -c "npm run check:" .github/workflows/ci.yml` against the script count answers it in one line. The second cost is worse than the missed regression: a guard nobody executes accumulates a baseline that ROTS, and four of its 61 files did not exist - `routes-lead-assignment.ts` (41), `routes-cross-module.ts` (14), `routes/task-routes.ts` (12), `routes-auto-lead-routing.ts` (4), all deleted by earlier rounds of the same session. Such an entry claims credit for debt that was deleted rather than converted, and a file returning under that name arrives PRE-FORGIVEN for everything it carries. Baseline 1392 -> 1285 once the guard ran; it is in CI now.

A BASELINE WRITER CAN LAUNDER A PER-FILE GROWTH THROUGH A FALLING TOTAL (CR-023, round 86). `--update-baseline` refused only when the TOTAL rose, so a tighten converting forty responses in one file while ten appeared in another absorbed the ten in silence - exactly the growth the normal run exists to catch. It refuses per FILE now and names each one. **Fifth time this session a total stood in for a property**, after `applyUserScope` calls, `fetchAllRows` calls, `RESERVED_SEGMENTS` words and `isMissingTableError` checks - and the first inside a WRITER rather than a test, which is worse: a failing test tells you something, a writer silently records the wrong line. Every `--update-baseline` in `scripts/` is worth the same question. VERIFY IT BEHAVIOURALLY, not by reading the source: inflate one baseline entry so the total falls by thousands, add one finding elsewhere, and watch it refuse.

CONVERTING A FILE'S ERROR BODIES IS NOT A VOTE ON WHETHER THE FILE LIVES (CR-023, round 86). `routes-renewal-management.ts` is held-not-deleted pending AUDIT-026 - a complete renewal-management feature over four real tables, never wired to a screen, beside a different renewal model that was. Making its 46 responses answer `{ message, code, details, requestId }` through `server/lib/error-response.ts` costs nothing whichever way that product call goes, so the right move is to convert AND assert the header still says "DO NOT 'FIX' THIS FILE WITHOUT READING AUDIT-026". The test also asserts the AUTH that WF-S-11 added survives, because the regression came from a correct security fix and converting its responses must not quietly undo it.

THE COMMENT TRAP FIRED IN A TEST WRITTEN WITH THE RULE IN FRONT OF ME (round 86). Asserting `req.headers['x-tenant-id']` is absent from the converted file failed against a correct file: the header QUOTES that broken read in prose explaining what WF-S-11 fixed. Sixth occurrence this session and the first self-inflicted one. The rule has a second half worth stating, because it is what makes the first half safe to apply: strip comments for an absence assertion, and assert against the RAW source only where the comment IS the property - the AUDIT-026 annotation in the same file is checked against raw on purpose.

RUN THE SCRIPT A PLAN RESTS ON BEFORE BELIEVING THE PLAN (COP-B00, round 87). Five priority-1 stories wait on COP-B00, and COP-B00's whole unblocking path is `npm run crm:reconcile` then `--apply`, run ONCE against production by a human with credentials. Nobody had ever executed it. Run against a scratch Postgres 16 with all 80 journalled migrations replayed (the COP-M07 recipe) and rows seeded to reach every classifier verdict, the report and apply paths both work - ids preserved, a second `--apply` copies nothing, all three documented exit codes hold - and the MAPPER migrates every prospect as an active customer. `companies.business_record_type` has FOUR live values (the canonical typeMap is in `supabase/functions/business-records/index.ts`: Lead, Customer, Prospect, Former Customer) and `mapCompanyRow` tested for 'lead' and sent everything else to 'customer'. **Prospect is the one a live surface produces**: `LeadsPage.tsx`'s "Convert to Prospect" writes exactly `{ business_record_type: 'Prospect', activity: 'qualified' }`, and `ProspectsPage` is a routed board over it. As a customer that row gets `status: 'active'` - 'qualified' is a LEAD status, so it fails the customer vocabulary and coerces to the customer fallback - and an account that has never bought anything then appears in churn risk, QBR, contract renewal and every customer count. The coercion counter fired, which is worse than silence in one respect: it reports "an activity outside the vocabulary", which reads as a data-quality note rather than as a lifecycle stage being destroyed. **When a mapper collapses a vocabulary to a boolean, count the vocabulary first** - and derive it from the tree (a test reads that typeMap, so a fifth value fails rather than silently becoming a customer).

A ONE-SHOT SCRIPT'S HEADER AND ITS LAST LINE ARE BOTH LOAD-BEARING (COP-B00, round 87). Two smaller defects in the same tool, and both only matter on the run it exists for. Its usage block documented `node scripts/crm-table-reconcile.mjs`, which cannot work: the script imports a `.ts` and node answers ERR_UNKNOWN_FILE_EXTENSION before reading a row. The npm script uses tsx and works, so the header told you to do the thing that does not. And the refusal line printed "Nothing was merged" unconditionally - including directly under "✓ Copied 4 row(s) into business_records" - and it is the LAST thing on screen after a long report, so an operator reads it about a run that merged rows and re-runs or escalates. Say what happened, not what usually happens; the exit code stays 1 because a human is still owed a decision.

`notnull` IS A POSTGRES OPERATOR, NOT AN ALIAS (COP-M00, round 87). `check:drift` died at `syntax error at or near "notnull"` (42601) on every database it has ever been pointed at: `(is_nullable = 'NO') notnull` parses as the POSTFIX "IS NOT NULL" operator. It exits 2 on that, so it fails safe - but the schema-drift tool of the migration story has never once reported on drift. Renamed to `is_required` rather than quoted, because `"notnull"` would parse and leave the next editor one unquoted copy from the same error.

`information_schema` NAMES NEITHER YOUR ENUM NOR YOUR ELEMENT TYPE (COP-M00, round 87). Once it ran, `check:drift` produced 332 findings of which 331 were comparisons it cannot win: `data_type` answers 'USER-DEFINED' for every enum and 'ARRAY' for every array and never the specific type, while the drizzle side names the enum (`review_decision`) or the element (`text array`) - so all 252 enum columns and 78 array columns mismatched by construction. Collapse the enum at COLLECTION time via `PgEnumColumn`, where enum-ness is still known, because `getSQLType()` returns only the name and nothing downstream can tell an enum from a domain. **332 findings -> 2, both actionable**, one of them real drift (`location_history.id` is uuid in drizzle and varchar live). A guard whose output is 99.7% false positives is one nobody reads - the same rule this repo applies to a baseline holding known non-defects, one layer out.

MEASURE AN EXIT CODE WITHOUT A PIPE (round 87). `cmd | tail -3; echo $?` reports TAIL's status, and twice in one round that read as a defect that was not there - once nearly producing a write-up claiming `check:drift` "crashes and exits 0" when it correctly exits 2. Run the command redirected to /dev/null and read `$?`, or capture it before the pipe. Same round, same discipline in the other direction: seeing `DEFAULT 'gen_random_uuid()'` quoted as a string literal in a DDL (7 tables carry that spelling against 449 with the unquoted form), I predicted the second insert would violate the primary key. Two inserts produced two distinct real uuids. **Prove it against the database before it becomes a finding** - a wrong prediction caught in a shell costs a minute, and the same prediction in a story note costs whoever reads it next.
