A GUARD THAT CONNECTS TO AN EMPTY DATABASE CAN STILL PASS VACUOUSLY (round 87). `check:stage-resolution` reports "Checked 0 deal(s) against 0 legacy stage(s) and 0 canonical stage(s) ... ✓ Every deal resolves to exactly one canonical pipeline stage." Its own note says it exits 2 when it CANNOT CONNECT so "did not run" is never read as "passed" - it guards the connect case and not the empty one, so a wrong `DATABASE_URL`, a fresh replica or a just-reset staging database reports green. Recorded rather than fixed: it wants a row-count floor, the same shape every walk-based guard here carries.

`check:drift` IS A ZERO-TOLERANCE GATE NOW, AND THE ONE FINDING IT HAD WAS FIXED IN THE DECLARATION (COP-M00, round 88). With all 80 journalled migrations replayed into a scratch Postgres 16, it reports NO DRIFT across 682 tables - something nobody has been able to say, because until round 87 its SQL had never parsed. Its single finding was `location_history.id`, `uuid` in drizzle and `varchar` live, and **the declaration is what moved**: 343 of this schema's `id` columns are varchar against 300 uuid, `tenants.id` is `varchar PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid()`, nothing holds a foreign key to the column, and converting a primary key on a table that takes a GPS fix per technician per minute is a full table rewrite under an exclusive lock for no behavioural gain. `session_id` beside it STAYS uuid because it references `mobile_service_sessions.id`, which is uuid live - correcting the whole file would have created drift rather than removed it. **When a drift guard reports one mismatch, the question is which side is wrong, and the live shape usually wins.** Beware the replay too: `while read` drops a final line with no trailing newline, so round 87's replay silently applied 79 of 80 and reported a phantom MISSING_COLUMN.

A GUARD CAN GUARD "COULD NOT CONNECT" AND STILL PASS ON AN EMPTY DATABASE (COP-M07, round 88). `check:stage-resolution`'s header promises "did not run" is never read as "passed", and it delivered that for the connect case only. Against a replayed schema with no rows it printed "Checked 0 deal(s) against 0 legacy stage(s) and 0 canonical stage(s) ... ✓ Every deal resolves to exactly one canonical pipeline stage" - true of an empty set, on the guard this file says to run before and after touching any stage writer. A wrong `DATABASE_URL`, a fresh replica or a just-reset staging database all report green. It exits 2 now, keyed on DEALS deliberately: a tenant can legitimately have no canonical stages before its template is bootstrapped and a deployment can have no legacy stages once the migration finishes, but a database with no deals at all is the wrong database. **Every guard that reads rows wants this floor, not just the ones that connect.**

`count(*)` COMES BACK FROM node-postgres AS A STRING (round 88). The floor above was written `totals.deals === 0` and silently never fired, because the value is `'0'` - a bigint does not fit a JS number safely, so the driver hands back text. Caught by RUNNING it against an empty database rather than by reading it, which is the whole lesson: a floor that cannot fire is the same vacuous pass it was added to close, and the only thing that distinguishes the two is execution.

CHECK WHERE A MUTATION LANDED BEFORE CALLING THE TEST WEAK (round 88). Two mutants survived and only one was a test weakness. The real one: an assertion bounded the floor's block by searching for the next `process.exit(2)`, so a mutant changing the floor to exit 0 made the search run ON into the CATCH block's exit(2) - and the catch also says "unknown, not a pass", so every assertion matched text from a different construct. Fifth window-crossing this session. The other was not a weakness: `sessionId: uuid('session_id')` appears three times in that file and the mutation hit line 70, a different table entirely, so the declaration under test was never touched. **Resolve a column through `getTableConfig` rather than by reading source when the same line appears in several tables** - the source scan and the mutation both land on whichever comes first.

`check:schema` IS A REPORT GENERATOR WEARING A GUARD'S NAME (round 88). It always exits 0, and running it OVERWRITES two COMMITTED artifacts - `docs/DATABASE_SCHEMA.md` and `database-schema-report.json` - with whatever database it was pointed at, and drops two more beside them that git does not track (a quick reference and a validation helper). CORRECTED 2026-09-20 (round 89): this line first called all four committed, and `check:prd-refs` caught it, because the two untracked ones only resolved while a previous run's output happened to be sitting in the working tree. A generated file is not a reference target. Point it at a scratch database, commit, and the repo's schema documentation now describes a container that no longer exists. Revert those four after running it, and do not read its exit code as a verdict.

`db:migrate` COULD NOT CREATE A DATABASE, AND A PASSING STORY SAID IT COULD (PA-032, round 89). That story's evidence line reads "db:migrate exit 0 on an empty database, 668 tables, **43 migrations recorded**". There are 80, and it broke at number 72, so the disaster-recovery guarantee had been gone for 29 migrations with nothing re-checking. **A verification carries the number it was taken at; compare that number to today's before quoting it.** Two defects, each hiding the next, because drizzle runs the whole chain in ONE transaction and stops at the first error - so a fresh database ended with ZERO tables either way. `0072_seed_role_catalogue.sql` wrote 21 role_type values the enum has never had ('platform_role' where the enum says 'platform_admin', and three more of the same shape), in ONE INSERT with no statement-breakpoint, so all 45 rows failed together and **not one row has ever been inserted on any database** - which is what made editing a committed migration the right call rather than the usual mistake. `0073` then did `UPDATE roles SET ..., updated_at = NOW()` on a table that has `created_at` and no `updated_at`. THE CASUALTY WAS THE FEATURE 0072 EXISTS FOR: `signup` answers MISSING_ADMIN_ROLE when `roles.code='COMPANY_ADMIN'` is absent and tells the operator to "apply the migration chain and retry" - the one thing that could not work. Fixed and verified end to end: exit 0, 683 tables, 80 migrations, 52 roles, COMPANY_ADMIN at level 7.

A STATEMENT-TOLERANT REPLAY PROVES THE DDL, NOT THAT THE CHAIN IS APPLICABLE (round 89). Rounds 87 and 88 built 682-table scratch databases with `psql -v ON_ERROR_STOP=0` and never saw either defect: the DDL all lands, the schema looks complete, and the two failing statements are skipped in silence. It is the right tool for "does the schema match drizzle" and the wrong one for "can this repo provision a database". Use the real migrator for the second question - `DATABASE_URL=... npx tsx server/lib/migrate.ts` against an empty database, which is the only thing that exercises the one-transaction semantics.

`npm run check:migration-enums` (round 89) replays the chain in journal order, tracking every enum's membership (`CREATE TYPE ... AS ENUM` plus `ALTER TYPE ... ADD VALUE`) and every column declared with an enum type, then checks each INSERT's literals in those positions: 197 enum types, 260 enum columns. A value that is not a member AT THAT POINT in the chain is a guaranteed 22P02, and under one transaction that is not one failed migration - it is no database at all. Blind spots are in its header (values built by expression, UPDATEs, columns given an enum type by a later ALTER TABLE) and it exits 2 rather than passing if it parses fewer than ten types or columns.

EXPORT THE ANALYSIS SO A MUTANT CANNOT HIDE BEHIND A CONSTANT (round 89). A source-level assertion that the guard "accumulates ALTER TYPE ADD VALUE" SURVIVED a mutant that gutted the accumulation loop, because the regex CONSTANT it matched was still in the file - and the real chain happens to add `consent_type.recording` without any migration inserting it, so the branch is correct and unexercised. Neither a missing test nor redundant code: an unreachable-today branch that a source check cannot distinguish from a working one. The fix is `analyzeChain()` exported and CALLED with a fixture, accept case and reject case. **And the reject case earned its place immediately**: the accept case first passed vacuously because the fixture indented its closing paren, the CREATE TABLE pattern did not match, and no enum column was tracked at all - so "no findings" meant "nothing looked at". Any fixture-based accept case wants a reject case beside it for exactly this reason.

A GENERATED FILE IS NOT A REFERENCE TARGET (round 89). `check:prd-refs` went red on a CLAUDE.md line calling four schema artifacts "committed" when git tracks only two; the other two are dropped by `check:schema` and had resolved only while a previous run's output was sitting in the working tree. Before naming a path in prose, `git ls-files` it - a file being present is not the same as a file being in the repo.

A CHECK CONSTRAINT MARKED `NOT VALID` REFUSES NEW ROWS AND RE-CHECKS NO OLD ONES (COP-M07, round 90). `npm run seed:demo` died at 23514 on `service_tickets_status_check` because the seeder still wrote `status: 'resolved'` and `0078_wf_v05_ticket_vocabulary.sql` had fixed that column's vocabulary to open|assigned|scheduled|en_route|on_site|in_progress|on_hold|completed|cancelled. `NOT VALID` is why nobody hit it: every established database kept its pre-existing rows and only an INSERT is refused, so the defect is invisible everywhere except the one path that matters - provisioning a new environment. Both apparent sibling writers were checked rather than assumed and both were false alarms (`ai-employee/_execute.ts` returns 'resolved' in an AI helper's own object, `portal-service/classify.ts` writes a portal-request timeline with its own vocabulary), so one seeder line was the whole thing. `server/tests/unit/demo-seed-vocabularies.test.ts` derives both vocabularies from the migration and anchors on `ADD CONSTRAINT` rather than the constraint name, because that migration's prose header names the constraints it is fixing - the comment trap for the seventh time this session.

A REACHABILITY WALK IS ONLY AS GOOD AS ITS LIST OF FRONT DOORS (AUDIT-034, round 90). `check:server-orphans` rooted at `server/index.ts` alone, so every CLI in the repo read as dead product code: `db:migrate` runs `server/lib/migrate.ts`, `seed:demo` runs `server/seeds/seed-all-demo-data.ts`, and seed:rbac, seed:kpis, seed:reports, kb-cli, updater-cli and seven more are the same shape - **14 of 135 baselined entries, in a list whose own note calls it a TODO**, so a tenth of the todos were "delete this working tool". The roots are `server/index.ts` plus every server file a `package.json` script executes, DERIVED from the scripts block rather than listed, so a new seed script naming any file under `server/seeds/` becomes a root the day it exists. Baseline 135 -> 121; `check:story-orphans` 39 -> 35. Same thing `check:unreferenced-edge-fns` had to learn when pg_cron turned out to be a caller: **a repo has as many entry points as it has ways to start**, and a walk from one of them calls the rest dead.

ONE GUARD'S BLIND SPOT SURFACING AS ANOTHER GUARD'S FINDING is the shape worth recognising here. Nothing about `check:server-orphans` looked wrong from inside it - it walked correctly, its baseline was shrink-only, its note was honest. What exposed it was a SECOND guard consuming its output: round 89's note named `server/lib/migrate.ts` as a verified deliverable, `check:story-orphans` asked the orphan baselines whether anything reached it, got "no", and failed five tests in a file I had not touched. When a guard fails on work that is correct, check whether it is reporting the other guard's premise rather than your change.

A FIXTURE CAN ONLY DISTINGUISH IMPLEMENTATIONS IT CONTAINS AN EXAMPLE OF (round 90). The mutant that dropped the `server/` prefix from the script-scanning regex SURVIVED, because every command in the fixture named a `server/` path - a pattern matching any `*.ts` anywhere was indistinguishable from the right one. Adding a script that runs `scripts/check-phantom-columns.ts` killed it. Same family as round 89's territory fixture with no unowned row and the guard fixture whose story ids were `A-1`: **make the fixture contain the case that separates the implementations, not just the case the code handles.**

A NULL SURVIVES SERIALISATION AND AN UNDEFINED DOES NOT, WHICH IS WHY A BLANKET UPDATE OBJECT FAILS ONLY SOMETIMES (SEC-EDGE-001, round 91). `PUT /handoff-task-templates/:id` built one object naming every column, and four of the five fields were safe by accident: `body.templateName` and `body.tasks` are `undefined` when the caller omits them, and `JSON.stringify` DROPS an undefined key, so PostgREST never saw them. The fifth was `handoff_type: normalizeHandoffType(undefined)`, and that normalizer returns **null** - which stringify KEEPS. So a rename-only PUT sent exactly one column, `handoff_type = NULL`, into a NOT NULL column: 23502, proven on Postgres 16, surfaced to the caller as a generic 500. Every partial edit of a template was impossible while four fifths of the same object were fine. **The tell is a helper that normalises to null inside a payload literal** - `?? null`, a lookup that misses, a coercion that fails - because it converts "the caller said nothing" into "the caller said nothing is there". Build the plan field by field from what was actually sent (COP-B03), and prefer a pure exported function for it so the property is tested with real inputs rather than by reading the handler as text, which is how rounds 89 and 90 each lost a mutant to a constant still present in the file.

A FLOOR THAT ASSERTS A WORKLIST IS NOT EMPTY FAILS THE DAY THE WORKLIST IS FINISHED (SEC-EDGE-001, round 91). Ten test files had each grown their own copy of `expect(triage.counts.unexamined).toBeGreaterThan(0)`. It was the right assertion for nineteen rounds - it stopped anyone clearing `docs/edge-rbac-triage.json` by GUESSING verdicts from function names, which this story did four times and had to overturn - and settling the last entry broke all ten at once. Same shape as `phantom-cols-reachable-zero` in round 72, and the same repair: follow the floor's own purpose instead of relaxing it. **THE PROPERTY WAS NEVER "SOMETHING IS UNEXAMINED"** - it is that no verdict is unreasoned, and that an entry leaving that state records what was READ to get it there, which holds whether or not the list is empty. It lives once in `server/tests/unit/edge-rbac-triage-integrity.test.ts` now (counts derived from the entries, verdict vocabulary, no duplicate fn, every entry reasoned, every needs-gate saying why a level check is not the answer) and the ten files keep a one-line pointer. **Ten copies of one invariant is the liability the failure exposed**, not the failure itself: the duplication is why a single correct change turned ten files red.

NAME THE EXCEPTIONS, DO NOT COUNT THEM. Fifteen triage entries were examined before the paths-record discipline started, so "every examined entry says what it read" cannot be asserted flatly without fabricating fifteen records. They are listed BY NAME in a shrink-only array, asserted in both directions - a new examined entry with no paths record fails, AND a listed name that gains one must leave the list - so it cannot rot into a pre-forgiveness for whatever is added later, which is the defect `check:error-shape`'s baseline had when four of its files no longer existed. A count says how much is missing; a list says what.

A DEFAULT-ROW RULE NEEDS A READER THAT DOES NOT DEPEND ON THE WRITERS (round 91). `handoff_task_templates` had `is_default` set on create with nothing clearing the others, absent from the update map entirely (so a bootstrapped default could never be replaced), and read back with `.order('is_default').limit(1)` and no tiebreak - arbitrary selection wearing matching logic's clothes, the renewal-playbooks shape. Both halves were fixed, and the second is the one worth copying: **clearing the other defaults makes the common case right, and a `updated_at`/`id` tiebreak on the READ makes the answer deterministic whatever the table already holds.** A reader that is only correct when every writer behaved is one bad row from being wrong. Third defect in the same file: `if (existing?.tasks)` treats an EMPTY ARRAY as a usable template, so an active empty row handed operations a checklist with nothing on it - and the fix must not bootstrap a replacement, because a row already exists and inserting one per handoff piles up duplicates for as long as the empty one stays active.

`prd.json` IS MIXED-ENCODING AND A FULL RE-STRINGIFY CHURNS IT (round 91). Some non-ASCII in that file is raw (an em dash at offset 4312) and some is `\u`-escaped (a check mark later on), so neither `JSON.stringify(prd, null, 2)` nor the escape-everything variant reproduces it: appending one note through a parse-and-rewrite produced a **247-line diff** that buried the change. Splice into the RAW TEXT instead - find the story's `"notes": "` value, walk to its unescaped closing quote, and insert `json.dumps(note)[1:-1]` there - which gives the 1-line diff the change actually is, and round-trip the result before writing. The `docs/edge-rbac-triage.json` entry in this file records the same trap with a different remedy; the general rule is that a hand-maintained JSON file's exact serialisation is part of its contract.

ROW-SCOPED IS NOT GATED (SEC-EDGE-001 AC4, round 92). `check:edge-rbac` files a function as row-scoped when its reads narrow to the caller, and that is a statement about WHICH ROWS YOU CAN SEE - it says nothing about whether you may write one. `technician-management` sat in that category with **all five of its writes ungated**: creating a technician, editing one, adding a skill, setting availability, and DELETING a record. Two gating sweeps had already passed over it, because a function whose reads are scoped reads as protected. Its Express predecessor made the distinction the edge function lost - `PERMISSIONS.SERVICE.TECHNICIAN.MANAGE` on the writes, `.VIEW` on the reads - and that code IS seeded, so the split was deliberate and satisfiable. The router is still mounted and `/api/technician-management` is not proxied, so **dev has been the safe host and production the open one**, the dev/prod split running in its worse direction. When a function is classified row-scoped, ask separately what its writes require; the two controls answer different questions and only one of them was ever installed.

THE EXPRESS PREDECESSOR IS EVIDENCE ABOUT INTENT, NEVER A LIST TO COPY (SEC-EDGE-001 AC4). That criterion asked for the inventory - which permission each edge function's Express predecessor enforced - BEFORE the gating work, as the source of truth for what to install. It was never built, the gating went ahead on a different basis (mirroring `navigation-permissions.ts` with a LEVEL check), and building the inventory afterwards says that basis was right for a reason the story could not have known: SEC-EDGE-002 found two permission vocabularies here, so a code copied verbatim can be unsatisfiable. `/api/crm` is the worked example - **all four codes its predecessor named are unseeded**, so that gate denied everyone below platform admin, and copying it would have exported a lockout rather than closed a hole. `docs/edge-rbac-express-predecessors.md` (generated by `scripts/edge-rbac-predecessors.mjs`, `npm run check:edge-predecessors`) carries the seeded/unseeded verdict on every row for exactly that reason. Most predecessors were retired by PROD-008b and QUALITY-002, so **the live tree answers a fraction of the question** and the rest comes from git history at each file's last committed version - `git rev-list -n 1 HEAD -- <path>` then `git show <rev>^:<path>` recovers a deleted router's last state.

A TODO NAMING A GATE IS NOT A GATE, AND A SCAN THAT READS COMMENTS COUNTS IT AS ONE. `server/routes-deal-desk-copilot.ts` carries `// TODO(rbac): requirePermission(['sales.quote.view_margin'])`, and an inventory that reported it would claim a control nobody wrote. Strip comments before extracting ANY control from source - the same rule this file already gives for absence assertions, pointed at a measurement instead.

IMPORTING A SCRIPT FOR ITS PURE HELPERS MUST NOT RUN IT (round 92). The predecessor tool's unit test imports `analyzeRouter`/`isGap`, and top-level code in an ESM module executes on import - so the test regenerated a tracked doc from whatever the test tree happened to hold, silently, every run. Wrap the IO in `main()` and call it behind an argv check: **`import.meta.main` is a Deno API and is always undefined in Node**, which this file already records costing a seeder that ran and did nothing. `const isEntryPoint = !!process.argv[1] && resolve(process.argv[1]) === fileURLToPath(import.meta.url)`.

A VACUITY CHECK CAN ITSELF BE VACUOUS (round 92). Proving the new gap rule fires, I removed the gate CALL SITES from the function and the guard reported nothing - which read as the rule being broken. It was not: removing the call sites left the `requireSupervisor`/`denySupervisor` DEFINITIONS, which still name `requireRoleLevel`, so `check:edge-rbac` kept classifying the function as gated and there was no gap to find. Reverting the whole file to HEAD (`git stash push -q <path>`) proved the rule fires on the real pre-fix state. **Simulate a defect by restoring the code that had it, not by deleting the part you think matters** - a partial revert can leave the signal the guard keys on.

A MUTANT SURVIVED BECAUSE THE FIX REMOVED THE ONLY EXAMPLE (round 92). Disabling the row-scoped branch of the gap rule passed every test, because gating `technician-management` left no row-scoped row for it to find - the rule was load-bearing for the next regression and exercised by nothing. `isGap` is pure and exported now, tested against fixture rows including a row-scoped one whose predecessor gated a write. Round 90's lesson in a new costume: **a fixture can only distinguish implementations it contains an example of**, and fixing the tree is one of the ways the example disappears.

TWO COLUMNS CAN NAME THE SAME PERSON IN DIFFERENT ID SPACES, AND THE WRONG ONE RETURNS AN EMPTY WEEK (WF-V-07, round 93). `/technician-management/:id/schedule` receives a `technicians.id`. All three tables that actually schedule a technician key the assignee on `users.id`, which is provable from live readers rather than from the column names: `equipment-lifecycle`'s crew day does `.eq('technician_id', user.id)` on `installation_schedules` and `.eq('driver_id', user.id)` on `delivery_schedules`, and `service-tickets` hands `assigned_technician_id` to `rowInScope` against the caller's user id. **Rewriting the route straight onto `techId` would have returned nothing for every technician in the product** - the same silent empty the story was filed about, wearing a fix's clothes. Resolve `technicians.id -> user_id` first, and say so when a technician row has no `user_id`: a contractor with no login cannot be the subject of any of those columns, so an empty list would be a claim about their week rather than about what can be looked up. Before repointing any query at a "technician" column, find a WRITER or a working reader and see which id it compares against.

ANCHOR A TABLE-NAME GREP OR A SUBSTRING WILL TELL YOU A PHANTOM TABLE IS REAL (round 93). WF-V-07 says `work_orders` has no schema and no migration, which is true - and `grep -rn work_orders` returns 21 hits in `database-exports/schema.sql`, which reads as the story being wrong about a table that exists in production. Every one is `field_work_orders`, `mobile_work_orders`, `work_orders_accessed` or `work_orders_completed`. Use `grep -rnE "(^|[^a-z_])<name>([^a-z_]|$)"`. The same shape in reverse is already in this file (`/api/pipeline\b` matching `/api/pipeline-config`, because `-` is a word boundary); the rule is that a table or route name needs its own delimiters on both sides, every time.

A SHARED QUERY-BOUNDING HELPER IS INVISIBLE TO `check:phantom-cols` (round 93). Applying the date window through `const bound = (q) => q.gte('scheduled_date', ...)` put the literal on a function with no call chain, so the guard attributed `scheduled_date` to `technicians` - the last `.from()` it had seen - and reported a phantom column in correct code. That is round 79's finding again, and the remedy is the same: **restructure rather than baseline**, because a hard gate holding a known non-defect is where a real one hides. The bounds are inlined into all three chains, with open-ended sentinels (`EPOCH`, `FAR_FUTURE`) so an absent bound does not change the query SHAPE - a conditional `.gte()` is what pushed the code into a helper in the first place. `equipment-lifecycle` carries the same note above the same repetition, and I had read it an hour earlier and still wrote the helper.

DELETING A PHANTOM-TABLE FUNCTION MEANS FINDING ITS OTHER READERS FIRST. `work-orders` was the obvious one and `technician-management` was the one the story named; `service-contracts`' contract detail was a THIRD, reading `work_orders` for `serviceHistory` with the error discarded - so every contract reported an empty service history, which reads as "this equipment has been trouble-free" rather than "we could not look". It reads `service_tickets` against the contract's covered equipment now (that table has no `contract_id`), short-circuits rather than sending an `.in()` with no values, and answers **null** on a failed read. An anchored grep across the whole tree before the deletion is what found it; the story's evidence line named two of the three.

WHAT A DELETION LOSES GETS WRITTEN DOWN, AND WHERE THE CAPABILITY SURVIVES. `work-orders` had dedicated `/assign`, `/start`, `/complete`, `/parts` and `/labor` endpoints. Those actions all survive on `service_tickets`, which carries `assigned_technician_id`, `status`, `parts_used`, `labor_hours`, `resolution_notes` and `customer_signature`; what does NOT survive is per-entry labour and note logging, because `work_order_labor` and `work_order_notes` were phantom too. Saying which half is covered is what separates a deletion from silently retiring an idea.

A TRANSPORT FIX SENDS THE BILL TO THE OTHER HOST (SEO-TRANSPORT-001, round 94). Moving `SEODashboard`'s mutations off raw `fetch` onto `apiRequest` is what makes them carry a Bearer token and reach the functions host - and that is precisely what exposed the second half: **seven of the eleven endpoints the page calls had no branch in `supabase/functions/seo/index.ts`**, so the fix turned a request that silently went nowhere into one that 404s. The 404 is the better failure and it is not the feature. Whenever you convert a page's transport, enumerate the endpoints it calls against the function that will now receive them, in the same pass; PROD-013's own rule ("check the prod-side branch and the response shape before converting") is about shapes, and this is the coarser version - does the branch exist at all.

THREE FABRICATIONS IN ONE SECURITY PANEL, AND THE FIX FOR EACH IS DIFFERENT (round 94). `checkSecurityHeaders` returned `certificateValid: hasHttps` and `httpsRedirect: hasHttps` - two fields asserting checks nothing performed, derived from whether the URL string starts with `https://`. **A URL SCHEME IS NOT A CERTIFICATE CHECK**: what a successful request proves is that a handshake succeeded, not that a chain is valid, in date and issued for this host, so `certificateValid` is DELETED and named in `unbacked`, and the `certificate_valid` column is left NULL rather than filled with a boolean nothing measured. `httpsRedirect` was MEASURABLE and is measured - probe the `http://` form with `redirect: 'manual'` and see where it lands - staying **null** when the probe itself fails, because false says "it does not redirect" and null says nothing was observed. The third was `estimateCoreWebVitals()`, returning LCP 2500ms, CLS 0.1 and a performance score of 75 on any PageSpeed failure, which the route STORED: deleted, because a fabricated measurement that is persisted is indistinguishable from a real one forever after. The rule these resolve to is AUDIT-019's: delete a claim with no backing data, measure the one that can be measured, and say plainly what is not measured.

`|| 0` ON A METRIC IS A PERFECT SCORE (round 94). `checkCoreWebVitalsWithAPI` wrote `audits['largest-contentful-paint']?.numericValue || 0` on all eight vitals, so an audit Lighthouse did not return became 0ms LCP and 0 CLS - not "we did not measure" but instant rendering with no layout shift, on the two numbers the panel exists to show. NULL-IS-NOT-ZERO pointed at a performance metric, where the zero is not merely wrong but flattering. Keep a real zero (0 CLS is a measurement) and name what was absent.

`cheerio.css()` DOES NOT COMPUTE STYLES (round 94). `analyzeMobileFriendliness` counts "small text" with `$('*').filter(el => parseInt($(el).css('font-size')) < 12)`. `.css()` reads an inline `style` attribute and nothing else - there is no cascade and no stylesheet - so that figure only ever counts elements carrying an inline font-size, which is close to none on a modern page, while reading as a measurement of the page's typography. Worth knowing before porting any cheerio analyser: it is a parser, not a browser, and anything it reports about layout, computed style or visibility is about the markup alone.

A GUARD'S GRANULARITY CAN BE COARSER THAN THE GAP (round 94). `check:edge-path-coverage` keys on the first sub-segment, so serving `/seo/check/security` cleared `check` from its baseline while `/seo/check/broken-links` and `/seo/check/mobile` stayed unserved. Tightening was still right - the ratchet should stay honest about what it measures - but it means the guard can no longer see those two, so they are asserted BY NAME in the story's test, in an assertion that FAILS when one is served. **When tightening a baseline would make a guard vouch for coverage it does not have, put the remainder somewhere that breaks on success**, not in a comment.

ONE MODULE BEATS A PARITY TEST WHEN BOTH HOSTS CAN IMPORT IT. `shared/seo-checks.ts` is imported by the Deno edge function (`../../../shared/seo-checks.ts` - several already do this) and by the Node service (`@shared/seo-checks`), so there is nothing to keep in sync. The print-cost calculator and the GPT-5 prompts each ship as two near-verbatim copies held together by a parity test, which works and costs a test plus a standing instruction; check whether `shared/` can just hold the logic before reaching for the copy.

A PROMISE TURNS A UI STORY INTO A FIND-AND-REPLACE (UI-BROWSER-DIALOGS-001, round 95). Twenty `window.confirm` calls and five `window.prompt` calls were filed as "an AlertDialog and a state machine per call site - a UI story, not a find-and-replace", and the filing note warned that several sit inside a mutation callback rather than a click handler, so the dialog would have to be hoisted and the surrounding logic split in two. **`client/src/components/ui/confirm-dialog.tsx` answers with a promise**, so `if (!(await confirm({...}))) return;` keeps the shape `if (!confirm(...)) return;` had and a callback is as easy as a click handler, because there is nothing to hoist. The only per-file work was making five enclosing functions `async`, which tsc named exactly. **And a PROVIDER, not a hook returning JSX**: a hook handing back `{ confirm, dialog }` needs every one of twenty-five components to find somewhere in its render tree to put `{dialog}` - twenty-five judgement calls in files the change otherwise does not touch. Mount it once, inside `TooltipProvider` so the dialog inherits the theme and portal container, and above the router so a confirm survives the navigation a destructive action often triggers.

A PROMISE THAT NEVER SETTLES IS WORSE THAN EITHER ANSWER. For a delete handler it is a button that did nothing and said nothing. Escape, the overlay and the cancel button all resolve false; **a second question answers the first with false rather than dropping it**; and outside a provider the answer is false, not a throw and not a silent true - a destructive action reached through a tree nobody wrapped must not proceed unasked, and throwing would turn a missing provider into a broken page. The text variant resolves **null** on cancel, exactly like `window.prompt`, so those call sites kept their `if (reason)` test unchanged, and an empty answer is a cancel unless the caller opts in.

WIDENING A GUARD TO COVER ITS OWN REPLACEMENT NEEDS THE ARGUMENT SHAPE, NOT THE NAME (round 95). `check:browser-dialogs` had to add `await` to its prefix set or every converted call site would report itself - and that immediately admits `await confirm('really?')`, which is still the browser dialog wearing the fix's clothes. The exemption keys on the CALL'S ARGUMENT: the hook takes an options object where the browser takes a string, so `confirm({` is ours and `confirm('` is not. Whenever a guard has to stop matching the thing that replaced what it watches, find a property of the replacement that the original cannot have - an identifier it shares is not one.

AN OPTIONAL PREFIX GROUP MOVES `m.index`, AND INVERTS THE CHECK BUILT ON IT (round 95). Asserting every call site awaits its answer, `/(\w+\s+)?(confirm|textPrompt)\s*\(\s*\{/` swallowed the `await` into the match, so the "what precedes this" slice started BEFORE the await and all 27 correct call sites reported as unawaited. Match the identifier alone and read what precedes it. Worth recognising because the failure looks like the code is wrong - 27 findings, every one real-looking - when the regex is.

BUILD A BACKSLASH, DO NOT ESCAPE IT THROUGH TWO LAYERS (round 95). A mutation anchor naming a regex (`|=>|\bawait)`) cannot survive a bash heredoc and a python string literal intact; two attempts at the escaping both produced "anchor matched 0 times", which reads as the code having moved. `BS = chr(92)` and string concatenation gets it right first time. Same family as CLAUDE.md's existing rule about writing PRD notes from a file rather than inline in a shell string.

A LINE WINDOW IS NOT A SCOPE, AND IT IS WRONG IN BOTH DIRECTIONS (UI-DEAD-BUTTONS-001, round 96). The scan that opened that story excluded a `<Button>` within 15 LINES of a `<form>` or a Dialog trigger. A submit button in a form declared thirty lines up therefore read as DEAD, and a button merely FOLLOWING a `</form>` read as ALIVE - so the same heuristic produced false positives and false negatives at once. `scripts/check-dead-buttons.mjs` walks a tag stack, so the exclusion is real containment at any depth: 107 candidates became 95. This file already records the same mistake in three other costumes (a fixed 200-character window that ran into the next branch, a `'\n  }'` slice that matched a brace at a guessed indentation, a 260-character header slice that ran past the branch it was checking) - **a window is not a scope, however it is measured.**

A BARE BOOLEAN ATTRIBUTE DOES NOT MATCH `\bname\b\s*[={]` (round 96). `asChild` is how this codebase hands a Button's behaviour to its child - `<Button asChild variant="outline"><a href={x}>Export .ics</a></Button>` - and a handler-detection pattern requiring `=` or `{` after the name misses every one of them. BlogCalendar's export link read as a dead button. Found by hand-checking five findings against the real JSX before baselining, which is the step worth keeping: a baseline holding a known false positive is where a real one hides, and the check costs five minutes.

WIRING A LINK WITHOUT WIRING ITS READER REPRODUCES THE DEFECT (round 96). TodayDashboard's "Add Task" had no handler, and TaskHub owns the create-task dialog, so the fix is `?action=new` - which is EXACTLY the shape AUDIT-014 found broken: nine quick-action links carried that parameter and **no page read it**, so all nine dropped the user on a list and did nothing. `use-action-param` exists and six pages consume it; TaskHub was not one of them. Wire both halves in the same change, or the button goes from doing nothing to navigating somewhere that does nothing.

A BUTTON THAT DUPLICATES ITS PARENT'S ACTION WHILE PROMISING MORE IS WORSE THAN NO BUTTON (round 96). "Log Activity" sat on a stale-deal card whose whole surface already navigates to that deal through `clickableProps`. There is no `/activities` route, and the activity composer lives on the deal record (CRM-008), so the button could only have gone where the card already goes - while its label promised something more specific. Deleted rather than repointed, per AUDIT-016. The nested-interactive-element problem is the same finding wearing an accessibility hat: a `<Button>` inside a clickable div has two handlers competing for one click.

THREE MUTANTS SURVIVED BECAUSE THE ASSERTION COULD NOT REACH ITS BRANCH (round 96), each a different way of failing to contain the distinguishing case. A self-closing-tag fixture used `<Separator />`, where a mutant that wrongly pushes it is indistinguishable from the fix - neither a div nor a separator excludes anything, so the button is dead either way; a self-closing `<DialogTrigger />` separates them. A "every verdict carries a reason" check was asserted over baseline DATA in which every entry was `unexamined`, so the filter was empty whatever the code did - the guard's enforcement had to be run against a temporarily-mutated baseline instead. And a "the baseline keeps its file and label fields" check read the COMMITTED file when the mutant only changes what `--update-baseline` writes. **Before trusting a passing assertion, ask which of the two implementations it would distinguish.**

A NEW BASELINE WRITER MUST ADOPT THE `existingNote()` IDIOM, NOT JUST THE BEHAVIOUR (round 96). `baseline-notes-preserved.test.ts` detects note preservation by looking for that helper name, and a new writer that preserves its note through a differently-named helper reports as a regenerator. The guard is right to be name-based here - the alternative is running 33 writers - so the convention is part of the contract. Verify it BEHAVIOURALLY anyway: write a sentinel note into the file, run `--update-baseline`, and check the sentinel survived. That immediately caught the next thing, which is that `git checkout` cannot restore an untracked file, so the sentinel was still sitting in the baseline until a test that reads the note's content failed.

THREE TABLES FOR ONE JOB IS A VOCABULARY PROBLEM BEFORE IT IS A MERGE (WF-V-06, round 97). A dealer orders toner three ways and this product keeps them in `supply_orders` (the toner pipeline's own, per MACHINE and COLOUR, raised by a prediction), `device_supply_orders` (a coordinator ordering against a monitored DEVICE, with an approval step) and `customer_supply_orders` (the CUSTOMER's basket, with an order number and a delivery address). They are genuinely different objects with different writers, so `shared/supply-order-union.ts` unions them for READING and leaves each write path alone - and `GET /device-monitoring/supply-orders?sources=all` is read-only because an Approve button over three lifecycles would work for a third of what it lists. **"pending" IS NOT THE SAME WORD IN ANY TWO OF THEM**: the defaults are `pending_approval`, `pending` and an enum's `draft`. Map them through an explicit per-source table, COUNT the coercions, and send anything no map knows to `unknown` NAMED - COP-B00's rule, one layer up. **And `draft` stays its own state**: only the portal can produce one, it is the customer's unsent basket, and flattening it into `pending_approval` puts items nobody has ordered into an operations queue.

A COMMA LIST HANDED TO `.eq()` MATCHES NOTHING, AND ON A QUEUE THAT READS AS "NOTHING OUTSTANDING" (round 97). `SupplyOrders.tsx` sends `status=pending,approved,ordered,shipped` for its Open filter and `delivered,cancelled` for Closed; the handler did `.eq('status', <that whole string>)`, a literal no row has ever held. Two of the page's four filters were therefore empty lists - not an error, not a blank state, just a queue that looked clear. Use `.in()` for a list, `.eq()` for one value, and let an absent filter skip PostgREST entirely, which rejects an `.in()` with no values. **Worth grepping for wherever a client builds a filter by joining with commas**: the server side of that contract is a different function from the one that wrote the URL.

A PATH SEGMENT IS SHADOWED BY AN ID; A QUERY PARAMETER IS NOT (round 97). The union could have been `/supply-orders/all`, and `parts[1]` on that route is an order id - so an order whose id is the string "all" would take the route, which is SUPA-024's `/library` defect with a word instead of a slug. `?sources=all` has no such collision, and being opt-in leaves the existing response shape untouched for the caller that already depends on it.

AN ABSENCE ASSERTION ABOUT A WRITE MUST NOT MATCH A READ (round 97). `fleet-monitoring-port.test.ts` asserted `device-monitoring/index.ts` never contains the STRING `from('supply_orders')`, because AUDIT-031 found its Order Toner button inserting into that table with columns it does not have. Adding a correct READ of the same table reported as the defect. The property was always about INSERTING, so it walks each occurrence and checks that chain for `.insert(`/`.update(`/`.upsert(`/`.delete(`. **Same family as the comment trap**: an assertion that matches a NAME rather than a CONSTRUCT will eventually report the right thing as wrong.

A MUTANT CAN BE WEAK WITHOUT THE TEST BEING WEAK (round 97). One survivor removed a header's title LINE while leaving the block beneath it, so the assertions about that block still held - the mutation was not the change it claimed to be. Removing the whole block is caught. Before strengthening a test against a surviving mutant, check that the mutant actually removed the thing the test is about; this file already records the inverse (a mutation landing on a different table's identical line) and both come from the same habit of not reading where the edit went.

A SWITCH WITH A `default` HIDES A MISSING STEP, AND ESLINT CANNOT SEE IT (WF-L-11). `EnhancedOnboardingForm` has TEN steps in its progress bar and its `renderStepContent` switch handled FOUR - 1, 2, 5 and 10. Steps 3, 4, 6, 7, 8 and 9 fell to `default: return null`, so half the wizard was a progress dot over an empty card and the nineteen `networkConfig` fields the page's own zod schema declares had never been reachable by anybody. The consequence is one layer down and invisible from the page: WF-L-10 writes `onboarding_network_config` from that payload and gates `is_configured` on an address, a VLAN, a switch port, a gateway, DNS servers or a naming convention being present - every one behind the blank step - so **`networkIsConfigured` returned false for every checklist ever raised through this wizard**, WF-L-13's installed->active gate was meaningless, and the checklist PDF's network section printed empty for exactly the reason WF-L-10 thought it had fixed. CRMX-016's "a whole feature can ship behind three missing Route lines" with a case label instead of a Route, and no guard here watches it: a switch with a default is legal code, the identifiers are all used, and the step renders without throwing. **Count the branches of a step/tab switch against the list that draws the tabs** - that comparison is the whole check, and it is the same catalogue-versus-slots shape COP-B01 AC6 records for My Day's card ids.

COVERAGE IS A RESOLUTION PROBLEM WHEREVER A LOOKUP COLUMN IS FREE TEXT AND NULLABLE (WF-L-11). `oid_mappings.model_series` decides which SNMP bundle printer monitoring polls a machine with, and it is a nullable varchar: null means every model of that manufacturer, and the SEEDED row `'VersaLink/AltaLink'` means either of two, so a slash is a list separator in data that already exists. `shared/onboarding-readiness.ts` resolves it - split on slash and comma, match at TOKEN BOUNDARIES against a normalized model, prefer the longest matching series, then `is_default`, then id, so a narrower mapping added later takes effect rather than losing a coin toss. **WHAT IT REFUSES TO MATCH IS THE POINT**: `'iR-ADV'` is the trade abbreviation for `'imageRUNNER ADVANCE'` and resolves to NONE, visibly, with a link to `/oid-management`. Matching it would decide unreviewed that two spellings are one thing, and being wrong here points a machine at another model's counter and reports a meter figure that becomes an invoice - COP-B10's rule with a billing consequence. **THREE OUTCOMES, NOT TWO**: `unknown` is separate from `none`, because a device whose model nobody typed in has not been checked, and "not set up" over a blank field sends a technician to fix nothing (the literal string `'Unknown'` counts as unknown too - it is what this form's own equipment default writes). A manufacturer-wide mapping IS coverage and says which it is, since a generic bundle really is what monitoring would use. `oid_mappings` has no `tenant_id` (round 76), so the read is cross-tenant by construction and the panel says so rather than implying a per-dealer answer.

A WIZARD STEP THAT RUNS BEFORE THE RECORD EXISTS CANNOT TAKE AN ID (WF-L-11). `GET /onboarding/config-readiness` deliberately has no checklist id: the Network Setup step runs before `POST /onboarding/checklists`, so there is no row to read equipment off and the caller sends what is on screen. Two rules follow. Only the fields the checks read travel (manufacturer, model, equipment type, features, SMTP name) - serials, asset tags, site addresses and contacts stay out of a query string that ends up in access logs. And a derived requirement SAYS it is derived: nothing on this checklist asks "does this site use scan-to-email", so the answer comes from an MFP/scanner type, a feature naming scan, or an SMTP name, and the derivation is printed rather than presented as a stored fact - an address-book warning over a fleet of plain printers trains people to ignore the panel.

RENDER THE FIELDS THE WRITE PATH KEEPS, AND ONLY THOSE (WF-L-11). Twelve of the nineteen network fields are rendered; the seven left out are exactly `NETWORK_FIELDS_WITHOUT_COLUMNS` in `_shared/onboarding-config.ts`, which the write path drops. An input that silently discards what a technician types is worse than one that is absent (COP-M01's "a configured field the record does not carry"), and the test DERIVES both lists from that module rather than pinning a copy, so adding a column moves the form instead of breaking a test. The wireless passphrase is the one nobody should want back as it stood: a shared key typed into a form that renders into a signed public PDF link.

A COMPLETE BACK END WITH NO SLOT TO RENDER IN (WF-S-09). BANT qualification shipped as five endpoints over two real tables created by migration 0000, with a qualification-history trail, analytics, and `/api/lead-scoring` already in `crmProxies` under a comment naming `BANTAssessment.tsx` as its caller - and `grep -rn BANTAssessment client/src` returned only that file. **The dependsOn was not a blocker and checking it was the first real question**: WF-S-01 is the companies-vs-business_records split, `verifyLead` reads `business_records`, and LeadDetail's `/api/leads/:id` reads `business_records` too, so the split does not reach the record page. Read the story's blocker against the specific tables, not against its name.

TWO DEFECTS WOULD HAVE SHIPPED WITH THE WIRING, AND BOTH READ AS "THE FORM LOSES EVERYTHING" (WF-S-09). The write path mapped camelCase to columns field by field and always worked; **both read branches answered the raw PostgREST row** while the component hydrates with `{ ...defaultBANTData, ...bantData }`, so every stored snake*case key landed BESIDE an untouched camelCase default - an assessment stored correctly and displayed as nothing. CRM-008 round 65's shape, invisible from either side alone because the POST really did persist. The second is the same fact with a louder symptom: the success toast read `data.qualificationStatus.replace('*', ' ')`, a TypeError on a snake row, so **saving threw AFTER the write landed**. Camelise with `toCamelShallow`here -`pain_points`and`blockers` are jsonb - and leave the branches with no caller raw, each noted, because camelising a shape nothing reads is a change with no way to be verified.

A LIVE PREVIEW THAT DRIFTS FROM THE STORED NUMBER IS WORSE THAN A WRONG CONSTANT IN EITHER (WF-S-09). The BANT arithmetic existed twice - `calculateEstimatedScore` in the form, an inline copy in the handler - agreeing by luck. The preview is what a rep watches while filling the form and reads as the answer; the server's is what reaches the pipeline. `shared/bant-score.ts` is imported by both (`../../../shared/bant-score.ts` from Deno, `@shared/bant-score` from the client), which is round 94's rule again: one module beats a parity test whenever both hosts can import it. It also holds the LABEL for a stored status rather than deriving one from a score, because a row written before a threshold moved keeps the status it was saved with and relabelling it from the number would rewrite history; an unrecognised status renders as itself so a vocabulary that grows on the server stays visible.

A SECTION AND ITS SLOT ARE ONE CHANGE, AND THE CHECK IS DERIVABLE (WF-S-09). `shared/record-layout.ts` gains `lead-qualification` and LeadDetail supplies the slot in the same commit - a section the layout names with no slot renders as nothing and is reported in `unrenderable`. The test derives every leads section whose `propertyFields` is EMPTY (those are the slot-driven ones) and asserts the page slots each, so a future section added without one fails rather than quietly showing an admin less than they configured - COP-B01 AC6's catalogue-versus-slots comparison, applied to layout sections. **NOT collapsed**: the story's whole defect is a feature nobody is sent to, and a collapsed section is one a rep never discovers.

AN UNBACKED COLUMN IS NOT DELIVERED BY ADDING IT (WF-S-09). That story asks for the BANT score and grade as sortable columns on the lead list. **Both lead lists read `companies`** - `/crm/leads` through the registry's `/api/business-records` (whose own header calls it a wrapper that delegates to companies) and `/leads-management` through `/api/companies` directly - while BANT keys on `business_records.id`, so the column could never hold a value. It is left out per COP-M01, with a test asserting the registry carries no BANT column AND that business-records still delegates to companies: it FAILS the day COP-B00 lands, which turns the gap into work rather than a note that goes stale. Related and separate: **neither lead list links to `/leads/:id` at all** - the only inbound link in any client tree is `CompetitiveIntelligence.tsx:415`, which carries a `business_records` id from the COP-B10 coverage report, so the record page is reachable from exactly one place.

THE COMMENT TRAP, EIGHTH OCCURRENCE, IN AN ORDERING CHECK (WF-S-09). Asserting that a glance card renders "Could not load" BEFORE "Not assessed" in its ternary chain failed against correct code, because the comment above that branch quotes "Not assessed" in prose explaining what must not render - so `indexOf` found the explanation first. An ordering assertion is an absence assertion's cousin and wants the same treatment: strip comments, then compare positions.

A SERVICE CAN IMPORT `db` AND NEVER QUERY, WHICH IS HOW A MOCK PASSES FOR HALF-BUILT (MEETINGS-READS-001). That story said four of the router's eight handlers "genuinely call that service, which is real, db-backed". the deleted `meeting-scheduling-service` under `server/services/` was 775 lines with `import { db } from '../db'` at the top and **zero `.select(` and zero `.from(`** in the body, and the same for the `advanced-scheduling-service` it delegated to. `grep -c "db\."` on such a file returns a non-zero number - the import and the type references - so the cheap check agrees with the story and the expensive one does not. **Count `.select(`/`.from(`/`storage.`, not `db.`**, before believing any claim that a service is backed. Tenth stale claim corrected here by reading the tree.

THE MIGRATION THAT NEVER LANDED EXPLAINS THE MOCKS ABOVE IT (MEETINGS-READS-001). the `run-meeting-scheduling-migration` script under `server/` read `migrations/meeting-scheduling-migration.sql`, **a file that is not in the repo** - so the tables `meetings`, `meeting_types` and `meeting_rooms` were never created, and every handler on BOTH hosts had to be a mock because there was nothing to read. That runner had been sitting in `docs/server-orphans-baseline.json` the whole time and nobody had joined "this file is unreachable" to "therefore this feature has no tables". When a surface is mocked on every host, look for its migration before looking at its handlers: a missing DDL file is a one-line explanation for a whole fabricated feature, and the orphan baselines are where it hides.

410 AND 501 SAY DIFFERENT THINGS, AND PICKING THE WRONG ONE INVITES THE DUPLICATE BACK (MEETINGS-READS-001). `GET /meetings` and `/meetings/:id` were mocks - one invented "Weekly Team Sync" for the list, and THE SAME object for every id on the detail, so no two requests could disagree. They are **410 with a `USE_CALENDAR_EVENTS` code naming `GET /meetings/calendar/events`**, because the capability is not missing: that endpoint reads `calendar_events` with provider propagation and has a live caller. 501 would have said "nobody built this", which is how a second reader of the same concept gets built twice. `/types` and `/rooms` ARE 501, with a code naming the table that does not exist - the distinction is whether there is somewhere else to send the caller.

A FABRICATED WRITE OUTCOME IS HARDER TO CATCH THAN A FABRICATED READ (MEETINGS-READS-001). `POST /schedule/:requestId` answered **201 Created** with `meeting-${Date.now()}` for a row it never stored: a caller booked a meeting, got an identifier back, and nothing existed. Three guards here watch fabricated reads (`check:fabricated`, `check:no-static-posture`, `check:no-random-metrics`) and none watches this, because **the success is the evidence** - there is no `|| literal`, no `Math.random`, and no static JSX. Round 78 found the same shape in a bulk-delete loop that toasted a count of attempts. When auditing a surface for fabrication, read the CREATE paths and ask what row the response's id belongs to.

CHECK WHAT SURVIVES BEFORE DELETING AROUND IT (MEETINGS-READS-001). `handlers/meetings.ts` held five handlers and only four were mocks: `/analytics` counts `calendar_events` for real, which AUDIT-020 had fixed in that exact file. Deleting the file wholesale - the obvious move once four of five are mocks - would have silently undone a previous story's work. Read every exported handler in a file before retiring the file.

WIDEN A TEST THAT NAMES A FILE YOU DELETE, AND PROVE THE WIDENING WITH A PROBE (MEETINGS-READS-001). `random-metrics-retired.test.ts` had four assertions reading the deleted service by name: no fatigue score, no flexibility score, no 30%-chance participant conflict, no confident "9 AM tomorrow" free slot. The property is that an invitation is never sent to a time somebody is busy, so it now walks every file in `server/services` and `supabase/functions` with a corpus floor - and the way to confirm a widened absence check works is to DROP A PROBE FILE carrying the banned string into each root and watch it go red, not to read the regex.

TWO MORE COMMENT-TRAP COSTUMES IN ONE HOUR (MEETINGS-READS-001), ninth and tenth. An importer walk asserting nothing names the deleted service reported `routes-registry.ts`, because the unmount comment NAMES the service while explaining that it is gone - strip comments in the walk. And it then reported the TEST that asserts the file's absence, because a proof necessarily names the thing it disproves - `server/tests/` is excluded by RULE, the same shape one directory up. Separately, `check:prd-refs` reports a CLAUDE.md correction that names a deleted path, which is legitimate history: that is what `--update-baseline` is for, and the guard's own message says so.

A BRACKET IN A COMMENT HID TWENTY-FIVE ROUTERS FROM A GUARD, AND IT READ AS PROGRESS (AUDIT-035). `scripts/lib/route-parity.mjs` captures the root-mount array in `routes-registry.ts` with `\[([\s\S]*?)\]` - **non-greedy to the first `]`** - so an unmount note containing `/api/teams/:id[/members|/capacity|/insights]` closed the capture at its own comment and none of the six real module paths after it were read. `check:uncalled-express` went from 72 baselined to 47 and reported 27 domains as "now have a caller or are gone": approvals, executions, triggers, workflows, the whole email-marketing family. **A RATCHET IMPROVING A LOT IS A PARSE FAILURE UNTIL PROVEN OTHERWISE** - the instinct this file already records for the typecheck ratchet, and it transfers to every guard. The bisect that settles it is one command: `git stash push -q <the file you edited>`, re-run, `git stash pop`. Fixed by stripping comments before the array match (the comment rule pointed at a PARSER rather than an assertion) plus a floor that THROWS when a registry mounting at bare `/api` resolves no module, because without it the next parse break looks like progress again.

A REAL ISLAND INSIDE AN UNREACHABLE FILE IS NOT UNWIRED WORK THAT WORKS (AUDIT-035). the deleted `team-collaboration-service` under `server/services/` had two genuinely real methods - AUDIT-021 rewrote `analyzeTeamCapacity` and `getTeamMembers` over the `tasks` table - and thirteen mocks around them, with `getTeamProjects` returning a hardcoded "Q4 Sales Campaign" at 75% complete. PROD-008c's rule ("would it do anything if it were wired?") says keep; the answer here is still DELETE, because `supabase/functions/teams/handlers/teams.ts` already computes the same capacity from the same table and is what production runs. The test is not just "does some of this work" but "does anything it does have no live counterpart" - and what the richer version could do and the live one cannot (per-member analytics, named bottlenecks, recommendations) goes in the story note so the idea is not retired silently.

A MIGRATION RUNNER POINTING AT SQL THAT IS NOT IN THE REPO IS A ONE-LINE EXPLANATION FOR A FABRICATED FEATURE (MEETINGS-READS-001, AUDIT-035). Four `server/run-*-migration.ts` files read a `migrations/*.sql` that does not exist - meeting-scheduling, team-collaboration, advanced-workflows, ai-documentation - so each throws on `readFileSync` before touching the database. All four sat in `docs/server-orphans-baseline.json` and nobody had joined "this file is unreachable" to "therefore that feature has no tables". For meetings that WAS the reason every handler on both hosts was a mock; for team-collaboration the tables exist elsewhere, so the runner was merely redundant - which is why the check is worth running rather than assuming. `server/tests/unit/team-collaboration-retired.test.ts` now sweeps every surviving runner and asserts its SQL exists.

`stub: true` IS THE DIFFERENCE BETWEEN A CATALOGUE AND A FABRICATION (AUDIT-035). `supabase/functions/teams/handlers/templates.ts` returns three built-in workflow templates (equipment installation, customer onboarding, monthly maintenance) with `stub: true` and a header saying there is no `collaboration_templates` table. That is kept, while the meeting TYPES retired an hour earlier were deleted - and the distinction is not the shape of the data, it is what the response CLAIMS. A built-in product catalogue is a product decision that declares itself; three invented meeting types presented as the dealer's own configuration is a claim about their data that nothing could have made.

TWO STORIES IN A ROW DELETED A FILE A PER-FILE ASSERTION NAMED, so the repair became a shared helper (AUDIT-035). `random-metrics-retired.test.ts` had a `walk()` built inline for the meetings half in one iteration and needed the same thing for the team half in the next. The property in both cases is "this invented phrase appears nowhere", which is a tree walk with a corpus floor, not a file read. When the second copy of a walk appears, extract it - and confirm a widened absence check by DROPPING A PROBE FILE carrying the banned string into each root, rather than by reading the regex.

`check:edge-boot` CATCHES A PRODUCTION 404 THAT NOTHING ELSE SEES, AND A HANDLER IS FOUR LEVELS DEEP, NOT THREE (REPORTS-CHARTS-002). `supabase/functions/server.ts` loads each function with `await import()` inside a try/catch and OMITS the ones that throw, so a single unresolvable import makes the WHOLE function answer 404 "Function not found" in production - silently, and invisibly in dev when Express serves the prefix. The trap is the shared-module precedent: `supabase/functions/seo/index.ts` imports `../../../shared/seo-checks.ts` and is right, because it sits at depth three. A file in `<fn>/handlers/` needs **four** (`../../../../shared/...`); with three it resolves to `supabase/shared/`, which does not exist. Two functions shipped that way in one session - `lead-scoring` (committed two stories earlier, so /api/lead-scoring was 404 in production) and `reports` - and tsc, eslint, `check:edge-undef`, `check:shared-helper-imports` and the whole suite were green on both. **Run `check:edge-boot` after adding any import to an edge handler**, and assert the specifier RESOLVES rather than pinning its depth: the old test pinned `../../../shared/` and therefore failed on the corrected file.

TWO `check:*` SCRIPTS ARE REPORT GENERATORS THAT OVERWRITE COMMITTED ARTIFACTS, so a full sweep dirties the tree (REPORTS-CHARTS-002). Round 88 recorded `check:schema` rewriting `docs/DATABASE_SCHEMA.md` and `database-schema-report.json`; **`check:system` does the same to `system-check-report.json`**, whose committed copy was generated on a Windows machine and carries absolute `C:\Users\...` paths, so re-running it here replaces the whole file. Running every guard by exit code swept it into a commit before I noticed. Check `git status` after any full sweep and revert those three, and do not read either script's exit code as a verdict.

RUN THE GUARDS BY EXIT CODE, NOT BY READING A TAIL (REPORTS-CHARTS-002). `check:invalidation-keys` had been RED since the WF-S-09 commit because I piped it to `tail -3` and read "Remove them. An exemption that outlives its code stops being evidence." as the end of a passing run. It is the stale-exemption message. The loop that finds this in one command is `for g in $(node -e "...scripts...grep ^check:"); do npm run --silent $g >/dev/null 2>&1 || echo "RED $g"; done` - six guards are legitimately red in this container (`check:drift`, `check:schema`, `check:schema:ssh`, `check:schema:docker`, `check:security`, `check:stage-resolution`) and anything else is yours. The defect itself is worth knowing too: that guard keys an exemption on the bare IDENTIFIER it extracts with `/queryKey:\s*(\w+)/`, so an entry written as `bantQueryKey(leadId)` matches nothing and shows up as a stale exemption AND an unexplained finding at the same time.

A BAN THAT NAMES ONE SPELLING BANS ONE SPELLING (REPORTS-CHARTS-002). DATE-SETMONTH-001 closed `.setMonth(x.getMonth() ± n)` at zero and `check:month-arithmetic` has been green ever since. It matched `setMonth` only, so **`setUTCMonth` - the identical overflow, one method name over - sat uncaught in `supabase/functions/reports/_date.ts`**, where `rangeForPeriod('month')` on 31 March asked for "31 February" and got 3 MARCH: a 28-day window entirely inside the current month with February excluded, and every report handler taking its range from that one function was wrong together on the last three days of any long month. `period=year` had the leap-day version through `setUTCFullYear`. The guard now matches `set(UTC)?(Month|FullYear)` against `get(UTC)?(Month|FullYear)`; adding the YEAR form surfaced 15 `setFullYear` sites, baselined 0 -> 15 with a note saying the count grew because the guard improved. Use `subtractUtcMonths`/`subtractUtcYears` from `_shared/date-months.ts` for a UTC window - the existing `subtractMonths` builds its cursor with `new Date(y, m, 1)`, which is LOCAL midnight and shifts a UTC bound by the deployment's offset.

A TEST THAT PROVES A GUARD CATCHES AN IDIOM CONTAINS THE IDIOM (REPORTS-CHARTS-002). Asserting that the widened rule matches `start.setUTCMonth(start.getUTCMonth() - 1)` put three instances of the banned text into the test file, and the baseline jumped 15 -> 18. `check:month-arithmetic` exempts such files BY NAME rather than by a `tests/` glob, deliberately, "because a real offence in a test helper should still be reported and a blanket exemption is how one hides" - so the new file joins the named list. Same family as the comment trap, at file granularity.

WHEN A PANEL SERVES A CATEGORY LIST, THE REFUSAL NEEDS AS MUCH CARE AS THE CHART (REPORTS-CHARTS-002). `/reports` offers eight categories and only three - sales, service, finance - have a table behind them (`deals`, `service_tickets`, `invoices`). Charting the other five would be AUDIT-020's fabrication redrawn from the server, so `GET /api/reporting/charts` answers `charted: false` with a reason naming THAT category: "nothing in this product records HR activity" is a different fact from "the executive category is a roll-up of the others, so a chart here would restate the sales, service and finance series". One generic line under every category is a line nobody reads. A test asserts the page's declared categories and the charted-plus-uncharted sets are the same, so a ninth category fails rather than falling through to a blank.

AND THE TARGET LINE WAS A SCHEMA QUESTION, NOT A DESIGN ONE. The mock drew a hardcoded 40000. `sales_goals` is the only goal table and its `goal_type` is an ACTIVITY enum (calls, emails, meetings, proposals, demos) with `target_count` - a count, not a currency figure - so there is no per-tenant revenue target that could be drawn. `target: null`, with the reason as the first entry of the response's `unbacked` array, which the panel renders.

A BOTH-DIVERGENT DOMAIN CAN BE BROKEN ON BOTH HOSTS FOR TWO DIFFERENT REASONS, AND THAT IS WHAT KEEPS IT HIDDEN (PROD-008, round 109). `/api/leads/:id/activities` is the iOS quick-log FAB's write path - `ActivityQuickLogService` posts there, `OfflineWriteQueue` retries it, `APIEndpoint.swift` reads the list. PRODUCTION 404'd, because `supabase/functions/leads/` had no `activities` branch and the request fell past every one to the trailing 404. DEV answered **23502**, because the Express handler spread `...req.body` into Drizzle, iOS sends `type`, the column is `activity_type`, and Drizzle DROPS a key that is not a column - so a NOT NULL column with no default arrived null (reproduced on Postgres 16, not inferred). Neither environment contradicts the other when both are broken, which is the opposite of the usual dev/prod split and much quieter. The read side had drifted the same way: iOS decodes `leadId`, `type` and `activityDate` against columns named `business_record_id`, `activity_type` and `completed_date`, and every one of those Swift properties is optional, so the timeline decoded cleanly and rendered a generic icon with no date. `shared/lead-activity-write.ts` is imported by both hosts and holds the whole vocabulary; **the location fields the quick-log sends have no columns and are NAMED in `ignoredFields` rather than dropped** (COP-B06), because a rep who believes a call was geotagged and finds nothing is the failure that matters. NO `crmProxies` ENTRY: `import-eda` is still Express-only, so proxying the prefix would take it from working-in-dev to 404-in-dev.

ONE PRESENTER, TWO HOSTS, ONE `instanceof Date` BRANCH. `String(v)` is right for PostgREST, which returns text, and wrong for Express, which returns drizzle rows whose timestamps are Date objects - `String(date)` gives "Mon Sep 21 2026 15:04:00 GMT+0000" and the iOS decoder reads that as no date at all. Caught by running the presenter against a real row rather than by reading it. Wherever one module serves both hosts, ask what each host's driver hands it.

`check:edge-path-coverage` WALKED `client/src` AND NOTHING ELSE (PROD-008, round 109). It shares `computeParity` with `check:routes`, which walks that one tree, so a path only a native client calls could never put a domain on its list - and `leads` therefore read as carrying one unrelated gap while its most-used mobile write path 404'd. **`check:unreferenced-edge-fns` had already learned this exact lesson**, and CLAUDE.md records it ("reading `client/src` alone reported five false positives... `today-dashboard`, `APIEndpoint.swift:305`"), but the lesson had not travelled to the sibling guard. Widened to the same seven trees, with two rules that both cost a wrong first answer: native TEST fixtures are excluded (`ios/PrintyxTests` posts to `/api/leads/123/activities`, and counting it puts `123` in the baseline as a missing endpoint - a baseline holding a known non-defect is where a real one hides), and Swift `\(id)` plus Kotlin `$id` are placeholders, without which the literal behind an id is never checked and the whole path is dropped even once the file is in the corpus. **33 -> 43, and all ten new entries are native-only**, proven by diffing a run with the native corpus against one without rather than by reading the diff of the baseline. When a guard shares its corpus with another guard, the corpus is part of BOTH contracts - and widening the shared `computeParity` would have changed `check:routes`, whose `deadInBoth` class is gated at zero, so the widening lives in the one guard that needed it.

A TEST THAT ASSERTS A DEBT LEVEL FAILS IN BOTH DIRECTIONS (round 109). `edge-path-depth.test.ts` first demanded at least 19 deep `/:id/` shapes in the baseline, which made the story that closed them look like a regression; it was relaxed to exactly zero, which then made a corpus widening that found three REAL ones look like a regression too. Both spellings were claims about how much debt exists rather than about a property. It is a NAMED shrink-only list now, plus the property the guard actually owns - a deep entry is a whole shape carrying a placeholder and a literal leaf. Third instance of this file's own rule: name the exceptions, do not count them.

A LIST THAT CHECKS ITSELF IS NOT A TEST (round 109). "names the location fields it cannot store" looped over `ACTIVITY_FIELDS_WITHOUT_COLUMNS` and asserted each entry was reported as ignored - so deleting `latitude` from the constant shortened the loop and the test stayed green while the response stopped naming a field it had silently dropped. It derives the sent-field list from `ios/.../QuickLogModels.swift` now. Same family as counting `applyUserScope` calls and pinning membership, and the sixth time in this repo's history that an assertion was about the thing it was supposed to be checking. Two guard mutants also survived first time - dropping the native files from the caller loop, and dropping the test exclusion - because the gap this story FIXED is closed on either reading, which is round 92's "a fixture can only distinguish implementations it contains an example of" arriving through the fix rather than the fixture. Both are killed by derived assertions: at least one reported gap has no caller in `client/src`, and no gap is a bare id.

AN ENDPOINT THAT LOOKS ALREADY BUILT ELSEWHERE MAY HAVE SHIPPED ITS OTHER HALF (PROD-008, round 110). `POST /api/service-tickets/:id/attachments` is the iOS ticket photo picker - "attach photos of error codes, meters, or damage" - and nothing served it: the service-tickets edge function had no `attachments` branch, so every upload fell through to its trailing 405, and Express has no handler for that path either. **`POST /mobile/photos` reads like the existing implementation and is not.** Its own header says "actual file handled separately", it REQUIRES an `object_path`, and nothing in the tree ever uploads bytes to produce one - so the metadata half shipped and the file half never did, and the domain read as covered. Before porting an endpoint that appears to exist under another prefix, check which half of it was built. One thing this defect did NOT do is lie: the picker uses `requestVoid`, distinguishes `.offlineQueued` from a real failure, and showed each photo as failed - worth saying, because the recent findings here have mostly been the opposite.

A PHOTO TAKEN ON A CUSTOMER'S SITE IS THE `qbr-artifacts` CASE (PROD-008, round 110). `service-photos` is a new PRIVATE bucket. Those pictures carry the customer's equipment, their serials and often the inside of their premises, and `service_photos` has latitude and longitude columns beside them - so this is not the `branding-assets`/`blog-assets` case, where a logo and a post image are meant to be fetchable by anyone. `public: false` is stated explicitly at creation rather than left to a default, and every read hands back `createSignedUrl` (15 minutes) and never `getPublicUrl`, which is the distinction `docs/storage-bucket-inventory.md` exists to make: `getPublicUrl` returns a working-looking string whether or not the bucket is public, and that is how the QBR decks became world-readable. The bucket is recorded in that inventory AND in `scripts/audit-storage-buckets.ts`'s expected map, because a bucket missing from that map is one the audit calls unexpected rather than one whose visibility it checks. It is deliberately NOT in the pin migration yet - the bucket is created on first upload, so a deployment that has never had a photo has no row to pin, and the doc says so rather than leaving it to be discovered.

THE SNIFFER RECOGNISES MORE THAN A PHOTO ENDPOINT SHOULD ACCEPT (PROD-008, round 110). `sniffUpload` identifies PDF alongside the image formats, so a handler that took everything it can name would accept documents on a photo endpoint. `supabase/functions/_shared/ticket-attachment.ts` keeps an allow-list of five image types, and the gap between what the sniffer knows and what the endpoint allows is deliberate and tested. Calling `sniffUpload` with NO text fallback is what makes SVG and anything unrecognisable a rejection rather than a guess. **SIZE IS CHECKED ON THE DECODED BYTES**: base64 inflates by 4/3, so a limit applied to the request string is the wrong number by a quarter - and the test that proves it needs a file UNDER the cap whose ENCODED form is over it, because an over-limit fixture is over on either reading and cannot separate the two implementations.

AN UPLOAD THAT LANDS BEFORE ITS ROW MUST CLEAN UP AFTER ITSELF. The object goes into the bucket first, so an insert that then fails would leave bytes nothing can reach and no row records; the handler removes the object and answers 500 rather than 201. Two orderings travel with it: the scope check runs BEFORE the upload, because a refusal that runs after the write is not a refusal, and not-found is answered in the branch rather than inside `denyIfTicketOutOfScope`, which returns null for a missing ticket ON PURPOSE so that no branch leaks which ids are real.

A SURVIVING MUTANT THAT IS REDUNDANT CODE GETS LABELLED, NOT DELETED (round 110). Removing `.split(/[\\/]/).pop()` from the attachment filename sanitiser changed nothing, because the character-class replace two lines below already rewrites a path separator to a dash - so the basename step is belt and braces and the character class is what holds the tenant prefix. Kept, because taking the basename is what a reader expects to see there, with a comment saying which line is the control; the test asserts the PROPERTY (a sanitised name carries no separator) so the guarantee survives whichever line moves. Same round, the mutation for it had to be applied twice: the first attempt silently matched nothing because prettier had reformatted the file. Check where a mutation landed before believing a mutant survived - third time this file has had to record that.

`createSignedUrls` IS PLURAL, AND USING IT IS CHEAPER THAN TRIAGING THE LOOP. A ticket can carry twenty photos, and signing them one at a time is an N+1 that would have arrived on `docs/nplus1-triage.json` as a new candidate. One batched call takes the whole list. The same round, that line-keyed file drifted TWICE (482 -> 647 -> 661) because a branch was inserted above an existing loop - sixth and seventh occurrences this session, and the fix is always to move the key.

A MISSING ENDPOINT IS SOMETIMES THE ONLY THING HOLDING A GUARDRAIL UP (PROD-008, round 111). `POST /api/proposals/:id/send` is the iOS quote list's Send swipe action, offered on a DRAFT, and nothing served it - the proposals function has branches for `status`, `track-view`, `email`, `generate-from-template`, `share`, `line-items` and `comments`, so the request fell past all of them to the trailing 404. **QUOTE-006/016 - the margin floor and the effective-discount ceiling - lived INLINE in the `PATCH /:id/status` branch and nowhere else.** So writing `send` as "email it, then mark it sent" with its own copy of the policy would not have produced a duplicate; it would have produced the bypass: a rep swiping Send on a phone with an over-policy discount. The gate is extracted to `pricingGateRefusal` and both callers are the same code, with a test asserting exactly one implementation (`PRICING_APPROVAL_REQUIRED` appears twice, both inside that helper) and exactly two call sites. **Before adding a second way to perform an action, find where the policy for the first one lives; if it is inline, extracting it IS the change.**

ORDER IS THE OTHER HALF OF A COMPOSITE ENDPOINT. The gate runs before anything is emailed, because a refusal after the customer has the PDF is not a refusal. The status moves only after the email SUCCEEDS, because a quote marked sent that nobody received is worse than an error the rep can act on. And a status update that itself fails answers 200 with `statusUpdated: false` and a warning rather than a 500 - the customer already has the quote at that point, and a 500 invites the rep to send it twice. One more rule worth copying wherever a "do it again" action exists: an ACCEPTED or REJECTED quote is re-sent without moving its status, because re-sending is legitimate (a customer asking for the PDF again) and resetting the row to `sent` would erase their answer.

A POSITIONAL ASSERTION WITH TWO ANCHORS PERMITS A REARRANGEMENT THAT KEEPS THEIR ORDER AND CHANGES THE MEANING (round 111). Asserting `update > email` for "the status moves only after the email succeeds" survived a mutant that moved the email LATER while still leaving it above the update. The property needs THREE positions - the email call, then the `if (!outcome.ok) return` bail, then the update - and stating it that way kills the mutant that hoists the whole status block above the email. Same family as the fixed-window and adjacency traps this file already records, one level up: the window was right and the anchors were too few.

A TEST THAT PINS ONE SPELLING BLOCKS ITS OWN IMPROVEMENT (round 111, fourth occurrence). `pricing-approval-unblock.test.ts` asserted the exact string `if (cur && !hasPricingApproval(cur))`, so extracting that gate into a helper - where the same check becomes an early `if (!cur || hasPricingApproval(cur)) return null` - failed a change that is strictly better.
