Rebound to the property (a stamped row leaves the gate without a refusal, whichever way round the condition is written) and widened in the same edit to assert the one-implementation-two-callers claim the extraction makes possible. After the adjacency match, the indentation-scanned object keys and the inline bulk-delete shape, the pattern is clear enough to state as a rule: when a test fails on a refactor that makes the code better, the repair is to assert the property, never to restore the shape.

A 200 UNDER THE WRONG KEY NAMES IS A BLANK SCREEN THAT LOGS NOTHING (PROD-008, round 112). `/api/team-reports` has no caller in `client/src` - the only consumer of the whole edge function is the iOS manager reports screen - and all four of its cards were empty, two ways. `activities` and `no-touch` 404'd. `pipeline` and `leaderboard` answered **200 with key names ManagerReportsModels.swift does not read**: `{totalDeals, totalValue, byStage}` against `pipelineValue`/`weightedValue`/`openOpportunityCount`/`closedWonThisMonth`/`closedWonCount`, and `{count, value}` against `closedWonAmount`/`closedWonCount`/`rank`. Every Swift property is optional, so decoding SUCCEEDED. PA-040's shape, pointed at a native client: **when a native screen is blank, compare the key names before assuming a 404.**

`check:edge-path-coverage` IS BLIND TO A MISSING SUB-PATH WHOSE WORD APPEARS ELSEWHERE IN THE FILE (round 112). `GET /team-reports/activities` had no branch - the handler spells it `activity`, singular - and the guard passed, because `activities` appears in that source as `metric === 'activities'` and inside table names. Its `appearsIn` rule is generous on purpose (its own header says a false positive de-gates a domain that is actually broken, which is the worse trade), so this is a cost of a correct design rather than a bug in it. Worth knowing when reading that baseline: absence from the list is not proof a sub-path is served, only that its word is somewhere in the function.

A COLUMN THAT LOOKS BUILT FOR THE JOB AND IS WRITTEN BY NOTHING (round 112). `deals.last_activity_date` is the obvious source for a no-touch alert. It is read in four places and SET IN NONE, so an alert built on it flags every open deal in the tenant forever - a list that cries wolf about everything, which is the same class of defect as a dashboard of zeroes and just as invisible from the code. The touch date comes from the activity rows instead: `deal_activities` for the card AND `business_record_activities` for the account behind it, because a rep who logs a call against the customer has touched the opportunity whether or not they logged it twice. A deal with neither is measured from the day it was raised, and the row SAYS which (`measuredFrom`), because "nobody has touched it since it was raised" and "nobody has touched it since the last call" are different conversations.

EXTEND THE PURE MODULE THAT ALREADY EXISTS (round 112). `shared/team-rollup.ts` had the unassigned-bucket, member-at-zero, total-is-a-floor and order-by-size rules plus `rollUpActivity` and `memberName`, so the four manager-report shapes went in beside them rather than into a parallel file. Three rules the new functions add and all three generalise: a missing `probability` weights at ZERO rather than at certainty, or the forecast reads higher than the pipeline it comes from; RANK IS DENSE AND SHARED ON A TIE, because presenting a tie as an ordering invents a difference the data does not carry; and a status nobody recognises stays IN the pipeline rather than vanishing from it. `deals.status` is the discriminator and that was checked rather than assumed - `supabase/functions/pipeline-config` writes status, probability and actual_close_date together when a card enters a closed-won stage, so the column is maintained by the same write that moves the card.

A WINDOW IS NOT A SCOPE, FOURTH COSTUME (round 112). A test asserting the pipeline branch reads `deals` sliced a FIXED 900 characters from the branch header. That ran past the branch into `no-touch`, which also reads `deals`, so a mutant pointing pipeline back at `business_records` survived. Bound to the next `if (req.method === 'GET' && reportType` instead. After the 200-character header slice, the brace at a guessed indentation and the 260-character gate window, the rule is now unambiguous: bound a per-branch assertion to the NEXT BRANCH, never to a character count. The same round produced a WEAK MUTANT worth distinguishing from a weak test: rewriting `(row.ownerId && nameById.get(row.ownerId)) || 'Unassigned'` as `nameById.get(row.ownerId ?? '') ?? 'Unassigned'` is behaviourally identical for every input the function can see, so the mutation proved nothing until it was rewritten to drop the unowned row outright.

A DEV/PROD SPLIT CAN RUN THE WRONG WAY, AND NOBODY FILES IT (PROD-008, round 113). The React Native field-service screen calls `/api/mobile/time-tracking/start`, `/stop` and `/api/mobile/service-tickets/:id/status`. `supabase/functions/mobile/` served `sessions`, `photos` and `sync` and nothing else, so all three fell to its trailing 404 - while Express served them and `/api/mobile` is not proxied, so every developer machine was fine. Production is where the technician is standing. `server/routes-registry.ts` already carried a note saying a `crmProxies` entry for the prefix would not be safe until these were ported; that note was right, and this was the port it was waiting for.

**ALL THREE EXPRESS HANDLERS WERE ALSO BROKEN**, so the port is a fix and not a move. `start` wrote `status: 'in-progress'` WITH A HYPHEN, which WF-V-05's CHECK constraint refuses - a 23514 in dev too, since migration 0078 - and set `assigned_technician_id` to the caller with no scope check, so any tenant member could take any ticket by pressing Start. `stop` bumped `updated_at` and answered `{ success: true, stoppedAt }`: no session closed, no duration recorded, the technician told the timer had stopped and nothing stored the time. That is round 78's FABRICATED WRITE OUTCOME on a number somebody is paid on, and none of the three fabrication guards watches it. `status` passed the body through unvalidated.

STOPPING THE CLOCK IS NOT FINISHING THE JOB. Start and stop are the session machinery under another name - `mobile_service_sessions` already carries both timestamps and the hour columns, `time_tracking_entries` is the per-event log - so they reuse it rather than opening a second timer model (round 111's rule). The one semantic that had to differ is that the session CHECK-OUT path completes the ticket and is right to, being the "I am done here" action, while the field screen has a separate status control, so `stop` closes the session and leaves the ticket alone. Three rules travel with it: start is IDEMPOTENT against an open session, because a technician who backgrounds the app must not restart the clock and two sessions on one ticket double the hours; a clock that ran backwards records NULL rather than negative hours; and "no running timer" is a 200 saying so, not a 500 that makes them press it again.

A SCOPED PROXY ENTRY IS THE INSTRUMENT FOR A PART-MIGRATED PREFIX. `'/api/mobile/time-tracking'` and `'/api/mobile/service-tickets'` are `{fn, pathPrefix}` entries; the bare `'/api/mobile'` deliberately is not, because `/dashboard` and `/jobs/:jobId` are still Express-only and a whole-prefix entry would take them from working-in-dev to 404-in-dev. The three Express handlers are deleted rather than left shadowed, so both hosts run one implementation.

ASK WHETHER A SEGMENT IS _COMPARED TO_, NOT WHETHER IT APPEARS (round 113). `check:edge-path-coverage` matches a segment anywhere in the function source, which is lenient on purpose. Running the stricter question over all 82 native call shapes - is this segment ever on the right-hand side of an equality, a case, or a path literal - found **24 paths with no routing comparison**. It ships as a test in `server/tests/unit/mobile-field-service.test.ts` rather than as a guard, because the strict rule has its own false positives (`proposals/:id/pdf` is matched by a regex, not an equality), which is precisely why the shipped guard is lenient. Its worklist is named in the test: `mobile/dashboard` (AUDIT-033, a fixture on both hosts), `mobile/device-tokens` and `mobile/push-token` (there is NO token table and NO push sender anywhere in the tree - both native apps register into the void and both swallow the failure deliberately, so a 501 naming the gap is all that is honestly available), `activities/recent`, `analytics/performance-metrics`, `equipment/:id/service-history`, and five domains with no edge directory and no `server.ts` alias: `client-metrics`, `extension`, `health`, `proposal-templates`, `service-dispatch`. **`extension` is worth knowing about**: the Chrome extension calls `/api/extension/...`, the directory is `chrome-extension`, and that function serves exactly the three paths it asks for - an EDGE-005a alias gap - except the extension builds its base URL from stored configuration rather than through `getApiUrl`, so which host it reaches is a deployment question this container cannot settle.

`toMatch(/column_name:/)` IS SATISFIED BY `column_name: null` (round 113). Two mutants survived on that: `total_hours: null` kept the key in the file, and a single check for the `totalHours >= 0 ?` guard passed while `working_hours` lost its copy because `total_hours` still had one. Bind a write assertion to the VALUE, and walk BOTH sites - the same "a total is not a property" rule this file records for `applyUserScope` calls, `fetchAllRows` calls and `isMissingTableError` checks, now pointed at a column literal. The third repaired mutant was my own branch-slicing helper searching forward for the next `if (req.method`: the stop branch ran into the status branch, which reads `service_tickets`, so a correct file failed. Fifth window-is-not-a-scope this session, second in two rounds - bound to the next known MARKER, never to a guess about formatting.

ONE ENDPOINT, THREE CONSUMERS, THREE SHAPES, AND A FIXTURE MATCHING NONE (PROD-008, round 114). `/api/mobile/dashboard` is read by the React Native dashboard screen as a stats object (`openLeads`, `activeTickets`, `revenueMtd`, `totalEquipment`, `revenueTrend`, `leadsTrend`), by the React Native field-service screen as a TICKET LIST (`assignments?.tickets || assignments?.assignments`), and by `client/src/pages/MobileServiceApp.tsx` as `jobsQueue`. Express answered a fixture - "TECH-001", a 4.8 rating, 1247 completed jobs, $2,340.50 of revenue today - so two screens showed blanks and the third showed fiction, and the edge function had no branch at all. **When one path has several consumers, enumerate what each one READS before porting it**; picking either shape alone leaves the other broken in a way that logs nothing.

A PAGE THAT DEREFERENCES A FIXTURE'S SHAPE CANNOT BE HANDED REAL DATA (round 114). `/api/mobile/dashboard` is deliberately NOT in `crmProxies`, because `MobileServiceApp.tsx` reads `mobileData.technician.name`, `mobileData.todaysSummary.totalRevenue.toLocaleString`, `mobileData.partsInventory.vanStock.tonerCartridges` and `mobileData.performanceMetrics.thisWeek.customerSatisfaction` WITH NO GUARD - so wiring it would turn a dev-only fixture into an immediate crash. Every one of those is underivable (no technician rating column, no per-van stock table, nothing records first-time-fix rate, and CSAT is CSAT-PRODUCER-001's open gap), so this is a decision about what the page should claim rather than a data-contract fix. The page carries a header saying so, AND a test asserting those unguarded reads are still there - so if somebody guards them the note gets revisited instead of quietly outliving its reason. **A warning about a file wants a check that the condition it warns about still holds.**

MONEY BILLED IS NOT MONEY COLLECTED (round 114). `revenueMtd` sums `invoices.amount_paid` on rows SETTLED this month, not `total_amount` on rows raised. A home screen that conflates them tells a dealer they are doing better than they are, and the difference is every unpaid invoice. Two rules travel with it, both already in this file in other costumes: a trend over an empty previous window is NULL rather than zero (going from nothing to something is not "up 100%"), and ONE FETCH spans both months and is split in the pure function, so the two halves cannot end up measured against different boundaries - which is precisely how the customer portal came to multiply the current period by 0.9 and call it the previous one.

A MARKER USED TO BOUND A COMMENT-STRIPPED SOURCE HAS TO BE CODE (round 114). A branch slice was bounded by `fn.indexOf('TIME TRACKING + TICKET STATUS')` - a comment banner - and `fn` is the comment-STRIPPED source, so `indexOf` returned -1, `slice(at, -1)` ran to the end of the file, and a tenant-scoping assertion was quietly counting three other branches. Sixth window-is-not-a-scope this session and the first where the bound was invisible rather than merely too wide. Bound on a declaration or a call, never on a banner.

AN INDEX NAME IS NOT A COLUMN NAME, AND FIVE WRONG ONES SAT IN THREE LISTS AT ONCE (PA-047, round 130). `supabase/functions/platform-crm/` named `phone`, `estimated_revenue`, `address`, `assigned_rep` and `last_activity_date` on `platform_business_records`; the real columns are `primary_contact_phone`, `annual_revenue`, `address_line1`, `assigned_sales_rep` and `last_contact_date`, confirmed against migration 0000 and the drizzle declaration, which agree. `assigned_rep` is the one to recognise: the INDEX is named `platform_business_records_assigned_rep_idx` and sits ON `assignedSalesRep`, so an index name is where the wrong column name came from. **THE SAME WRONG NAME FAILED THREE DIFFERENT WAYS IN ONE FILE.** `EXPORT_COLUMNS.join(',')` goes straight into `.select()`, and an unknown column fails the WHOLE statement, so the CSV export button has been a 42703 - "Failed to build export" - on every request since it shipped. The write whitelist meant a PATCH naming any of them wrote nothing. And `camelRow` camelises the RAW row, so `PlatformBusinessRecordDetail`'s `record.assignedRep`, `record.lastActivityDate`, `record.estimatedRevenue` and `record.phone` were undefined for every record: "Unassigned" printed over an assigned account and "Never" over one contacted yesterday, which is PA-040's key-name shape with the endpoint innocent. `check:phantom-cols` sees none of it (named Sets are its documented blind spot), and **`platform-export-csv.test.ts` asserted the CONSTRUCT** - "names its columns instead of selecting \*" - **which is a different property from "the names resolve"**, so a test written about that exact `.select()` stayed green throughout. When a test pins how a query is BUILT, ask separately whether what it builds can run; `server/tests/unit/platform-record-assignment.test.ts` derives the real column set from the migration and checks all three lists against it.

PICK THE COLUMN A WRITER MAINTAINS, NOT THE ONE WHOSE NAME FITS (round 130). `last_activity_date` had two plausible replacements and the closer-sounding one is dead: NOTHING in the tree writes `last_engagement_date`, while the only writer of `platform_business_records` anywhere - `platform-activities`' counter bump - stamps `last_contact_date` on every activity. That is a live defect one function over. `platform-cs` computed `daysSinceLastActivity` from `last_engagement_date` with a `: 999` fallback, so `engagementScore = max(0, 100 - 999*2)` was **0 for every tenant on 20% of the health score**, and the churn model's 60-day dormancy term never fired once - both errors in the flattering direction, on the two numbers that page exists to produce. Both sites go through one `lastActivityAt()` taking the most recent of the two, which stays correct whichever column a future writer maintains. The method is the reusable part: when a name is ambiguous between two columns, find the WRITER rather than reasoning from the label (round 93's rule, applied to a column instead of an id space).

A DISABLED BUTTON IS AN HONEST PLACEHOLDER UNTIL SOMEBODY BUILDS THE BACKEND, AND THEN IT IS A DEAD BUTTON (PA-047, round 130). That story disabled bulk assign with the title "Bulk assign is not available yet" - correct, because `POST /api/platform-crm/business-records/bulk/assign` was served by nothing on either host (no Express route, and the edge function's branches stopped at `business-records/:id`, so it fell past them). The page's `bulkAssignMutation` was left in place, and eslint reported it only as "assigned a value but never used", which is the same disguise CRMX-016's unrouted pages and `ManagedServices`' dead Edit button wore. **Building the endpoint and leaving the button disabled is the same defect from the other end**, so the round that adds the branch enables the control, and a test asserts the mutation has a caller. Two choices worth copying: the branch sits ABOVE the `/:id` branches (or `bulk` is read as a record id, SUPA-024) which makes `bulk` a reserved id, free because these are uuids; and the picker offers `GET /platform-crm/managers` rather than a text field, because `assigned_sales_rep` holds a USER ID and free text would store an id nobody can verify.

`platform_lead_assignment_history` HAD NO WRITER, AND THE REASON WAS THE FEATURE ABOVE IT (AUDIT-028, round 130). A real table carrying assigned_from, assigned_to, assigned_by, a reason and a timestamp - the whole answer to "who moved this prospect, and when" - and nothing filled it, because the only action that would was unbuilt. `shared/platform-record-assignment.ts` is the plan builder, and its rules are the ones this repo keeps re-deriving: a record ALREADY HELD by that rep is unchanged rather than rewritten, so the trail does not fill with rows saying nothing moved and the count the UI reports is what happened (round 78); `assigned_from` and an unnamed `assigned_by` are NULL rather than sentinels, because "was unassigned" and "we did not look" must not read the same; ids matching no record are REPORTED rather than absorbed into the skipped count, since a stale selection and an already-assigned record are different answers; an oversized request is refused rather than truncated (COP-I01 on a write); and whatever did NOT move stays selected, so a retry is not a re-hunt.

CHECK WHERE YOUR OWN EDIT LANDED, NOT ONLY WHERE A MUTANT DID (round 130). A one-line rename aimed at `BUSINESS_RECORD_COLUMNS` landed in `EXPORT_COLUMNS` instead - both are `'assigned_rep',` on a line of their own - and the six-line comment explaining the fix went with it, so the file read as fixed in the wrong place and the test that failed looked like a window-bounding problem rather than a misplaced edit. This file records the same rule three times for mutation testing; it applies identically to an edit. Anchor a replacement inside the block it belongs to (`s.index(marker) .. s.index(end, at)`), never on a line that appears more than once.

A FALLTHROUGH IS NOT ONE MISSING BRANCH, IT IS EVERY FUTURE MISSING BRANCH (PROD-008, round 131). PA-020 fixed `supabase/functions/customers/`, which answered every tab - invoices, equipment, service history, financials, supplies - with the CUSTOMER OBJECT at 200, and its own note says why that survived an audit: a component mapping over an object renders an empty list and reports nothing. **Eight more edge functions still had the same shape**: `activities`, `contracts`, `notifications`, `platform-activities`, `service-tickets`, `deals` and `opportunities` (plus `equipment`, closed in round 115). `deals` is the expensive one - it routes `activities`, `quotes`, `summary` and `equipment`, and anything else returned the deal row, so the ninth sub-path somebody adds to the CRM's busiest record page reads as an empty tab rather than a route nobody built. All seven answer **404 naming the segment**, which is the part that matters: without the name a caller cannot tell a wrong id from a missing branch. Non-GET methods already hit an honest terminal 405/404, so the guard is GET-scoped and the fix is one branch per file. `npm run check:subresource-fallthrough` is the hard gate; 37 functions provably covered.

FIVE CONSTRUCTS IN THIS TREE ALL MEAN "AN UNKNOWN SUB-RESOURCE IS REFUSED", AND A RULE THAT KNOWS ONE OF THEM REPORTS CORRECT CODE (round 131): `if (id && sub) { 404 }` (equipment, method-agnostic); `if (GET && id && sub) { 404 }` (this round's seven); `if (id && sub) return handleSubResource(...)` (customers - a DISPATCH whose own tail 404s, which is why the rule binds to the CONDITION rather than to the response body); `if (GET && id && !sub)` (companies, leads - guarded BY CONSTRUCTION, since the branch cannot match when a segment is present); and a bare `if (id) { 404 }` (business-records, PROD-008b). The first cut of the guard knew two of the five and would have accused four correct functions - a hard gate holding a known non-defect is where a real one hides, so each shape was added by reading the file rather than baselined.

TWO PARSING TRAPS THAT HID THE CRM CORE FROM ITS OWN GUARD (round 131). **THE SEGMENT ARRAY IS SPELLED THREE WAYS** - `parts`, `pathParts`, `segments` - so a pattern naming one spelling guards one spelling (the `setMonth`/`setUTCMonth` lesson at identifier granularity), and `deals`, `companies`, `business-records` and `leads` were all silently out of scope on the first run. **AND A NORMALISATION LINE CAN STEAL THE ID**: `deals` opens with `const pathParts = rawParts[0] === 'deals' ? rawParts.slice(1) : rawParts;`, whose right-hand side ALSO starts with `<array>[0]`, so a pattern that does not require the subscript to END the initializer matches that line first, resolves the id to "pathParts", and drops the whole function - which is exactly how `deals` concealed its own fallthrough. Require the terminator (`\[0\]\s*;`), and keep a fixture for it.

COUNT AND PRINT WHAT A GUARD CANNOT SETTLE (round 131). 50 edge functions bind `(id, sub)` and route sub-resources in a shape where no generic `/:id` branch can be located, so `check:subresource-fallthrough` neither clears nor accuses them - and says so on every run. Most read `parts[0]` as a RESOURCE NAME rather than a record id (`endpoint`, `resource`, `first`, `p0`), so there is no parent row to fall through to and reporting them would be the `check:drift` mistake (99.7% noise is a report nobody reads). A blanket second tier measured 76 before the extra guard shapes were taught to it; the number belongs in the output rather than in a comment, because a green run that silently excludes a third of the candidates reads as full coverage.

A MUTANT THAT CHANGES NOTHING IS A FIXTURE THAT CONTAINS NO EXAMPLE, SIXTH OCCURRENCE (round 131). Swapping the comment-stripper to block-comments-first survived, and the reason is the fixture rather than the test: its two lines were `// ... /reports/sales/*` and one statement, and with no `*/` anywhere the non-greedy `[\s\S]*?\*\/` matches nothing, so both orders behave identically. Adding a real block comment below the trailing-`/*` line killed it. This file already records the same shape for an unowned territory, a ticked-off meeting, a `server/`-prefixed script path and a base64 file under the decoded cap.

CHECK WHERE THE MUTATION LANDED, NOT ONLY WHAT THE RUN SAID (round 131). Three of six mutants "survived" in one batch and none of them had been applied - the assertion in the harness failed on escaping and bash printed the traceback above a green test line, which reads as a pass. Writing the mutation as a file rather than an inline shell string applied all three, and two of them died immediately. This file records the same rule for a prettier reformat and for a landing on a different table's identical line; the new costume is a harness whose own failure is quieter than its output.

A BULK DELETE THAT COUNTS THE REQUEST TELLS THE OPERATOR EVERY ROW THEY SELECTED IS GONE (PROD-008, round 132). Five endpoints across both hosts answered `deletedCount: ids.length` - the number of ids in the REQUEST. Each filters by `tenant_id`, so an id from another tenant, or one a colleague removed thirty seconds earlier, matches nothing and the response still said "Successfully deleted 20 invoices": `server/routes-bulk-operations.ts` (invoices, equipment), `server/routes-crm-bulk.ts` (deals x2, business records), `supabase/functions/invoices/` and `supabase/functions/deals/`. **The deals edge function is the cleanest illustration of how invisible this is**: its bulk-UPDATE already measured what it touched (`.select('id')` then `data?.length`) and its bulk-DELETE two dozen lines below reported the request - same file, same author, opposite answer. **`blog-paa-miner` wrote the fabricated number into the AUDIT LOG** (`deleted_count: ids.length`), and a wrong number in an audit record outlives the toast that carried it. Round 78's fabricated WRITE outcome in its bulk form, and the three fabrication guards are all blind to it because THE SUCCESS IS THE EVIDENCE - no `|| literal`, no `Math.random()`, no static JSX. `npm run check:bulk-write-counts` is the hard gate.

FIX EVERY LAYER OR THE PAGE KEEPS LYING (round 132). `Invoices.tsx` discarded the response and toasted `ids.length`, and its `executeBulk` callback returned a hardcoded `{ succeeded: batch.length, failed: 0 }`, so the progress tracker reported a clean run over rows nothing had touched - the same fiction at three levels, and correcting the server alone would have left the visible one intact. The page also carried a SECOND delete implementation, a `bulkDeleteMutation` that had never been called; eslint reported it only as "assigned a value but never used", which reads like a tidy-up rather than a duplicate destructive action (the disguise round 78 and CRMX-016 both record). One delete path now, reporting what the server measured.

`shared/bulk-result.ts` IS THE SHAPE, NOT THE TECHNIQUE. Each host obtains the affected ids its own way - `.returning({ id })` on Drizzle, `.delete().select('id')` on PostgREST - and hands them to `summariseBulkWrite`, which is what makes the two answer identically. The pattern already existed in this tree twice (`product-models` and `software-products` both take the length of what the write RETURNED), so the module generalises what was there rather than inventing anything: check for the correct version before designing one. **Ids that matched nothing are NAMED, never folded into the difference** - "17 of 20" is not actionable and "which three" is (COP-B00's rule for a migration, round 130's for a bulk assign, pointed at a delete). **The three verify-then-400 updates were converted too, although their counts were already right**, because a row can go between the existence check and the update, and one uniform shape beats a special case: an exemption rule inside a guard is a hiding place.

READ A VALUE TO THE COMMA AT DEPTH ZERO, AND BUILD THE FIXTURE THAT PROVES IT (round 132). `check:bulk-write-counts` reads the expression after `deletedCount:` up to the comma or brace that ends it. Stopping at the FIRST comma regardless of nesting reads `Math.min(total` out of `Math.min(total, ids.length)` and clears a fabricated count - and the fixture that catches that mutant has to contain the nested form, because `pick(ids.length, 0)` still carries `ids.length` after truncation and cannot separate the two implementations. Seventh time this session a mutant survived on a fixture with no distinguishing case.

INSERTING AN IMPORT WITH A REGEX CAN LAND INSIDE A MULTI-LINE IMPORT (round 132). A "first import line" match put the new specifier between `import {` and its first named binding in `blog-paa-miner`, producing a parse error that tsc never sees (the edge tree is outside the project) and that `check:edge-undef` does not report either - **`check:edge-boot` caught it**, which is what that guard exists for: `server.ts` loads each function inside a try/catch and omits the ones that throw, so a parse error there is a silent production 404 on the whole function. Anchor an import insertion on a complete single-line import statement, and run `check:edge-boot` after touching any edge import.

THE INVOICES DIVERGENCE ITSELF, worth knowing before anyone proxies the prefix: `/api/invoices` is NOT in `crmProxies` and Express registers only the two bulk POSTs, so `GET /api/invoices`, `/:id`, POST, PATCH and DELETE reach no dev handler while working in production - the split running backwards, which nobody files because production is fine. The page sidesteps it by listing from the proxied `/api/billing/invoices`, which is why it was never noticed: one screen, two backends, and only the bulk half crosses the host boundary.

THE FIRST TWO SCREENS A NEW TENANT SEES 404'D IN PRODUCTION (PROD-008, round 133). `/api/onboarding` is not proxied and `getting-started` and `wizard-state` existed only on Express, so `GettingStarted.tsx` and `SetupWizard.tsx` could not read or save a single tick for any deployed user - and LAUNCH-008 made self-service signup actually provision a tenant sixteen rounds ago, which is what makes these the first thing a new account hits. **The wizard's store was `new Map()` in module scope**, so its progress did not survive a restart in dev either and would have been per-instance under any real deployment; the handler directly below it carried a comment calling itself "durable... unlike the in-memory wizard-state above", so the defect was written down beside the code and nobody joined it to the 404. Both flows are ONE ROW in `onboarding_progress` (`flow_type`, `current_step`, `completed_steps`, `is_complete`), so the port made the wizard durable rather than merely reachable. **Two SCOPED `crmProxies` entries, not the whole prefix**: Express still owns `PUT/DELETE /api/onboarding/sections/:id` and `/tasks/:id`, which the edge function does not serve, so a bare entry would take those from working-in-dev to 404-in-dev.

AN UPSERT NEEDS A UNIQUE INDEX AND THIS TABLE HAS NONE (round 133). `.upsert(..., { onConflict: 'tenant_id,user_id,flow_type' })` is the obvious port and would have been **42P10 on every database**: migration 0000 indexes `onboarding_progress.tenant_id` and `.user_id` separately and declares no unique constraint. Read the DDL before reaching for `onConflict` - PostgREST resolves it against a real index, so the failure is total rather than degraded. Adding the index as a fresh migration would have made the feature depend on a chain this repo ships UNAPPLIED (`0057` is still committed and applied nowhere), so read-then-write is the right shape, with the same race the Express handler had; the READ then needs `.order('updated_at', desc).limit(1)` rather than `.maybeSingle()`, because without the constraint two concurrent saves leave two rows and a reader with no tiebreak answers arbitrarily (round 91).

AN EXPORT THAT NAMES A FORMAT IT DOES NOT PRODUCE (round 133). `server/routes-export.ts` set `Content-Type: application/pdf` and a `.pdf` filename on a body that was **HTML**, under its own comment "in production use puppeteer", and the xlsx content type with a `.xlsx` filename on **`JSON.stringify(data, null, 2)`**. "Export as PDF" downloaded a file no reader opens; "Export as Excel" one Excel refuses. **A DECLARED TYPE IS NOT EVIDENCE** - SEC-SVG-002 records that rule pointed at an upload, and here the SERVER was the one declaring it. The CSV was genuinely CSV and named two columns `onboarding_equipment` has never had (`equipmentType`, `location`), so both were blank on every row ever exported; tsc could not see it because the generators took `any`. All three 404'd in production, **and the client could not have reached them there in any case**: `handleExport` built a relative URL into `document.createElement('a')` - a plain navigation with no Bearer token, which cannot reach an edge function and in production resolves against the static origin where Cloudflare Pages answers the SPA shell. Three layers of one feature, each independently unable to work, which is why no single reading of any one file exposed it. The CSV is now one edge branch through the shared `toCsv` (the old escaper wrapped every field in quotes without doubling an embedded one), the PDF goes through the function's existing `generate-pdf` signed link rather than a second implementation, and **Excel is gone rather than faked** because this tree has no xlsx writer (AUDIT-016).

A TOAST THAT FIRES BEFORE THE REQUEST IS A FABRICATED WRITE OUTCOME (round 133). `handleExport` raised "Export Started - Your PDF export is downloading..." immediately after appending the link element, so a 404 produced a success message and nothing else. Worth grepping for wherever a download is triggered by navigation rather than by a promise: there is no response to check, so the toast can only ever be optimistic.

ANCHOR AN IDENTIFIER MATCH ON BOTH SIDES, IN A TEST AS MUCH AS IN A GUARD (round 133). A derived assertion collected every `item.<column>` the export builder reads and checked each against the migration. `/item\.([a-z_]+)/` pulled **"serial"** out of `item.serialNumber` and reported a correct file as naming a phantom column. `(?![A-Za-z0-9_])` after the capture is the fix, and it is the same rule this file already records for table names (`work_orders` matching `field_work_orders`) and route segments (`/api/pipeline\b` matching `/api/pipeline-config`).

A PREFIX WORD CAN BE TWO DOMAINS, AND THE PAGE THREW BEFORE ANY OF IT COULD BE SEEN (PROD-008, round 134). `/api/catalog` was the PLATFORM MASTER catalogue on Express (`master_product_models`, `master_product_accessories`, `enabled_products`, writes gated on platform admin) and the TENANT's own `product_models` in `supabase/functions/catalog/`, on every branch. AUDIT-031's shape with five consequences at once: `ProductHubUnified` types its list `MasterProductModel[]` and sends `{ masterProductIds, defaultOverrides }`, so in production the list rendered blank cells (tenant rows in snake_case under a camelCase type), `POST /models/:id/enable` looked a master id up in the wrong table and 500'd, `bulk-enable` wanted `productIds` and answered **400 every time**, `PATCH /models/:id` matched nothing, and `import-enhanced` had no branch and 404'd. **The edge function was a duplicate of `supabase/functions/product-models/`**, which serves that table for the routed ProductModels page - checking that is what made repointing it safe rather than a guess. **THE SIXTH DEFECT CRASHED THE PAGE FIRST**: `/api/enabled-products` answers `{ data, total }` on the functions host and a bare array on Express, and the page used the DEFAULT queryFn, so `enabledProducts` held the ENVELOPE and `enabledProducts.some(...)` - called from `isProductEnabled` during the catalogue render - was not a function. Before cataloguing what a page shows, check that it renders; and a page reading two prefixes is reading two contracts.

THREE DEFECTS FELL OUT OF MOVING ONE CSV IMPORTER INTO `shared/` (round 134). `shared/master-catalog-import.ts` is imported by the Deno function and the Node side rather than shipped as two copies with a parity test (round 94). It **split the file on newlines and parsed each line alone**, so a quoted field containing a newline - a description pasted out of a spreadsheet - ended the record early and shifted every column after it; `parseCsv` from `shared/catalog-import.ts` is a real parser. Money went through **`parseFloat`, which reads '12abc' as 12 and '1.234.56' as 1.234**, so a junk cell became a price rather than an absent one - and a price in this catalogue is what a quote is built from, so a wrong number is worse than a blank; `Number()` on the cleaned string rejects both and keeps a real 0. And a duplicate merge that **reported nothing**, so an operator could not tell a 400-row file producing 400 products from one producing 250. Row numbers are the SPREADSHEET's: a cell holding an embedded newline is one row there, however many lines it spans in a text editor.

THE TABLE DECIDES THE GATE LEVEL, NOT THE FUNCTION (round 134, round 76's rule applied). `master_product_models` and `master_product_accessories` carry NO `tenant_id`, so a write changes what every dealer sees - PLATFORM ADMIN, and a manager is not enough. Enabling a product writes `enabled_products`, which IS tenant-scoped and decides what a rep may sell and at what - MANAGER, and checking the page first showed those controls are already behind COMPANY_ADMIN, so the gate is looser than the UI and breaks nobody. Reads stay open on both, because `/product-hub` has no minLevel and browsing is the point of it. **What disappears is written down**: three Express handlers with no caller in any of the seven client trees are not ported, and one of them - a Canon-specific price-list parser hardcoding `manufacturer: 'Canon'` - **was the only writer of `master_product_accessories` and of the base-model-to-accessory relationships anywhere in the tree**, so that table has never been filled through a reachable path.

A STALE OWNERSHIP-BASELINE ENTRY IS PRE-FORGIVENESS FOR WHATEVER LANDS UNDER THAT NAME NEXT (round 134). Proxying two prefixes took `docs/route-ownership-baseline.json`'s bothDivergent list 44 -> 40, and two of the four it dropped were **`mobile` (round 113) and `onboarding` (round 133)**, whose scoped proxy entries had made them unambiguous rounds earlier with nobody tightening the baseline. `check:route-divergence` reported all four as STALE the moment the list moved. Tighten the ownership ratchet in the same commit as a proxy entry. And a resolved domain LEAVES `docs/route-divergence-triage.json` entirely, so its `resolved` count is not a history of everything ever fixed - which is now said in that file's own note.

TWO MUTANTS THIS ROUND, BOTH SHAPES ALREADY IN THIS FILE. A presence check for `notFound` inside the bulk-enable branch passed while the RESPONSE stopped carrying it, because the `const notFound = ...` declaration was still there - bind a report assertion to the response OBJECT, not to the branch that computes it. And a paging assertion counting `fetchAllRows` calls at `>= 5` was a total standing in for a property; it walks each `.from('master_product_*')` site now, deciding by the operation **immediately after `.from()`** whether the chain is a read at all, since a trailing `.select()` on an insert is its RETURNING clause and not a query.

ONE MODULE OVER TWO PARSERS IS HOW A CHECK THAT READS HTML GETS SHARED (PROD-008, round 135). Four of SEODashboard's buttons - `analyze/images`, `check/broken-links`, `check/mobile`, `validate/structured-data` - had no branch in `supabase/functions/seo/` and so 404'd for every deployed user, and the reason they had been held back is real: they parse HTML, Node has cheerio, Deno has node-html-parser, and neither resolves on the other runtime. **The shareable part is the JUDGEMENT, not the function.** Each host extracts a `PageFacts` - attribute reads and nothing else, deliberately too simple to drift - and every decision about what those facts mean lives once in `shared/seo-page-facts.ts`, which both import. Round 94's rule where the whole function cannot move. Round 94 had also NAMED these four in a test designed to fail the day one was served; that block fired correctly and was inverted rather than deleted, which is the mechanism working as designed.

FIVE CLAIMS NOTHING MEASURED, IN FOUR ANALYSERS (round 135). `potentialSavings: 50000` on every non-webp image - a constant wearing a measurement's clothes that summed into a page total, and which never reached the database anyway because the column is `potential_savings_bytes` and drizzle drops a key the table does not have. `smallText`, counted with cheerio's `.css('font-size')`, which reads an INLINE style attribute - there is no cascade in a parser - so it only ever counted elements carrying an inline size, close to none, while feeding an issue string AND the mobile score. `hasTouchFriendlyElements: true`, hardcoded under its own comment saying it "would need more complex analysis". `mobileLoadTime/Fcp/Lcp: 0`, which is instant rather than unmeasured, and two of the three DID store. And `richResultsEligible`, set from the presence of `@context` and `@type`, which says nothing about the required properties of the specific type. All five are `unbacked` strings the page renders under "Not measured by <check>".

FOUR MARKUP-CHECKER DEFECTS WORTH RECOGNISING ANYWHERE (round 135). **`alt=""` IS NOT A MISSING ALT** - it is how a decorative image is marked and a screen reader skips it correctly, so `!altText` reported the page that had done the accessible thing as having done nothing. **AN EXTENSION IS ON THE PATH, NOT THE URL**: `/hero.png?v=2` gave a format of `png?v=2`, so a cache-busted image was neither optimized nor recommended anything. **`@graph` IS THE COMMONEST JSON-LD SHAPE AND WAS UNHANDLED** - one envelope carrying `@context` and an array of nodes reported "Missing @type" on correct markup, as did a top-level array of schemas; members now inherit the envelope's context. **A FETCH BUDGET COUNTS WHAT YOU WILL REQUEST**, not raw anchors: a page opening with twenty `#section` links spent the whole allowance on links it skips and requested none of the real ones. Plus: Flash detected by the `type` attribute alone misses `<embed src="...swf">`, which carries none, and a zoom-blocking viewport (`user-scalable=no`) was not flagged though it fails WCAG 1.4.4.

A PANEL'S FALSE BRANCH CAN BE AN INVENTED FINDING (round 135). `MobileAnalysis` declared `hasViewport`, `touchElementsSize` and `textReadability`; the service has always sent `hasViewportMeta`, `hasTouchFriendlyElements` and `hasReadableText`. All three were `undefined` on every response, so the panel rendered **"✗ No", "✗ Too Small", "✗ Issues" for every URL ever checked**. PA-040's key-name shape with the damage inverted: a missing key usually renders blank, and here each ternary asserted a failure. When a panel's rows are ternaries over optional fields, compare the key names before reading the verdict.

TWO WRITE PATHS CLOSED ON THE WAY PAST (round 135, round 122's rule). All four Express handlers spread the analyser result into drizzle (`...image`, `...link`, `...mobile`, `...schema`), so what stored was whatever happened to line up; they name columns explicitly now, and the six that need a rendered page or a byte count are left NULL rather than filled. And `POST/PUT /api/seo/keywords` spread `...req.body`, so a caller could set `currentPosition`, `bestPosition`, `impressions`, `clicks` and `ctr` - **exactly the columns the position checker measures** - and the rank-tracking panel would report numbers the user typed in.

TWO BASELINES HOLD ONE GAP, AND CLOSING IT TOUCHES BOTH (round 135). Four domains cleared from `docs/edge-path-coverage-baseline.json` and only one was this round's: `catalog: import-enhanced` (round 134), `onboarding: export/getting-started/wizard-state` (round 133) and `integrations: oauth` (round 129) had all been served rounds earlier with nobody tightening this particular ratchet - the stale-entry-is-pre-forgiveness shape round 134 found in the OWNERSHIP baseline, one guard over.

THREE ASSERTION SHAPES THIS FILE ALREADY RECORDS, ALL THREE FIRED AGAIN (round 135). A fixed 1400-character window round an insert ran into the response object below it, whose `unbacked:` key then read as a phantom column - bound on `createCorsResponse(`, the construct that always closes one of these. A check for `'...mobile,'` reported `res.json({ url, ...mobile })`, which is a RESPONSE and correct - bound to the `.values(` call instead. And `not.toContain('mobileResults.hasViewport')` failed against the correct `mobileResults.hasViewportMeta`, because the first is a substring of the second - anchor on `(?![A-Za-z0-9_])`, the same overlap trap as `subject:` inside `activity_subject:`. One more, from the round before it was needed: `seo-dashboard-endpoints.test.ts` pinned `export const CHECKED_LINK_LIMIT = 20;` in a file the constant correctly left, so it blocked the change that made the two hosts share one budget - the fourth time a test has had to be rebound from a spelling to a property.

A WEAK MUTANT IS NOT A WEAK TEST, AND CHEERIO IS THE WORKED EXAMPLE (round 135). Swapping `$(el).html()` for `$(el).text()` when reading a JSON-LD block survived, and reads as a missing test about entity decoding. It is not: htmlparser2 stores script content as a raw-text node, so both accessors return the same string for a `<script>` and the mutation changes nothing. The test binds to the PROPERTY - `Ben &amp; Jerry` still reads as `Ben &amp; Jerry` by the time `JSON.parse` sees it - which is what the HTML spec says about character data in a script and what an entity-decoding read elsewhere would quietly rewrite. The code keeps `html()` as the explicit raw-content accessor with a comment saying which line is load-bearing.

A WORD COUNT THAT COUNTED JAVASCRIPT, AND THREE MORE MEASUREMENTS THE AUDIT GOT WRONG (PROD-008, round 136). `POST /api/seo/audit` is SEODashboard's primary button and had no edge branch, so it 404'd for every deployed user; round 135's extraction split made the port mechanical, with `PageFacts` extended and `shared/seo-audit.ts` holding the whole evaluation for both hosts. **Word count came from `$('body').text()`, which includes the text of every inline `<script>`** - a JS-heavy page reported thousands of words of minified JavaScript and passed the thin-content check, and a page with no body reported ONE word because `''.split(' ')` has length 1. **Internal links were `a[href^="/"], a[href^="' + $('base').attr('href') + '"]`**; with no `<base>` that second selector is the literal `a[href^="undefined"]`, so a site writing absolute same-origin URLs had no internal links by this measure and was told to add some however many it had. **The performance score could only be 93 or 100** - exactly one issue existed to deduct for - so a page with no compression and no caching headers scored a perfect 100 while the recommendations underneath said otherwise. And the heading check reported "Heading hierarchy is not properly structured" with no location; it names the jump and the heading now, which is the difference between a verdict and a change somebody can make.

TWO ANALYSERS CAN DISAGREE ABOUT THE SAME PAGE (round 136). The audit counted images with `$('img:not([alt])')` - correct - while the image analyser one file over used `!altText`, which treats `alt=""` as missing. The same page got two answers depending on which button was pressed; both read the shared rule now. Worth grepping for wherever two features measure one thing: the disagreement is invisible from inside either one.

A CLAIMED ROW NEEDS A FAILURE PATH, AND THE GUARD FOUND THE ONE I LEFT (round 136). The audit branch writes `status: 'running'` before the fetch so a request that dies mid-audit leaves a record; a refused or unreachable URL then marks that row `failed`, because otherwise the history list shows the audit as in progress for ever. `check:swallowed-writes` caught the marker ITSELF discarding its result - the case where the row really does stay stale - so the response carries `auditRecordStale` and the audit id. **A guard firing on code you wrote ninety seconds ago is the cheapest time it will ever fire**, and this is the second round running that one has.

THREE MUTANTS SURVIVED IN ONE BATCH AND ALL THREE WERE THE FIXTURE (round 136). The noisy `<script>` sat in the HEAD, so stripping scripts from the BODY never mattered. No image carried `alt=""`, so counting an empty alt as missing was indistinguishable from the fix. And compression and caching were varied TOGETHER, so either could carry the assertion while the other was dropped in silence. Round 90's rule three times over: make the fixture contain the case that separates the implementations - and vary one input at a time when several feed one score. **A fourth came from the property shorthand**: `toMatch(/\n\s+bodyText,?/)` also matches `bodyText: ''`, so a mutant returning nothing passed; require the comma immediately after the identifier.

## SEO surface (SEO-001..016)

THE PUBLIC SITE IS CLOSED AND THAT CHANGES WHAT IS TRUE. `App.tsx`'s `COMING_SOON`
gate defaults to ON (`VITE_COMING_SOON` must be the string `'false'` to open it), so
every marketing route serves `pages/marketing/ComingSoon.tsx` and only 10 routes
render themselves: the five auth pages, and `signup` + the eight legal pages. Anything
you conclude by reading the marketing pages is about a page nobody is being served.
The gate's `noindex` is applied at RUNTIME by `usePageSeo`; the STATIC head is
rewritten to `noindex, nofollow` at build time by the `printyx-robots-meta` plugin in
`vite.config.ts`, from the same variable. Both halves are needed - before SEO-003 the
static head said `index, follow` on 24 URLs that are all one holding page.

**Three build artifacts, one source each.** `client/public/{sitemap.xml,robots.txt,llms.txt}`
are generated, committed and served from disk by Express (`publicFile()` in
`routes-seo-core.ts`, NODE_ENV picking dist-vs-client/public so a stale `dist` cannot
win in dev). Regenerate with `npm run seo:sitemap` / `seo:llms`; `npm run check:sitemap`
fails when the committed sitemap is stale. Do NOT reintroduce a per-request version of
any of them: `routes-seo-core.ts` used to compose sitemap.xml and robots.txt from
`seo_pages`, and because `registerSeoCoreRoutes` runs before `serveStatic` those won
wherever Express served the app while Cloudflare Pages served the files - two answers
per URL, decided by host (SEO-006). The DB-derived sitemap also published `/crm`,
`/reports`, `/product-hub`, `/service-hub` and `/product-catalog`, five login-walled
routes, one of which the robots.txt beside it disallowed.

**ONE WRITER FOR STRUCTURED DATA, AND IT READS THE ROUTE TABLE.** `PUBLIC_ROUTES_SEO`
in `client/src/lib/seo/seoConfig.ts` is the single source; `SEOProvider` applies it.
Three separate systems had grown alongside it and each one caused a distinct defect:
`usePageSeo` in `BlogPostLayout` (SEO-009) hardcoded `baseUrl = 'https://printyx.com'`
and, running last, gave every blog post a CROSS-DOMAIN `rel=canonical` - asking Google
not to index printyx.net's blog at all; `useSeo` (SEO-014) fetched `/meta.json` with a
raw relative fetch that Cloudflare Pages answers with the SPA shell, so its
`meta?.title || 'Printyx'` fallback titled the three highest-priority `/p/` landing
pages the bare word "Printyx"; and `SmartBreadcrumb` (SEO-016) injected a second
`BreadcrumbList` beside SEOProvider's. All three are gone. A component that hardcodes
a hostname is how the worst of these happened - `check:seo-assets` now walks all of
`client/src` and every same-origin asset URL must resolve.

**A DATE OR A PRICE IN STRUCTURED DATA IS A CLAIM.** `generateArticleSchema` set
`datePublished`/`dateModified` to `new Date()`, so every article reported it was
published at the instant it was viewed - the field Google prints beside a result
(SEO-009). Both are optional now: no date beats a wrong one. `llms.txt` advertised
`$49/user/month` against flat $79/$99/$149 Stripe products and the head's offer said
"per user per month" (SEO-007); `shared/pricing-plans.ts` is the one source, read by
`scripts/setup-stripe-products.ts` AND the llms generator, and `check:seo-assets`
fails when a published price or unit disagrees with it. The static head also carried
a hardcoded `aggregateRating` of 4.9 from 150 ratings that LEGAL-002 had removed from
the runtime provider and missed - fixing the runtime copy of a fabrication is not
fixing it.

**AN UNKNOWN ROUTE IS NOT PUBLIC (SEO-013).** `getSEOConfig` used to fall through to
`null` for anything outside a list of 22 noindex PREFIXES, and `SEOProvider` turns
null into an indexable default - 189 of ~250 app routes told crawlers to index them.
The default is inverted: anything not in `PUBLIC_ROUTES_SEO` is noindex. Adding a
PUBLIC page means adding a route-table entry, which it needs anyway. `getSEOConfig`
therefore never returns null; use `findSEOConfig` where "no metadata" must stay null
(link titles, related links), or every unknown link gets titled "Printyx".

**A URL FIX IS NOT A FEATURE FIX (SEO-004, SEO-008).** `SEODashboard` was written
against `/api/seo/<noun>/<verb>` and `routes-seo.ts` registers `/api/seo/<verb>/<noun>`,
so twelve endpoints hit no router in dev OR prod. Repointing four of them connected one
to `analyzeSemanticKeywords`, a TODO stub returning `intentConfidence: 80` for every
keyword - one of five stubs whose handlers STORED invented scores (`readabilityScore: 75`,
`seoScore: 80`, `readingLevel: 8.5`, `similarityScore: 0`) into the `seo_*` tables. All
five answer 501 now. Check what is behind a URL before wiring it. The real analysers in
`server/services/seo-service.ts` fetch the page and measure it. **CORRECTED 2026-09-20
(SEO-TRANSPORT-001 round 94): `checkCoreWebVitals` threw without a PageSpeed KEY and
fell back to `estimateCoreWebVitals()` on any API FAILURE - LCP 2500ms, CLS 0.1, a
performance score of 75 - which the route then stored in `seo_core_web_vitals`, so a
page nobody measured reported respectable vitals. That fallback is deleted and the
failure propagates.** `checkBrokenLinks` fetches only the
first `CHECKED_LINK_LIMIT` links and records the rest as UNCHECKED (null), not as 200.

**Guards.** `check:sitemap`, `check:seo-assets` (asset URLs across all of `client/src`,
JSON-LD parses, declared image dimensions vs the real PNG, no `aggregateRating`,
name-vs-property agreement between the head and every `setMeta` call, published price
vs the plan module). Both are zero-tolerance, not ratchets. MUTATION-TEST A NEW GUARD:
the first version of the asset walk passed cleanly while matching nothing, because its
comment-stripper used `/\/\/.*$/` which finds the `//` inside `https://` and deletes
the rest of the line. It looked healthy. Only mutating a URL and watching it NOT fail
exposed it.

**Open**: `tasks/prd-public-blog-disconnect.json` (the public blog is three .tsx files
and reads `blog_posts` nowhere, while the blog platform publishes there) and
`tasks/prd-seo-prerendering.json` (one static head describes the homepage on every
URL; masked entirely by the coming-soon gate and unmasked the day it lifts).

A TEST THAT PINS A SHARED MODULE SAYS NOTHING ABOUT WHETHER EITHER HOST CALLS IT (EDGE-024, round 138). `shared/task-workflow-state.ts` is the stage math behind every human workflow - which stage is current, whether completing a step cascades into an advance, what a regress resets - and **three implementations of it existed**. The edge function carried a 40-line REPLICA under a header saying "Deno cannot import from shared/", and the Express engine imported three of its exports and hand-inlined the rest (`applyStepCompletion` as a `.map`, `isStageComplete` as a `.every`, `completionOutcome` as an if/else). Both headers cited `server/tests/unit/task-workflow-state.test.ts` as pinning the transitions; that suite imports the shared module and **reads neither host**, so it was green while pinning the one copy nobody ran. Nothing else could see it either: tsc does not reach the edge tree, `check:edge-boot` only asks whether imports resolve, and a hand-inlined `.every(...)` is not a redeclaration any grep would flag. Both hosts import and call the one module now, `completionOutcome` decides the cascade on both sides, and `server/tests/unit/task-workflow-shared-state.test.ts` asserts the arrangement that makes the sibling suite mean anything - both import it, neither redeclares a transition, and the import SPECIFIER RESOLVES on disk rather than matching a pinned depth. **THE GENERAL CHECK: for any `shared/` module named as canonical, grep each host for a CALL, not an import.** An import that nothing invokes reads as wired and decides nothing, and a module whose exports have no caller is a test fixture wearing a contract's clothes. Where an export genuinely has no host caller because it is an internal building block, NAME it with a reason (`INTERNAL_ONLY` in that test, asserted in both directions) rather than letting the absence read the same as an oversight.

"DENO CANNOT IMPORT FROM shared/" IS FALSE AND HAD PROPAGATED INTO THREE HEADERS (round 138). A dozen edge functions already do it - `catalog`, `crm`, `deals`, `invoices`, `public-booking`, `sales-territories`, `record-layout-config`, `manufacturer-integrations` - and rounds 134-136 added three more. The claim is the stated REASON for two more live replicas: `shared/contract-pnl-math.ts` and `shared/quote-math.ts` both say it, both are duplicated inline in their edge functions, and `quote-math`'s copy is the money arithmetic on every quote. Collapsing those is separate work; what this round settles is that the premise is wrong, so nobody re-derives a replica from it. The constraint that IS real: a shared module must be dependency-free and reach for no environment, because `process.env` and `Deno.env.get` are the same configuration spelled two ways - round 123 turned a bucket name into a parameter for exactly that reason - and the new test asserts `shared/task-workflow-state.ts` has no import at all.

SEVENTY-ONE FILES TOAST THE RAW `NNN: {json}` BLOB AT THE USER (round 138). `throwIfResNotOk` builds `new Error(\`${res.status}: ${text}\`)` and 71 client files pass `err.message` straight into a toast description, so this product's documented error format - `{ message, code, details, requestId }`, which `check:error-shape` exists to enforce - reaches a person as `501: {"message":"File parsing is not available on this backend...","notImplemented":true}`. The server's sentence is right there and nobody can read it. CLAUDE.md already recorded the other half (CRM-008): `error.response?.data?.message` is always undefined here, because the thrown value is a plain Error and not an axios error, so pages that reached for the reason got the generic fallback instead. **THE `NNN: ` PREFIX IS LOAD-BEARING AND CANNOT BE REMOVED** - `queryClient.ts` and `rbacQueryClient.ts` branch on `message.includes('403')` and `startsWith('404')` for retry and returnNull behaviour - so the parsed response is ATTACHED to the Error instead (non-enumerable, so it does not leak into a log), and `client/src/lib/api-error.ts`'s `describeApiError` prefers it while still parsing the string for an Error raised anywhere else. Two rules it encodes. **A BODY THAT IS NOT A MESSAGE IS NOT SHOWN**: an unproxied path resolves against the static origin in production, where Cloudflare Pages answers the SPA shell, so the "message" would be a whole HTML document - markup is replaced by a sentence derived from the status. And **501 IS NOT A FAULT**: `notImplemented` is reported separately so a page can say "not available here" in the default variant rather than "failed" in red. Only the acute call site is converted (the workflow builder's file upload, which 501s on every production upload by design); the other 70 are a worklist, not a claim.

A FULL `format:write` REWRITES `prd.json`, WHICH IS MIXED-ENCODING (round 138). Round 91 records that a parse-and-rewrite of that file produces a 247-line diff because some non-ASCII in it is raw and some is `\u`-escaped; prettier does the same thing, and a `git status` taken mid-run also showed a baseline JSON being rewritten. Format the files you touched (`npx prettier --write <paths>`) rather than the repo, and check `git status` after any full sweep - this file already records `check:schema` and `check:system` dirtying the tree the same way. Two concurrent `prettier --write .` runs are worse than one: a backgrounded invocation that timed out is still running when the next one starts.

SQL THAT IS IN THE REPO AND IN NO CHAIN ANYTHING RUNS (round 139). `drizzle/functions/*.sql` holds twenty Postgres functions that edge functions reach for with `.rpc('name')` when PostgREST cannot express a query, and it is applied BY HAND (`\i`) - `db:migrate` never touches it, deliberately, because drizzle-kit has no schema delta for a function body. So the only thing connecting an edge function to the SQL it calls is that somebody wrote the file and somebody else ran it, and nothing checked either half: the name is a string, tsc does not reach the edge tree, and the failure is a PostgREST 42883 at runtime. **`geocode-leads` called `exec_sql`, which is defined in no migration, no function file and nothing else in the repo.** `npm run check:edge-rpc` is the gate; 22 call sites across 609 edge files, all resolving now.

**THE DEAD CALL WAS THE ONLY THING KEEPING AN INJECTION SINK DORMANT.** It built `WHERE tenant_id = '${tenantId}'` by interpolation and handed the string to `exec_sql` - and `exec_sql` is a common Supabase helper somebody adds to a project in an afternoon, so the code was one ops decision away from executing an interpolated identifier. It was the SINGLE interpolated SQL literal in the whole edge tree, which is why the guard can hard-gate that at zero. `sql-injection-prevention.test.ts` could not see it: CLAUDE.md records that suite being widened from one named file to every `.ts` under `server/`, and this is the edge tree - **the guard-scope lesson, at tree granularity rather than file granularity.** Its `{ data, error }` was also destructured and never read, so the direct PostgREST counts beneath it were not a fallback, they were the only path, and every GET spent a round trip on a call that always failed. That is the COP-B04 tell again: a local computed and never read is usually a body that was removed or never written.

**A README LISTING FOUR OF NINE FILES OMITTED EXACTLY THE DANGEROUS FIVE** (round 139). `drizzle/functions/README.md` documented `dashboard-widget-data`, `billing-aggregates`, `ai-employee-analytics` and `contract-pnl-refresh`, each with a paragraph on what happens when the file has not been applied. The five it did not list - `global-search`, `sales-pipeline`, `pipeline-config`, `lead-scoring`, `lead-assignment` - hold thirteen functions, and **twelve of the thirteen called ones fail the caller when the file is missing** - `global_search` is the only one that degrades - including `pipeline_deal_transition` (the deals board's drag-and-drop) and all three lead-scoring analytics. The correlation is not a coincidence: the four that were documented are the four whose authors had thought about the missing-function case and written a fallback, which is presumably why they also thought to write a row. So the document whose whole job is to say what a deployment still owes was silent about the endpoints that simply do not work until somebody runs the file. Same shape as `drizzle/cron/README.md` printing "Total: 16 jobs" over a table listing 19. The guard fails on an unlisted file now, so the next `.sql` added there cannot arrive undocumented.

**A FUNCTION WITH NO CALLER, FOUND BY LISTING WHAT THE FILES DEFINE RATHER THAN WHAT THE CODE CALLS** (round 139). `lead_assignment_reset_counters()` resets `leads_assigned_today` and `leads_assigned_this_week` on `rep_capacity`; no edge function calls it, no `drizzle/cron/*.sql` schedules it and no file under `server/` names it, so those counters only ever increase and any routing rule reading a per-day cap is reading a number that never resets. COP-B04's question - ask what FIRES a sweep - pointed at SQL instead of at an endpoint. **AND THE FIRST DRAFT OF THAT COUNT WAS WRONG**, caught by reading the two callers rather than trusting the scan that produced it: `lead_assignment_bump_rep_load` was written up as degrading and does not - `_engine.ts` THROWS on failure while `handlers/assign.ts` discarded the result entirely and answered 201 with the counter unmoved, so the rep kept their previous load and round-robin kept handing them the next lead. Two call sites of one RPC disagreeing about whether its failure matters is the shape to grep for; that path reports `repLoadUpdated: false` now rather than failing the assignment, because the history row is already written by then. **The note asserting it is backed by a test that re-derives the absence across all three hosts**, because a "nothing calls this" comment that one host contradicts is worse than no comment.

THREE MUTANTS SURVIVED AND ALL THREE WERE FIXTURES, TWO OF THEM FOR THE SAME RECORDED REASON (round 139). Swapping the comment-stripper to block-comments-first survived because the fixture was `// a path like /reports/sales/*` and one statement: with no `*/` anywhere, the non-greedy `[\s\S]*?\*\/` matches nothing and both orders behave identically - **round 131 recorded this exact trap and I reproduced it from scratch**, so the fixture now carries a real block comment below the trailing-`/*` line. Dropping the interpolation requirement survived because the real tree has no non-interpolated SQL template literal, and dropping the README rule entirely survived because the directory is now fully documented - in both cases a disabled rule and a working rule agree on a clean tree, which is round 92's lesson arriving through the FIX rather than through the fixture. The repair for the last one was to give `undocumentedSqlFiles(dir = FN_DIR)` a parameter so it can be pointed at a temp directory holding one listed and one forgotten file. **A hard gate at zero needs its rules exercised against fixtures precisely BECAUSE the tree is clean**, or the run proves only that nothing is wrong today.

A TAB THAT RENDERED NOTHING FOR EVERY CUSTOMER, BECAUSE A FILTER RETURNED `undefined` RATHER THAN `false` (round 140). `supabase/functions/contracts/` selected `*` and answered the raw PostgREST row; `CustomerContracts.tsx` reads camelCase. So `contractNumber` was undefined on every contract, and the filter read `a?.toLowerCase().includes(t) || b?.toLowerCase().includes(t)` - **which is `undefined` when both fields are absent, not `false` and not `true`** - so `matchesSearch && matchesStatus` was falsy and `filteredContracts` was EMPTY whatever the user typed, including nothing at all. The Contracts tab of the Customer Detail page showed "no contracts" for every customer on both hosts, and it looked exactly like a customer who has none. CRM-008 round 65's shape (a write path storing correctly while the read path has no mapper) with the optional chain turning a blank row into an empty LIST rather than a row of blanks, which is the harder one to notice.

**CAMELISING A SHARED RESPONSE IS A QUESTION ABOUT EVERY CALLER, AND THE ANSWER WAS MEASURED** (round 140). Seven callers, checked one at a time rather than reasoned about: four pages (`contracts.tsx`, `Invoices.tsx`, `MeterReadings.tsx`, `AdvancedReporting.tsx`) normalise with `row.contract_number || row.contractNumber || ''` and tolerate either spelling; two (`CustomerContracts`, `MeterBilling`) read camelCase only and were broken; and **iOS sets `.convertFromSnakeCase`, which only rewrites keys containing an underscore, so an already-camel key arrives unchanged and still matches the Swift property.** So the conversion fixes two callers and breaks none - but the tolerant-normalizer pattern is what made it safe, and finding that took one grep per file. PROD-013's rule (check the shape every caller reads before converting) applied to the response side.

**A PREDICTION DISPROVED BY READING, TWELFTH TIME** (round 140). The list answers an envelope `{ data, total, page, limit }` and the component types it `Contract[]` and calls `.filter()` on it, which reads as round 134's `ProductHubUnified` crash exactly. It is not: `getQueryFn` AUTO-UNWRAPS `{ data: [...] }` when `data` is an array, so the component receives the array. Worth knowing before writing up any envelope-versus-array mismatch on a query that uses the DEFAULT queryFn - the crash only happens when a page supplies its own.

**A COLUMN LIST READ FROM A LINE RANGE CAN BE A DIFFERENT TABLE** (round 140). A first pass at "which columns does `contracts` have" used `sed -n '1828,1885p' shared/schema.ts` and produced `contract_status`, `monthly_base_rate`, `bw_overage_rate`, `includes_toner`, `response_time_hours` - a plausible service-contract shape belonging to a DIFFERENT table, which led to a confident and wrong note that `status` was really `contract_status`. `awk '/^export const contracts = pgTable/,/^\);/'` bounds it to the declaration, and the migration chain settles it: 0000 creates twelve columns and 0070 adds four, so `contracts` has sixteen and `status` is one of them. **Bound a schema read on the declaration, never on a line range**, and confirm against the chain (round 88: when the declaration and the chain disagree, the live shape wins - here they agree, and the line range agreed with neither).

**EIGHT FIELDS WITH NO COLUMN, AND THE RIGHT ANSWER DIFFERED PER FIELD** (round 140). `contractType`, `autoRenewal`, `renewalTerms`, `totalContractValue`, `currentMonthlyBilling`, `lastBillingDate`, `nextBillingDate` and `equipmentCount` are on no `contracts` column and nothing derives them. Five were declared and never rendered - dead interface. The other three drove a billing-model badge, an Auto Renewal column and a `Yes (N months)` row, so every contract would have read "No" once the data arrived, which is a claim about renewal that nothing records: DELETED per AUDIT-016. Three judgement calls worth keeping. `acquisition_type` IS a column and was NOT substituted for `contractType` - it records how equipment was acquired (cash/lease), not the billing model, and conflating them is COP-B10's unreviewed merge. `hasTieredRates` is not a column either but IS derivable, so it is computed from `contract_tiered_rates` in ONE batched `.in()` over the page's ids rather than a lookup per row. And the per-click rates block now shows when the contract CARRIES per-click rates rather than when it is typed `cost_per_click` - the rates are their own evidence.

**A NULLABLE DATE PLUS `format()` IS A THROW, NOT A BLANK** (round 140). Migration 0070 dropped the NOT NULL on `contracts.start_date` and `end_date`, and date-fns `format(new Date(undefined))` raises "Invalid time value" rather than rendering nothing - so the fix that made rows appear would have crashed the tab on the first contract with no dates. `getContractStatus` had the quieter version of the same: `new Date(undefined)` compares false against everything, so it fell through and reported **'active'** for a contract with no dates at all. It falls back to the stored `status` now. Whenever a NOT NULL is dropped, grep for what formats or compares that column.

## Pre-Flight & Pitfalls

**Node 20 is mandatory — check this before believing any failure.** `engines.node` is `20.x` and
`.npmrc` sets `engine-strict=true`, so on any other major `npm install` aborts and leaves
`node_modules` **empty**. Everything downstream then fails for reasons that have nothing to do with
the cause: `tsc` bails before typechecking, `vitest` is absent, the app looks broken. `preinstall`
now blocks this (`npm run check:node`), but if you inherit a half-installed tree, `node -v` is the
first thing to check. Use `nvm use 20 && npm install`.

In a sandbox with no `nvm`, look for Node 20 on disk before concluding the suite
is unrunnable: this container ships it at `/opt/node20/bin` with a
`use-node-20` helper on PATH, while the default `node` is 22. Prefixing the PATH
(`export PATH="/opt/node20/bin:$PATH" && npm install`) installs cleanly and makes
`npm run check`, `npm run test`, `check:phantom-cols` and every other
tsx-dependent script work. Several rounds of this repo's history were written
under the belief that they could not be run at all, and a deleted handler's test
stayed red for a whole session because of it.

Before committing: `npm run check && npm run build && npm run format:write && npm run lint`.

Common mistakes:

0. Debugging a phantom broken repo on the wrong Node version (see above)
1. Missing `tenantId` filter (security)
2. Skipping Zod validation
3. Defining `requireAuth` locally instead of importing
4. Using `db:push` against production
5. Using `any` instead of fixing types
6. Missing TanStack Query loading/error states

Local dev browser login: export `VITE_SUPABASE_URL`/`VITE_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY` (from the server-side `.env` values) before `npm run dev` — the client crashes at boot without them. The dev CSP allows api/functions.printyx.net (server/index.ts). The anon keys currently in `.env`/`.env.example`/`wrangler.toml` are rotated/stale (GoTrue `/auth/v1/health` returns 401 with them); browser login needs a current anon key. Demo creds: `DEMO_USER`/`DEMO_PASSWORD` in `.env`.

Frontend endpoints must exist on BOTH backends: Express `server/routes-*.ts` serves dev, but prod hits the edge function directly — a query param (e.g. `?codes=`) supported only by Express silently breaks in production.

Billing edge fn (EDGE-002a): `/api/billing` is in `crmProxies`, so dev forwards the whole prefix to `supabase/functions/billing/` — full frontend parity lives there (`handlers/` per resource). The canonical billing schema audit is the header of `billing/handlers/_context.ts`: `billing_configurations`/`billing_cycles`/`billing_adjustments` are DRIFT tables (in no Drizzle schema/migration — raw-SQL-only legacy); handlers tolerate a missing relation (GET→`[]`, write→503) via `isMissingTableError`. The real `invoices` columns are `total_amount`/`amount_paid`/`balance_due`/`invoice_status`/`paid_date` — the legacy consolidated Express router referenced phantom cols (`balance`/`paid`/`total`/`tax`). `external_customer_id` doubles as the service-ticket id (`?ticketId=` filter). GET `/billing/invoices` returns `{ data, total }` — frontend consumers must use `extractRecords` (AdvancedBillingEngine crashed on `.map` before). Authenticated binary downloads use `client/src/lib/invoice-pdf.ts` (mirrors `quote-pdf.ts`); plain `fetch` has no Bearer header and 401s on edge functions. Cross-fn import `../email-marketing/_sendgrid.ts` is the accepted idiom (proposals fn does it). Stripe's live webhook URL is `/api/webhooks/stripe`, NOT under `/api/billing` — and that path is the GENERIC receiver in `server/integrations/webhook-routes.ts`, not the Stripe handler in `routes-subscriptions.ts`, which sits at `/api/subscriptions/webhooks/stripe`. INTEG-WEBHOOK-001 found the generic receiver's Stripe branch was a stub that verified the signature and discarded the event, so subscription state never moved from a real Stripe delivery; it now records to `inbound_webhook_events` and dispatches to `StripeService.handleWebhookEventEnhanced`. Two rules follow. A verified delivery is stored BEFORE anything acts on it, because a 2xx stops the provider retrying — never answer 200 from a handler that might not have persisted. And `verifyStripeSignature` enforces a 300-second replay window, matching `stripe.webhooks.constructEvent`; it did not before, so a captured signature was valid forever. AND UNTIL 2026-08-29 NONE OF THAT RAN: `/api/webhooks` is in `crmProxies`, the proxy registers at `routes-registry:297` and the receiver mounted at `:401`, so every inbound delivery went to the webhooks edge function, which calls `auth.getUser()` before routing - a provider sends no JWT, so Stripe and Google Calendar got 401, and the proxy falls through only on a NETWORK error, never a 401. The fix is ordering, not un-proxying: `inboundWebhookReceiver` (just `POST /api/webhooks/:provider`, with its rawBodyParser) is mounted BEFORE the proxy, while the health probe and outbound list stay proxied because the edge function owns them. It is safe because that edge function has `POST /`, `POST /:id/test` and `POST /:id/regenerate-secret` but NO `POST /:id`, so the shape was never contested. `server/tests/unit/inbound-webhook-ordering.test.ts` locks all of it, because ordering is invisible to tsc.

Accounts payable/receivable (EDGE-005a): the canonical tables are `accounts_payable` + `accounts_receivable` (shared/schema.ts, migration 0000) — their columns match the AccountsPayable.tsx / AccountsReceivable.tsx form fields exactly. The `account-payable`/`account-receivable` edge fns serve the FLAT URL shape the frontend calls (GET/POST `/`, GET/PUT/PATCH/DELETE `/:id`, plus `/summary`) and RETURN CAMELCASE rows (the pages read camelCase keys directly off the spread — snake renders blank). Prod resolves the plural `accounts-{payable,receivable}` segment via a `server.ts` route override → singular `account-*` dir; dev mirrors it through `crmProxies`. Earlier versions wrongly queried a phantom `bills` table (AP) and the E-Automate `invoices` table (AR) with nested URLs the frontend never called.

Quote math (QUOTE-016): `proposal_line_items.discount` is a DOLLAR amount off the whole line; `total_price` is stored NET of it, so the quote subtotal is automatically net and the quote-level discount applies after. Canonical formulas live in `shared/quote-math.ts` (replicated inline in the Deno proposals fn — keep in sync, locked by `server/tests/unit/quote-math.test.ts`). Guardrails evaluate the EFFECTIVE discount (`effectiveDiscountPct`: line + quote discounts over gross). Any discount requires `proposals.discount_reason` (+`_note`, migration 0019); the proposals edge fn POST/PATCH retry without those two columns on PGRST204 until the migration is applied. Server max-discount policy: `company_pricing_settings.max_discount_percentage` (0/absent = not enforced); min-margin policy: `pricing_settings.require_approval_below_margin` (defaults 15).

Recurring lines (QUOTE-017): every money field on a recurring line (`is_recurring=true`) is a PER PERIOD amount; `recurring_frequency` ∈ monthly|quarterly|annually (missing reads as monthly), `recurring_duration` = period count, NULL/0 = ongoing (one-time lines store NULL frequency/duration — the edge fn normalizes this). The quote-level discount applies to the ONE-TIME bucket only (`oneTimeBucketTotals` / `recurringTotalsByFrequency` in `shared/quote-math.ts`); the QUOTE-006 guardrail margin and `proposals.subtotal`/`total_amount`/`total_dealer_cost` stay COMBINED across both buckets (one period of each recurring line) so low margin can't hide in recurring lines. `_pdf.ts` renders a separate "Recurring Charges" section + split totals when any recurring line exists; otherwise the classic single-table layout is unchanged.

Quote wizard (QUOTE-019): `QuoteWizardProgress` step headers are clickable in both directions — ALL prerequisite validation lives in `QuoteBuilder.goToStep` (customer unlocks Products+, ≥1 line unlocks Pricing/Review; invalid jumps toast the missing prerequisite; leaving step 0 fires `ensureDraft`). Guardrail math (overallMargin/effectiveDiscount/belowMinMargin/overMaxDiscount) is computed ONCE in the QuoteBuilder body and shared by the send gate, the sticky summary bar (Products+Pricing steps), the Review banner, and the draft-save warning toast — don't re-derive it inline. Cost-derived numbers in always-visible UI are gated by `pricingVisibility.showMargin` (not `showDealerCost`, which gates the Manager Quote block).

QUALITY-002 typecheck ratchet: the canonical error count is whatever `node scripts/check-types.mjs` reports — it counts `: error TS\d+` regex MATCHES, NOT lines, because `--pretty false` suppresses tsc's "Found N errors" summary. `grep -c ": error TS"` undercounts (multi-match lines) and drifts the baseline; always measure batches with the script, not raw grep. Two build caveats: (1) CORRECTED 2026-07-30 (PROD-001): `npm run build` is **NOT** broken — it passes in 1m with the full bundle emitted, and type-only changes CAN be build-verified.
