The tell that settles which one the page was written for is the metadata object: its keys match `SignupRequest.metadata` field for field, and the test derives that list from the handler's own interface rather than pinning it. The documented twelve-character password policy lived in that handler too, so it had never applied to anybody - GoTrue's own minimum is six. **When a story says a page and a handler both exist, grep for the call between them**; two halves that each exist is what this one had.

AN UNREFERENCED EDGE FUNCTION'S FIRST CALLER IS WHAT MAKES ITS THROTTLE NECESSARY (LAUNCH-010, round 116). The signup endpoint is unauthenticated, creates a tenant plus a GoTrue user plus a `users` row, and asks GoTrue to email a verification link to whatever address it was handed - so open it is both a way to fill the tenants table and a way to send mail from this product's domain to anybody. It reuses `shared/public-throttle.ts` and `public_booking_attempts` rather than the in-memory limiter, on that module's own advice: `_shared/rate-limit.ts` says in its header that it is per-Deno-instance and that hard multi-instance limits want a database counter, and a per-isolate cap on account creation is worth what one isolate's memory is worth against a caller that reconnects. The attempts table's shape is generic - `bucket` plus `created_at`, no tenant, no page - and its prune sweep is prefix-agnostic, so a second surface namespaces its buckets and shares it rather than adding a table for four columns. **THE EMAIL BUCKET IS THE ONE BOOKING DOES NOT HAVE**: a per-source limit is defeated by a handful of addresses, and a botnet aiming twenty registrations at one victim's inbox stays under a surface-wide limit while sending that person twenty "verify your account" emails from this domain. It is hashed like the IP, for the reason the module already gives - an attempts table holding every attempted registration's email is a prospect list with no tenant - and lower-cased first, or the control is defeated by the shift key. **THE HONEYPOT IS DELIBERATELY NOT WIRED AND THAT IS A FINDING ABOUT COPYING**: `public-booking` passes `b.website` to `botSignals` because its form has no website field, and this form HAS one, so copying that call verbatim would have refused every registration from a company that filled its own website in.

EVERY LIMITER LAUNCH-010 MARKED "DONE" PROTECTED A PREFIX NOBODY CALLS (round 116). `loginLimiter`, `signupLimiter` and `passwordResetLimiter` are real `express-rate-limit` middleware on real handlers mounted at `/api/auth` - a prefix that is not proxied, so production resolves it to an edge directory that does not exist, while the web client calls `supabase.auth.signInWithPassword` and `resetPasswordForEmail` straight against GoTrue. AUDIT-034's shape again, and the honest answer is the deliverable: **brute-force protection for LOGIN is GoTrue's configuration and no middleware in this tree can see the attempt**, asserted in a test designed to FAIL the day the client stops calling GoTrue directly or a `supabase/functions/auth/` directory appears. The one login endpoint this repo does own, `mobile-auth`, already carries a limiter on its login and refresh branches - checked rather than assumed, and per-instance, which is a real question for a separate story rather than something to assert away.

TWO MUTATION-HARNESS FAILURES IN ONE ROUND, BOTH THE SAME MISTAKE ONE LEVEL APART (round 116). A stub digest that ECHOED its input made "the bucket never contains the address" fail on the fixture rather than pass on the code - COP-B14 recorded that exact failure once already, and it cost a second occurrence before the lesson stuck; use a real `createHash('sha256')`. And three mutants reported SURVIVED against a harness whose vitest target was hardcoded to two other files, so the mutation and the test run were about different code. **Check what the harness RAN, not only where the mutation landed** - this file already records the second half of that rule three times. The survivor that was real: a floor asserting `entries.length` does not bind to a loop that iterates something else, so a mutant walking an empty array passed it. Count INSIDE the loop and assert the count.

`check:api-request-args` CAUGHT A REVERSED ARGUMENT PAIR THAT TYPECHECKED (round 116). `apiRequest` is `(url, method, body)` and both are strings, so `apiRequest('POST', '/api/signup', body)` compiles, lints and builds - it sends the request to a URL of "POST" with the path as its HTTP method, and fails as a network error rather than a readable 4xx. The guard reported it in a full sweep after tsc, eslint and the build were all green on the same line. **Run the whole guard set before believing a change is done**, and pin the argument ORDER in the test rather than the two values, which is what the repaired assertion does.

TIGHTEN A BASELINE BEFORE THE LAST FULL SUITE RUN, NOT AFTER IT (round 116). Round 115 ran the suite green, then tightened `docs/edge-path-coverage-baseline.json`, so the tighten was never covered - and `edge-path-depth.test.ts`, which names the remaining deep shapes BY NAME, went red on the next run against a list that was correct the moment before. The repair is the one that file's own note prescribes: the entry LEAVES the list because it was closed, all three are now closed, and the empty list still bites (a new deep shape in the baseline fails until somebody examines it and lists it). **A baseline tighten is a code change and belongs above the verification, not after it.**

A PATH LIST WRITTEN WITH THE MOUNT PREFIX MATCHES NOTHING, AND IT FAILS IN BOTH DIRECTIONS AT ONCE (LAUNCH-008, round 117). Express rewrites `req.url` inside a handler mounted with a path, so under `app.use('/api', mw)` a request for `/api/health` arrives with `req.path === '/health'` and `req.baseUrl === '/api'`. Two middleware in `server/routes.ts` compared that stripped path against literals in full `/api/...` form, and **the two consequences are mirror images**: the auth gate's `publicPaths` matched nothing, so NO path was public and an unauthenticated caller was 401'd on the health probe, the CSRF token and the inbound webhook receiver - a signed Stripe delivery getting a 401 that no provider reports; and `blockRegistrations` matched nothing, so a pre-launch lock carrying a hardcoded "launching October 1st, 2025" message never fired once, on any host, since it was written. This file already records the same mistake one middleware over ("`pathRequiresMfa(req.path)` can never match, because `app.use(path, mw)` strips the mount prefix"), where it made a control inert rather than inverted. **Prove the semantics by running Express**, which is what the test does - one mounted handler, three requests, the real `req.path`/`req.baseUrl` values - rather than reasoning about them.

`server/lib/public-api-paths.ts` IS THE ONE LIST, AND IT KEEPS THE FULL SPELLING. `fullApiPath(req)` is `req.baseUrl + req.path`, which is neither `req.path` (stripped) nor `req.originalUrl` (carries the query string). Rewriting the list to the stripped shape was the other option and is worse: these paths are named `/api/health` in the proxy table, in the route registry and in every conversation about them, so a list spelled a second way is one somebody re-breaks the next time a mount moves. The segment-boundary rule stays and is why this is not a `startsWith`: `/api/authx` is not public, and `/api/signup-crm` is not reached through the `/api/signup` entry LAUNCH-008 added.

A DEAD LOCK IS DELETED, NOT REPAIRED, WHEN REPAIRING IT WOULD SPLIT THE HOSTS (round 117). `blockRegistrations` could only ever have run on Express, and production resolves `/api/signup` to the edge function on the functions host - so switching it on would have closed registration on developer machines while the deployed product stayed open. Deleting it opens nothing because it closed nothing, and the test proves that rather than asserting it. **What disappears is written down**: there is no registration kill switch, and a real one belongs in `supabase/functions/signup/` because that is what production runs; the assertion fails the day an Express-only lock returns.

A RATE-LIMIT TIER CAN BE CONFIGURED, MOUNTED, DOCUMENTED AND UNSELECTABLE (round 117). `globalTieredRateLimit` classified billing traffic with `path.startsWith('/api/billing')` and four siblings under a mount that had already taken `/api` off, so the billing tier at 20/min could never be chosen and billing, Stripe, subscription, invoice and payment traffic fell through to the mutation and read limiters at 100 and 200/min. Its auth branch survived only because it uses `includes('/auth/')`, which does not care where the prefix went - **the strictest tier worked by luck**, which is worth knowing before anyone tidies it into a `startsWith`.

`npm run check:mounted-path-lists` (round 117) resolves mount sites from the files that call `app.use` rather than guessing: for each `app.use('<prefix>', <arg>)` with a non-root prefix it searches the INLINE arrow at that call site, or the module an identifier was imported from, and reports a literal starting with that prefix in a body that reads `req.path`. **The exemption is what stops a guard arguing for the bug it watches**: a body using `req.originalUrl`, `req.baseUrl` or the shared `fullApiPath(` is spelling it correctly, and without naming the helper the guard reports the sanctioned fix and pushes the next reader back to the broken form. It carries a floor that exits 2 below five resolved mounts, because a mount table that resolves nothing reports nothing and that is indistinguishable from a clean run. Four entries baselined, all `api-versioning.ts`, all one decision: `legacyRouteSupport` is inert for this reason and repairing it would START rewriting every unversioned path into `/api/v1/...`, a routing change rather than a fix.

`git ls-files` READS THE INDEX, SO A WALK OVER IT CAN ENOENT (round 117). `server-orphans.test.ts` reads every file that command names; a file deleted from disk but not yet staged throws and fails the walk for a reason unrelated to its property. It skips what is not on disk now, and the existing corpus floor is what stops that becoming a way to pass by skipping everything. Same round, the same lesson in two more costumes: a floor inside `main()` is not exercised by importing the module (export the analysis and assert the walk's size where a test can see it), and an assertion that a sibling prefix appears in `server/routes.ts` pinned where a list happened to be WRITTEN and broke the moment the list moved into a module of its own - the property was that Express does not mount one prefix over the other, so the test runs Express and checks which handler answers.

A CONTROL THAT IS CORRECT AND REACHES ONE CALLER IS THE SAME GAP AS ONE NOBODY WROTE (SEC-002, round 118). `supabase/functions/_shared/safe-fetch.ts` does the four things that matter - scheme allowlist, private and reserved ranges, DNS RESOLUTION (what stops a public hostname pointing at 127.0.0.1), a timeout, all re-run on every redirect hop - and `social-media` was its only caller out of 255 edge functions. Two endpoints fetched a URL the caller chose AND HANDED THE ANSWER BACK, which makes them read primitives rather than blind requests: `POST /seo/check/security` returns the response headers of `body.url`, `POST /seo/detect/redirect-chains` returns every hop of `body.sourceUrl` with its status and Location, and `POST /webhooks/:id/test` stores `response_body` in `webhook_logs` where the caller reads it. An edge function runs inside the deployment's network with the service-role key in scope. **The SUPERVISOR gate on `webhooks` gets no credit: a role check says who may make the request, not where it goes** - reading it as covering this is how the gap survived a security sweep.

A CLIENT THAT FOLLOWS REDIRECTS IS THE WRONG CLIENT FOR CODE THAT MEASURES THEM (round 118). Two SEO sites cannot use `safeFetch`, because their job is to OBSERVE a 3xx - the https probe and the redirect-chain walker need the status and the Location, and a client that resolves them answers a different question. `safeFetch` grew an exported `assertSafeUrl(url)` for exactly that caller, and now uses it itself for the first request and every hop rather than keeping two inline copies. **The walker validates INSIDE the loop**: the Location of hop N is chosen by whoever controls hop N, so checking only the URL the caller supplied checks the one address an attacker does not need.

A REFUSAL SWALLOWED MID-CHAIN READS AS A HEALTHY REDIRECT (round 118). The walker's catch was `if (steps.length === 0) return 502`, so a refusal on a later hop fell through and the chain summarised as a normal terminus - an operator checking a redirect that ends at an internal address would have been told it was fine. It reports `blockedAt`, and `summariseRedirectChain` answers **`destinationUrl: null`** for a chain that did not reach an end - truncation included, which the module's own header had called "a claim that the redirect ended there" while returning it anyway. A loop keeps its destination, because a loop has one. **Then the NOT NULL column decided the rest**: `seo_redirect_analysis.destination_url` cannot represent "unknown", so an incomplete chain is NOT STORED (`stored: false` plus a reason) rather than stored with the last hop standing in - that column is what an analyst reads as where the redirect goes. The Express twin also stopped spreading the result into its insert: `blockedAt` has no column and drizzle drops an unknown key silently.

`npm run check:ssrf-fetch` (round 118) reports an outbound `fetch` in the edge tree whose URL is not a constant, exempting `safeFetch`, an `assertSafeUrl` above the call in the same function, a literal, and a constant whose value is a literal or `Deno.env.get` - an operator setting an endpoint is not a caller choosing one. 24 baselined, GROUPED BY VERDICT rather than listed: a tenant-configured endpoint, a URL found in content, and a provider call the walk could not prove constant. `edgeFiles()` is exported so the corpus floor is asserted where a test can see it, because a floor inside `main()` is not exercised by importing the module.

THE COMMENT-STRIPPING ORDER BUG, REPRODUCED IN A GUARD WRITTEN THE ROUND AFTER READING ABOUT IT (round 118). Stripping block comments before line comments reads a line comment ending in `/*` as a block opener and blanks everything to the next `*/`. It made `ai-gpt5`'s URL constant vanish and reported the fetch beside it as caller-supplied. This file already records the identical failure in `check:shared-helper-imports`, and I wrote the same bug into both of the last two rounds' new guards. **Line comments first, with the `(?<![:/])` lookbehind so `https://` survives, and blank block comments to SPACES rather than removing them** so reported line numbers stay honest.

FOUR TESTS BROKE ON CORRECT WORK IN ONE ROUND (118). `ssrf-parity` pinned `validateUrl(redirectUrl)` as a literal call, so extracting it into a shared helper failed a strict improvement; `seo-checks` pinned one spelling of an argument list; a fixed 400-character window ran past its branch for the fifth time in this repo's history; and `seo-dashboard-endpoints` asserted every field of a page interface is a COLUMN, which was only true while the response WAS the row. That last one is the interesting one: the rule is "a field the page reads has something behind it", and **a key the endpoint returns is another way to have something behind it** - derived from the shared result type rather than listed, so an invented field still fails.

A TEST THAT IMPORTS THE WHOLE SCHEMA CAN BREAK A TEST NINE FILES LATER (round 118). Asserting a column is NOT NULL through drizzle's `getTableConfig` means `await import('@shared/drizzle-schema')` inside a unit test: green alone, green paired with the file it broke, **red in the full run**, where `storage-tenant-scope.test.ts` reported `storage.getLocationHistory is not a function`. Bisected by moving one file out and re-running rather than guessed. The declaration in `shared/seo-schema.ts` is the source of truth for that column, so reading it costs nothing and pulls in no module graph - prefer the cheap check when the expensive one drags a module registry behind it.

A CORRECT SECURITY HEADER ON THE WRONG HOST IS NO HEADER AT ALL (SEC-003, round 119). Helmet was configured well in `server/index.ts` - nonce-based CSP, HSTS, frameguard, nosniff - and the story was marked COMPLETE. Helmet sets headers on EXPRESS responses. **In production the document a user loads is served by Cloudflare Pages** (`wrangler.toml`: `pages_build_output_dir = "dist"`), and `client/public/_headers` did not exist, so printyx.net answered with no CSP, no X-Frame-Options, no HSTS, no Referrer-Policy and no nosniff. The story's own AC said "all headers present on API **and page** responses" and only the first half was ever true. The policy is one module now (`shared/security-headers.ts`), read by Helmet and by `scripts/generate-security-headers.mjs`, which renders `client/public/_headers`; `npm run build` copies it to `dist/`, which was verified rather than assumed. **FOUR THINGS THE "COMPLETE" POLICY WOULD HAVE BROKEN** the day it reached a real document, each checked against the tree rather than reasoned about. `camera=(), microphone=(), geolocation=()` in Permissions-Policy: all three are used by ROUTED pages (`MobileFieldService`'s GPS watch, the barcode scanner in `useExternalIntegrations`, `VoiceTicketClose`'s MediaRecorder), and an empty allowlist is not advice - the browser rejects the API. Express DOES serve the document in dev, so that was **enforced on every developer machine and on nobody in production**, the dev/prod split running backwards again. `frame-ancestors 'none'` everywhere, while `/f/:token` is the hosted web form and `WebFormBuilder` hands a dealer an `<iframe>` snippet to paste into their own site - being embedded elsewhere IS the feature. No `frame-src`, so the `blob:` iframe behind `invoice-pdf-preview` would have been blocked, because `frame-src` falls back through `child-src` to `default-src 'self'`. And no Sentry origin in `connect-src`, so the CSP that called itself production-ready would have blocked error reporting. **A REPORT-URI IS HOST-SPECIFIC, NOT A CONSTANT**: Express asks for `/api/csp-report` and gets real reports in dev; the generated `_headers` asks for none, because on printyx.net that path is Cloudflare Pages answering the SPA shell (SEO-014's shape), so a report would be POSTed into a 200 of HTML - and `check:uncalled-express` excludes `csp-report` on the stated grounds that this directive names it, so deleting it outright would have made that exclusion's reason false.

THE TEST WAS WHY NONE OF THAT WAS VISIBLE, AND IT IS THE GENERAL SHAPE (SEC-003). `security-headers.test.ts` built its OWN Express app with a hand-copied directive object and asserted against that copy - 314 lines about a fixture. It passed while asserting `camera=()`, the defect, and while asserting a `report-uri` that only ever resolved on a host which does not serve the document. **A test that constructs its own subject cannot fail on the thing it is named for.** Rebuilt to construct the app from the same functions `server/index.ts` calls, with source assertions that the wiring holds (`directives: cspDirectives(`, `setHeader('Permissions-Policy', permissionsPolicy())`, `frameguard: isEmbeddablePath(req.path) ? false`) and an absence assertion that no directive map is hand-written beside the helmet call. `npm run check:security-headers` is a hard gate at zero with four rules, three of them pure and fixture-tested with an accept AND a reject case: the committed `_headers` matches the module; no Permissions-Policy feature the client tree uses is refused; every `<iframe>` embed snippet the UI generates has a frame exemption (`EMBEDDABLE_PATH_PREFIXES`); and the document carries no executable inline script, since a static host cannot mint a nonce. **JSON-LD blocks are exempt BY RULE**: the HTML spec never prepares a data block as a script, so CSP never applies to one - the four in `index.html` are fine and the single module bundle is same-origin, proven against `dist/index.html` rather than assumed. Stated blind spot: the embed rule needs the origin and the path in the SAME file, so a snippet built through a helper (`client/src/lib/booking-link.ts` is one) is invisible to it.

PROVE IT IN A SHELL BEFORE IT BECOMES A COMMENT, ELEVENTH TIME (round 119). A comment claimed `server/index.ts` must import `../shared/...` relatively because `build:fullstack` runs esbuild with `--packages=external`, which would leave `@shared/...` unresolved. **esbuild honours tsconfig `paths`**, so the alias bundles fine; the `ERR_MODULE_NOT_FOUND` that seemed to confirm the claim was `express` failing to resolve from the scratch directory the test bundle was written to - a confound in my own experiment, caught by grepping the bundle for a symbol from the module instead of trusting the error. The comment was deleted rather than reworded. A wrong prediction costs a minute in a shell and costs whoever reads the comment next much more.

AN AUDIT TOOL THAT CANNOT RUN IS WORSE THAN ONE NOBODY WIRED (SEC-005, round 120). `server/scripts/command-injection-audit.ts` was SEC-005's headline deliverable and had never executed once: its CLI guard read `path.resolve(__filename)`, this package is `"type": "module"`, so every invocation threw `ReferenceError: __filename is not defined in ES module scope` before reading a line. It had no npm script and no workflow entry either, and it called `process.exit` nowhere - so even a wired, working copy would have gated nothing. **Three independent reasons it could not have caught anything, in a story marked COMPLETE.** `fileURLToPath(import.meta.url)` is the ESM form; this file already records the mirror-image trap (`import.meta.main` is a Deno API and is always `undefined` in Node, so a seeder ran and seeded nothing). When a story's deliverable is a tool, run the tool.

A GUARD AT 97% NOISE IS A GUARD NOBODY CAN WIRE (SEC-005, round 120). The first real run reported **172 findings**: 151 MEDIUM were `RegExp.prototype.exec` in this repo's own check scripts, ten CRITICAL were Playwright's `page.$eval`/`$$eval` (a bare `\b` sits between `$` and `e`, so the word boundary matched), and four more were the scanner reporting its own pattern descriptions. **Three rules took it to six, and each is a property rather than an exclusion.** The callable names are DERIVED per file from that file's own `child_process` import - named, aliased, namespace, `require` and destructured dynamic import all parsed - because there is no other way to obtain `exec`; a file that never binds the module cannot be calling it, whatever its `.exec(` looks like. The eval pattern is `(?<![$\w.])eval\s*\(`. And the scanner, the prevention test and the audit's own test are exempt BY NAME, never by a `tests/` glob, because each necessarily contains the patterns it is about. **A FOURTH RULE TOOK IT TO THREE AND IS THE ONE TO COPY ANYWHERE**: `execFile`, `execFileSync`, `spawn` and `spawnSync` take an argument ARRAY and never start a shell, so only `exec` and `execSync` can be injected through their first argument - **reporting the remedy as a finding is how a guard teaches people to ignore it**, and `execFileSync(cmd, [args])` is exactly what this scanner's own advice says to write.

THE ONE REAL COMMAND-INJECTION FINDING WAS A CONSTANT TODAY AND A HOLE TOMORROW (SEC-005). `printyx-desktop/src/main/services/windows-service.ts` ran ``execSync(`sc query "${this.serviceName}"`)`` plus `net start` and `net stop` in the Electron main process. `serviceName` is a private field holding a literal, so nothing was exploitable - and the reason to fix it anyway is that a private field exists to be made configurable, and the day it takes an installer flag the shell string is a command injection with no escaping to get wrong. All three are `execFileSync('sc', ['query', this.serviceName])` now. The three sites that remain are developer tooling whose command is a literal in the same file, each carrying its reason in `docs/command-injection-baseline.json`, which REFUSES an entry with no reason. **`server/lib/safe-exec.ts` is the only file under `server/` that names `child_process` at all and nothing imports it** - the wrapper is correct and inert because the request path spawns nothing, which is the right state and not a gap; a test asserts that exactly-one-importer property so the next file reaching for a subprocess is pointed at the wrapper instead of rediscovering it.

FIVE OF SIX SQL FINDINGS WERE THE SCANNER AND ITS TEST REPORTING THEMSELVES (SEC-001, round 120). `audit:sqli` was in package.json and in NO workflow - CR-023's shape, with the story's own AC ("add an npm script") satisfied by a script nobody ran. Both its CRITICAL and its HIGH were `sql-injection-prevention.test.ts` quoting the banned pattern in comments, and two MEDIUMs were `sql-injection-audit.ts` carrying them as its own pattern descriptions. Exempting those two by name and skipping line comments is what made the sixth legible. **THE REAL ONE, AND WHY THE EXISTING TEST MISSED IT**: `server/lib/migrate.ts` built the migration lock's staleness window as `INTERVAL '${LOCK_TIMEOUT_MS} milliseconds'`. `sql-injection-prevention.test.ts` scans every `.ts` under `server/` for exactly that idiom and was green, because its unit pattern named `day|hour|month|year` - **a ban that names one spelling bans one spelling**, the `setMonth`/`setUTCMonth` lesson at unit granularity. The unit is `[a-z]` now and reverting `migrate.ts` turns that assertion red. The lock is `($3 * INTERVAL '1 millisecond')` with the timeout bound, PROVEN through node-postgres against a real Postgres 16 rather than predicted: a lock stale by ten minutes is claimable and a fresh one is refused. Both scanners are hard gates in CI now.

THE COMMENT TRAP, TWELFTH OCCURRENCE, INSIDE MY OWN ASSERT (round 120). A post-edit `assert 'execSync' not in s` on `windows-service.ts` failed against a correctly converted file, because the comment I had just written explains that the calls use "execFileSync with an argument array, not execSync with a template literal". The file was right and the check was wrong; the scanner reported 0 critical on the same tree a second later. An absence assertion over raw source will eventually report its own explanation as the defect, however many times the rule has been written down.

ADDING AN NPM SCRIPT CHANGES WHAT `check:server-orphans` CALLS DEAD (round 120). That walk roots at `server/index.ts` plus every server file a package.json script executes (round 90), so wiring `check:command-injection` made `server/scripts/command-injection-audit.ts` reachable and left a stale entry in `docs/server-orphans-baseline.json`. The guard itself went green and `server-orphans-entry-points.test.ts` went red in the full suite - one guard's premise surfacing as another guard's finding, again. Tighten the orphan baseline in the same commit whenever you add a script that runs a `server/` file.

A POLICY GATE ON A PATH PRODUCTION DOES NOT SERVE IS NOT A POLICY, AND IT IS NOW GUARDED (LAUNCH-013, round 121). `server/middleware/subscription.ts` exports ten gates, fifteen of them are MOUNTED across `routes-subscriptions`, `routes-csv-import` and `routes-crm-core`, and not one runs in production: `getApiUrl` rewrites `/api/<seg>` straight to the functions host, and `leads`, `import`, `subscriptions` and `billing` are all real edge directories, so Express never sees those requests on a deployed host. **What that leaves open was measured, not assumed**: grep `tenant_subscriptions|subscription_plans|usage_metrics` across `supabase/functions` and only `subscriptions/` (the management surface) and `_shared/stripe.ts` match - **`supabase/functions/billing/`, the function behind every billing operation, carries no plan check at all**, and neither does `leads`. So the story's criterion is false on both halves: the gate is not there, and the copy that exists cannot run. **THIS SESSION FOUND THE SAME SHAPE FIVE TIMES BY HAND FIRST** - LAUNCH-010's auth rate limiters, AUDIT-034's IP whitelist and MFA gate, SEC-003's Helmet, and these - so `npm run check:dead-policy-gates` is worth more than the eight entries in its baseline. It reproduces a finding round 92 made by hand (`technician-management`'s Express `requirePermission` against ungated edge writes), which is the vacuity proof a new guard needs.

**THE AMBIENT STACK IS THE WHOLE DESIGN PROBLEM IN THAT GUARD** (round 121). `requireAuth`, `isAuthenticated`, `resolveTenant`, `requireTenant`, `protectedRoute` account for 240 of ~500 route registrations and are dead on a proxied prefix BY DESIGN - the edge function authenticates and resolves tenancy itself. Reporting them buries the eight findings that matter under 240 that do not, which is the `check:drift` lesson (99.7% false positives is a report nobody reads). They are exempt BY NAME **with a reason each**, asserted by a test, and `requirePermission`/`requireFeature`/`enforceUsageLimits`/`can` are asserted NOT to be exempt - because the line between "the edge function does this automatically" and "this is a decision somebody wrote down" is the entire value of the guard. Each baseline entry then says COVERED (six: the SEC-EDGE-001 catalogue family and technician-management are gated edge-side) or GAP (two), so the list is a worklist rather than a tally.

TWO SMALLER FINDINGS FELL OUT OF THAT SWEEP (round 121), both worth knowing. `/api/companies/check-duplicate` has no branch in `supabase/functions/companies/`, so it 404s in production while Express serves it in dev - and the Zod validator on it is what made the guard report the path. And `supabase/functions/meter-readings/` has **zero role gates** while `routes-products-crud.ts` checks a permission on `/api/meter-readings` that nobody reaches: a meter write is what becomes an invoice, so that is a SEC-EDGE-001 entry the family sweep missed because meter-readings is not part of the catalogue family it was scoped to.

A FLOOR THAT LIVES INSIDE `main()` IS NOT EXERCISED BY IMPORTING THE MODULE, TWICE IN TWO ROUNDS (rounds 120-121). Both new guards lost the same mutant first time: setting the corpus floor to 0 left every test green, because the tests asserted `process.exit(2)` appears in the source and then measured the real repo, which is above any floor. The repair both times is to EXPORT the number (`MIN_CORPUS`, `MIN_REGISTRATIONS`) and assert it is meaningful, alongside asserting the walk size. A source check cannot tell a working floor from a disabled one - the constant is still in the file either way.

A COMMENT EXPLAINING A FIX DOES NOT APPLY IT TWELVE LINES DOWN (SEC-EDGE-001, round 122). `supabase/functions/meter-readings/`'s POST branch carries a comment naming six phantom columns - `customer_id`, `black_count`, `color_count`, `black_usage`, `color_usage`, `reading_type` - and saying every create used to be a 42703 because of them. **The PATCH branch twelve lines below still mapped five of those six.** An unknown column fails the WHOLE statement, so amending any meter value was a 42703 behind a generic 500, while amending only `notes` or `readingDate` worked - which reads as intermittent rather than broken. `check:phantom-cols` could not see either set: both payloads are named variables, its documented blind spot. Nothing in any client tree calls PATCH or DELETE here, so this is COP-B03's setup - harmless until somebody wires a caller - and it was rebound rather than left as a trap. **When you fix a phantom payload, read every other write branch in the same file before closing the story**; the comment you leave behind is evidence the next branch was never opened.

`supabase/functions/_shared/meter-reading-write.ts` IS THE WRITE PATH NOW, and three of its rules generalise. The column list is locked against drizzle's `getTableConfig` by a unit test, which is the COP-B04 treatment for a payload built by a helper and the only thing that can check one. Both spellings are accepted and `blackCount` means the COUNTER, because that is what the POST branch already reads it as - two branches each deciding for themselves what a request field means is how they drift. **THE DELTA IS RECOMPUTED, NEVER LEFT**: `black_copies` DEFAULTS TO 0 (COP-B05), so a stale delta after somebody corrects a counter is indistinguishable from a month in which the machine printed nothing, and every volume, cost-per-page and TCO figure downstream inherits it; a counter that ends up LOWER than its previous yields no delta at all, because that is a meter reset or a swapped machine and a negative page count on an invoice is worse than a blank. The stored row is read FIRST, so the merge is possible at all and so not-found is a 404 rather than a `.single()` error, and an empty plan answers 400 rather than a 200 that bumps `updated_at` (COP-M01).

A METER READING IS THE BILLING RECORD, WHICH IS WHAT SETS THE GATE LEVEL (round 122). `meter_readings` carries `invoice_id`, `invoice_number`, `billing_status` and `billing_amount` beside the counters, and `shared/fleet-assessment.ts` derives every volume figure from consecutive readings. So GET and POST stay open - `/meter-readings` has no minLevel in `navigation-permissions.ts` and submitting a reading is a technician's daily job - while PATCH and DELETE need SUPERVISOR: amending is a billing correction, and **deleting is the worse of the two, because it removes the evidence and silently changes every derived volume after it** with nothing on any screen saying a reading used to be there. The family sweeps of rounds 74-85 missed this function because it is not part of the `catalog-import-runner` family they were scoped to; `check:dead-policy-gates` found it in round 121 from the other direction - an Express twin guarding a permission nobody reaches.

THREE SURVIVING MUTANTS, THREE ASSERTION SHAPES THIS FILE ALREADY RECORDS (round 122). `expect(plan).toEqual({})` PASSES on `{ notes: undefined }`, so the mutant that stopped skipping undefined values survived - and PostgREST would have received the key and nulled the column, the opposite of ignoring it; assert `Object.keys(plan)` instead. A branch check bound to the MESSAGE (`'Meter reading not found'`) survived `if (false)`, because only the condition changed - bind to the condition. And an assertion that a response carries `NO_WRITABLE_FIELDS` said nothing about its STATUS, so a mutant turning that 400 into a 200 lived; the property was always "a write that changed nothing does not report success".

TWO PREDICTED DEFECTS DISPROVED BY READING, IN ONE ROUND (round 122). `MeterBilling.tsx` calls `apiRequest('/api/meter-readings', { method, body: JSON.stringify(...) })`, which looks like round 116's reversed-argument bug and like a double encode. `apiRequest` supports both an options object and positional arguments, and its `getBodyString` passes a string through untouched - so both readings were wrong. Checking cost two minutes; either one written up would have cost the next reader more.

A COMPLIANCE CONTROL IMPLEMENTED CORRECTLY ON THE WRONG HOST (LEGAL-004, round 123). `server/services/gdpr-storage-erasure.ts` had everything the story asked for - per-bucket outcomes, Art. 17(3) exemptions named per bucket, probe-in-order subject columns for the drift tables, and the backup note corrected because pg_dump archives do not contain bucket contents - and it ran for nobody. **`/api/gdpr` is not proxied, so production resolves it to `supabase/functions/gdpr/`**, whose `data-deletion` branch anonymised `company_contacts` and `business_records` rows, touched no storage at all, and answered "User data has been anonymized" to a data subject exercising Article 17. Same shape as LAUNCH-013 one round earlier, and `check:dead-policy-gates` cannot see it because an erasure is a HANDLER, not middleware - **that guard watches the middleware chain, so a control implemented inside a handler body on a dead prefix is still invisible.** The fix is `shared/gdpr-storage-erasure.ts`, imported by the edge function at depth three and by the Express service through a thin adapter (round 94: one module beats a parity test whenever both runtimes can read it).

**A MODULE THAT REACHES FOR AN ENVIRONMENT CANNOT BE SHARED** (round 123). `process.env.QBR_STORAGE_BUCKET` in Node and `Deno.env.get` in the edge runtime are the same configuration read two ways, so the bucket name became a PARAMETER and the logger became an injected `onError`. The test asserting the shared file names neither environment has to strip comments first, because the header explains why it reads neither and therefore names both - **the comment trap, thirteenth occurrence, in an assertion written with the rule in view.** Two more assertions moved with the thing they checked and were caught by mutation: `expect(DEFAULT_QBR_BUCKET)` compared against itself rather than against the literal `docs/storage-bucket-inventory.md` documents (the two hosts used to disagree, `routes-qbr.ts` defaulting to `qbr`), and a `type: 'customer'` literal survived its source array being replaced with `[]`, so the erasure skipped every business record while the assertion passed. Bind to the SOURCE, not just the shape it produces.

AN INCOMPLETE ERASURE MUST NOT REPORT SUCCESS (LEGAL-004). The production branch now answers `success: false` at 500 when any object could not be deleted, because a completion record that overstates its scope is its own compliance problem - the same false claim this branch had already been corrected for once at the row level (COP-M01 found it writing to columns that do not exist and reporting "anonymized" anyway). The storage-specific half of the backup note travels with it: **pg_dump does not include bucket contents**, so there is no retained copy ageing out behind a failed object removal and anything reported failed still exists. Excluded buckets keep a named reason (`branding-assets` and `blog-assets` are tenant-owned, not personal data about any subject) and the test asserts the REASON is present rather than the bucket merely being absent from the list - a silently absent bucket and a deliberately excluded one read the same otherwise.

CHECK THE BANNER BEFORE RE-FILING A FINDING (round 123). Round 121 flagged `/api/companies/check-duplicate` as a production 404 worth chasing. `server/routes-companies.ts` already carries the triage three lines above the handler: "Neither has a frontend caller, but deleting them removes capability rather than a duplicate, so they stay bannered and baselined." The finding was real and the decision was already made and written down where somebody would read it. Same round: LAUNCH-001 turned out to be genuinely closed, verified by grepping the BUILT bundle (`console.log` count is 0 - vite's `esbuild.pure` strips it) rather than by counting occurrences in source, where there are 72.

A FLAT LIST OF 44 NAMES HELD TWO PRIORITY-1 STORIES (round 124). `check:routes` already FINDS the ambiguous-ownership class - a domain Express registers handlers for, an edge function serves, and nothing proxies between them, so `getApiUrl` sends production edge-side while dev stays on Express - and `docs/route-ownership-baseline.json` recorded 44 of them as names and nothing else. **LAUNCH-013's `leads` and `import` and LEGAL-004's `gdpr` were all three in that list**, indistinguishable from the entries where the divergence is harmless, and each was found by hand a round apart. `docs/route-divergence-triage.json` gives every domain a verdict and a reason (`npm run check:route-divergence`): parity, divergent, resolved, express-only-capability, unexamined. The seeded state is 2 divergent, 6 parity, 4 resolved, 32 unexamined. **UNEXAMINED IS PERMITTED AND IS THE POINT** - a guess written down as a finding is worse than "nobody has looked", which is the rule `docs/edge-rbac-triage.json` paid for four times over when verdicts reasoned from function names had to be overturned. The guard refuses a reason under 40 characters, so "Not examined." fails and an unexamined entry has to name which files the next reader should open; and a `resolved` entry must name its round, asserted by a test, so the claim stays checkable rather than becoming another stale note.

CHECK BEFORE YOU BUILD A GUARD, THIRD TIME THIS SESSION (round 124). The first design for the above was a fresh scanner walking `server/` for route registrations on segments with an edge directory. It measured 40 domains — and `check:routes`' `bothDivergent` class had already computed the same set, with 44 entries, wired into CI since EDGE-018. `ls scripts/ | grep <topic>` and reading the baseline's own `note` field cost two minutes. What was missing was never the detection; it was the per-entry verdict, which is a much smaller and more useful thing to build.

FOUR P1 STORIES SPOT-CHECKED AND FOUND GENUINELY DONE (round 124), recorded so the next sweep does not re-open them: LEGAL-001 (consent gating is real in `client/src/lib/telemetry.ts`, with `check:telemetry-consent` enforcing it), LEGAL-006 (`supabase/functions/qbr/` uses `createSignedUrl`, not `getPublicUrl`), LEGAL-010 (the SOC 2 badge is gone and `SystemSecurity.tsx` says plainly that no report is held), and AUDIT-001 (metadata-first tenant resolution plus the header-mismatch 403 across all nine core CRM functions, enforced by `check:tenant-resolution` at 607 edge files). **Two service-role branches in `companies` and `dedup-companies` still read the header first and are correct to**: a caller presenting the service-role key already holds every tenant, so naming one there selects a target rather than granting access - both carry that reasoning in a comment, and reading it is what stopped a fifth false write-up.

A HARD GATE AT ZERO IS ONLY AS GOOD AS ITS VERB LIST, AND THE VERB IT WAS MISSING IS THE ONE EVERY POLICY ROW USES (round 125). `check:phantom-cols` matched `.insert(` and `.update(` and not `.upsert(` - and a settings row is written with "create it or change it" by nature, so **58 upsert calls across the edge tree had never been checked**. One word in two regexes took it from a clean run to **17 phantom columns in three files**, every one a guaranteed 42703 the moment its branch runs. Before trusting a gate that reports zero, read what it MATCHES on, not what its header says it is for; and when a gate's scope widens, the count jumps for a reason that belongs in the note beside it. Four findings came out of it and they are four different failure shapes. **THE ONE WORTH KNOWING IS THAT A PHANTOM WRITE TAUGHT A SECURITY CONTROL WHICH COLUMNS TO HIDE.** SEC-EDGE-001 round 74 gave `manufacturer-integrations` a redactor naming seven credential columns - `api_key`, `api_secret`, `client_id`, `client_secret`, `access_token`, `refresh_token`, `webhook_secret` - copied from the `connect` branch's upsert, which had been writing those same seven phantom columns since it shipped. `manufacturer_integrations` has never had one of them, so `column in view` was false every time: the redactor deleted nothing, added no `*_set` marker, and handed back the row unchanged. The dealer's HP, Canon and Xerox keys are one level down inside the NOT NULL `credentials` jsonb that `_shared/manufacturer-adapters.ts` reads as `.apiKey`/`.clientSecret`/`.accessToken`/`.password`, and **Express returned the raw Drizzle row too**, so the leak the story recorded as closed was open on both hosts. `shared/manufacturer-integration-view.ts` is the replacement and its first rule is the one that would have prevented this: **AN ALLOW-LIST OF WHAT LEAVES, NOT A DENY-LIST OF WHAT IS HIDDEN**, because a deny-list is only as good as its author's memory of the schema and fails OPEN - a column it forgets is a column it publishes. `credentialKeys` plus `credentialsSet` answer "which credentials are configured" without carrying one; the internal `resolveIntegration` keeps the raw row, because the adapter needs the real key; and the view camelises, since the page reads `integrationName`/`authMethod`/`isActive` and only Express was answering in that spelling. **CHECK WHETHER A SIBLING'S REDACTOR IS AIMED AT REAL COLUMNS BEFORE QUOTING IT AS PRECEDENT** - this file already records `manufacturer_connections` and `signatures` as redacting correctly, and the third member of that family was redacting nothing.

THE SECOND FINDING IS A GUARDRAIL NOBODY COULD CONFIGURE (round 125). `supabase/functions/proposals` reads `company_pricing_settings.max_discount_percentage` for QUOTE-016's discount ceiling, and its own rule is that 0 or absent means NOT ENFORCED. The only writer of that table production has is `supabase/functions/pricing`, which upserted `default_company_markup_percentage`, `default_minimum_margin_percentage`, `require_approval_below_minimum`, `auto_calculate_prices` and `pricing_currency` - none of them a column, four of them `undefined` and dropped by `JSON.stringify` before the request left, the fifth enough on its own. Its GET then answered a hand-written defaults object under those same phantom names rather than creating the row, so **no tenant had a policy row, no quote had a ceiling, and `/pricing/settings` displayed 20% as though there were one**. A displayed ceiling that nothing is checked against is worse than a blank field. `shared/company-pricing-settings.ts` holds the contract for both hosts, and **THE BOOTSTRAP INSERT SUPPLIES `tenant_id` AND NOTHING ELSE** - Postgres applies the declared column defaults, so there is no second copy of the ceiling in code to drift; Express restates eleven of them, which IS a live duplicate, so a test asserts it still agrees with `getTableConfig`. Three more defects on the same row. `POST /pricing/company-settings`, the spelling `PricingManagement.tsx` uses, had no edge branch at all and 405'd in production under a mutation with no `onError`, so Save did nothing and said nothing. The edge write had **no role check**, so any tenant member could raise their own discount ceiling and switch on dealer-cost visibility - and the Express `company-settings` POST was ungated too, while `PUT /api/pricing/settings` required `canEditDealerCost`: **one row, two doors, one lock**. And `PricingSettings.tsx` never saw the stored settings on EITHER host, because both `useState` initialisers read a query that is still loading on the first render and keep that value for the life of the component - so the page showed 13%/20%/5% as the company's policy and Save wrote them over whatever was stored. Destructive in dev; in production only the phantom column stopped it.

A THIRD WRITER OF A CONTESTED ROW IS RETIRED, NOT REBOUND (round 125). The other two findings were both in `supabase/functions/settings/`, which has **no caller in any of the seven client trees** and sits in `docs/unreferenced-edge-fns-baseline.json`. `PUT /settings/user` upserted a `settings` jsonb column `user_settings` does not have (its columns are flat - theme, language, timezone, plus `notifications`/`accessibility` jsonb), and `PUT /settings/dashboard` wrote `dashboard_layouts.layout`, which migration 0002 dropped. Rebinding the second would have made it the THIRD writer claiming "the one custom layout per user", which this file already records as an open defect rather than a pattern to follow. Both answer **410 with a code naming the canonical endpoint** (`USE_USER_SETTINGS` -> `supabase/functions/user/`, `USE_DASHBOARD_USER_LAYOUT` -> `dashboard-widgets`' `/user-layout`), and both targets were checked to exist rather than assumed: 410 says the capability moved, 501 says nobody built it, and 501 is how a second reader of one concept gets built twice (MEETINGS-READS-001).

A CONVINCING FABRICATED WRITE CALLS THE REAL API AND THROWS THE ANSWER AWAY (round 126). Working the route-divergence worklist, `quickbooks` was filed unexamined on a guess (the phantom-`config` class QUALITY-002 found on `system_integrations`); the real finding was larger and is the sharpest instance yet of the shape MEETINGS-READS-001 named. **NOTHING ANYWHERE WRITES A QUICKBOOKS CONNECTION TO A TABLE.** The Express OAuth callback puts the access token, the refresh token and the company id in `req.session`, under its own comment "Store connection details (in a real app, save to database)"; the edge function reads `integrations` and `quickbooks_mappings`, both of which had sat in `docs/phantom-tables-baseline.json` against that exact file the whole time. Nobody had joined "this table does not exist" to "therefore the status endpoint is lying" - the same failure to connect two recorded facts that CLAUDE.md already records for `system_alerts` and for `today-dashboard`. `GET /quickbooks/status` **discarded its error and answered `{ connected: false }` at 200**, so a 42P01 on every call rendered as a red "Not connected" badge and a Connect button, for every dealer in production, whether or not they had connected - and the Connect button leads to a branch that has 501'd since EDGE-002h. **False is a measurement and null is "we cannot look", and the two must not render the same**: the page now types `connected: boolean | null`, suppresses both connection controls when the server says unavailable, and prints the server's own reason.

**THE SYNC HALF COSTS DATA RATHER THAN CONFIDENCE, AND THE TWO HOSTS FAILED DIFFERENTLY.** The three edge branches inserted a `pending` row into `integration_sync_logs`, discarded that insert's error too, and answered `{ success: true, message: 'Customer sync initiated' }` beside a comment saying "In production, this would call the QuickBooks API" - no call was ever made. The two Express branches are the convincing version: they really call Intuit, really transform what comes back, and then `// In a real implementation, save to database here` followed by "Successfully synced 412 customers". **A real count, off a real API call, with no row written** - so a developer testing in dev sees a plausible number and concludes the integration works, which is precisely why this survived. Three guards watch fabricated READS and none watches this: there is no `|| literal`, no `Math.random` and no static JSX, because the success IS the evidence. `POST /sync/items` was also the one entity the page posts and the only one with no edge branch, so it 404'd while `invoices` and `payments` - which nothing calls - were served: **the endpoint set was the mirror image of the caller set**, worth checking whenever a function looks well covered.

`shared/quickbooks-availability.ts` IS IMPORTED BY BOTH HOSTS, AND CARRIES TWO CODES RATHER THAN ONE. `CONNECTION_NOT_STORED` needs a credential store plus moving OAuth state off the session; `SYNC_NOT_IMPLEMENTED` stays true even once a connection exists, so collapsing them would tell the next reader that one fix closes both. **501, not 503**: the `isMissingTableError` convention promises that a 503'd request will work once the relation exists, and here the feature was never built - which is what `/quickbooks/connect` already answered and what the two codes say out loud. Both hosts refusing identically is the point rather than a tidiness: a dev seeing a different answer from a dealer is how this lasted. The transform helpers are kept and still exported, because what is missing is the step that stores what they return, and deleting them would retire the idea with the defect.

CHEAP SCHEMA CHECK OVER AN EXPENSIVE ONE, PRE-EMPTIVELY (round 126). The first draft of that story's test asserted the two tables are undeclared through drizzle's `getTableConfig`, which means `await import('@shared/drizzle-schema')` inside a unit test - and round 118 records that exact import breaking an unrelated file nine positions later in the full run. Swapped to reading `shared/*.ts` and the migration chain as TEXT before running the suite rather than after discovering it, with two safeguards the text form needs: the table name is anchored on both sides (`integrations` otherwise matches `platform_integrations` and `system_integrations` - round 93), and the walk carries a FLOOR that requires a table which IS real to be found by the same pattern, so a regex that stops matching cannot report both tables as absent.

A 501 THAT NAMES A FALLBACK IS A CLAIM ABOUT THE OTHER HOST, AND NOBODY HAD RUN IT (round 127). `POST /rbac/seed` answered "not ported yet... it is a one-time initialisation that dev can still run through Express in the meantime" - and the Express handler died on its first INSERT, so **role management has never been initializable on any host**. `GET /rbac/status` gates the whole page on `enhanced_roles` holding a row for the tenant and that endpoint is the only writer, so the setup prompt's button has been dead for every tenant since it shipped, which matters more since LAUNCH-008 (round 116) made self-service signup actually provision one. **Three independent reasons, and each is a class worth recognising.** It omitted `lft`, `rght` and `depth` on BOTH tables plus `organizational_tier` and `created_by` on `enhanced_roles` - every one NOT NULL with no default, and the first three are a nested set that both read branches `.order('lft')` on, so the hierarchy they promise was an ordering over nulls. It wrote `hierarchy_level` as 'COMPANY'/'REGIONAL'/'LOCATION'/'DEPARTMENT'/'INDIVIDUAL' where `role_hierarchy_level` is `level_1..level_8`, and `unit_type: 'COMPANY'` where `organizational_tier` is lowercase: **migration 0072's defect (round 89) in handler code, where `check:migration-enums` cannot reach it**. And four of its ten role codes - OWNER, MANAGER, SERVICE_TECH, ADMIN_ASSISTANT - appear in that 45-role catalogue nowhere, so even a working insert would have created a third role vocabulary beside `roles` and `enhanced_roles`. **Verified against migration 0000's CREATE TABLE and the CREATE TYPE lines, not against the Drizzle declaration** (round 88: when the two disagree the live shape wins, so check the chain before writing a defect up).

`shared/rbac-seed.ts` BUILDS THE ROWS FOR BOTH HOSTS, and every number in it is derived. **THE CATALOGUE DECIDES THE LEVEL**: `hierarchy_level` is `level_${catalogue level}` and a template code the catalogue does not carry is REFUSED, because a seeded role's level is what every gate in the product compares against and inventing one hands out access nobody granted (COP-B00). The tier comes from `roles.role_type`, which 0072 already records. The nested set is computed by hanging each role off the nearest preceding one of a higher level - a derivation from data the template already carries rather than a reporting structure nobody specified. **THE GATE IS THE GLOBAL `roles` LEVEL, NOT `enhanced_roles`**: a tenant reaching this endpoint has no `enhanced_roles` row by definition - that is the condition it exists to fix - so gating on one is circular and would hand the top role to whichever member clicked first; `users.roles.level` is what signup grants the tenant's creator and what `assign-role` in the same file already trusts. Worth copying wherever a bootstrap endpoint gates on the thing it creates.

**A PHANTOM TABLE CAN BE BOTH WRITTEN AND READ, AND THE TWO FAIL DIFFERENTLY** (round 127). `rbac_audit_log` is in no schema and had sat in `docs/phantom-tables-baseline.json` against `supabase/functions/rbac/index.ts` all along. The assign-role branch inserted into it WITHOUT destructuring the result, so every role assignment succeeded with its audit entry going nowhere; `GET /audit-logs` read it, discarded its error and answered `[]` at 200, which on a role-management screen reads as "nobody has changed anyone's access" rather than as an audit trail that does not exist. Both go through `audit_logs` now - the write via `_shared/audit-log.ts`, the read ordered by **`timestamp`, not `created_at`** (COP-M01), because rebinding to the right table and the wrong column would have swapped a 42P01 for a 42703. **Tighten the phantom-tables baseline in the same commit**, or the entry keeps asserting a defect that is fixed.

A DERIVED TEST CAUGHT A BUG IN THE MODULE IT WAS WRITTEN FOR, BEFORE IT SHIPPED (round 127). The nested-set walk computed `const lft = counter++` and never put it on the row, so `lft` was `undefined` on every seeded role - the exact column the original omitted, reintroduced by the replacement. Four assertions went red at once: the NOT NULL sweep derived from migration 0000, the explicit lft/rght/depth check, "bounds are unique, paired and nested" (which printed `[4, 6, 7, 8, undefined, ...]`), and the parent-contains-child check. A test that reads the migration for its expectations catches what a hand-written list of columns cannot, because the hand-written list would have been copied from the same wrong row.

TWO RATCHETS WENT RED ON CORRECT NEW WORK AND BOTH WERE RIGHT (round 127). `check:swallowed-writes` caught the rollback delete in the new seed discarding its result - and it matters: a cleanup that itself fails leaves the company unit behind, and the NEXT attempt refuses on it as ALREADY_SEEDED, so the caller has to be told rather than left to rediscover it. `check:error-shape` caught the rewritten Express handler answering `res.status(...).json({ error })` in four places. Neither was a false positive and neither wanted a baseline entry; **a guard firing on code you just wrote is the cheapest time it will ever fire.**

TWO COUNTS PRINTED SIDE BY SIDE BECAME ONE FACT, AND THIS FILE RECORDED THE WRONG ONE (round 128). `check:raw-fetch` splits its findings into LIVE (the file is reachable from App.tsx, so a user can trigger the call) and DEAD (nothing imports the file), and **it baselines the LIVE ones only** - `allowed` is built from `liveFindings`. Its summary printed `(${allowed.size} baselined, ${deadFindings.length} on unreachable files)`: two disjoint sets, and at the moment both happened to be **9**. CLAUDE.md read that as one sentence and recorded "took this from 37 sites to 9, **all of them now on unreachable files - no reachable page makes one**". Every one of the nine was on a ROUTED page and broken in production - among them `IntegrationHub`'s Connect button, `TenantManagement`'s create-tenant, and the two DoD validators on the quote and proposal builders. `node scripts/check-raw-api-fetch.mjs --list` prints the split and settles it in one command. **When a guard's summary puts two numbers in one parenthesis, check which set each describes before quoting it** - and if you are writing one, name what each number counts, because the reader will not re-derive it.

FOUR OF THE NINE CONVERTED, AND THE OTHER FIVE GOT REASONS RATHER THAN A CONVERSION. SEO-TRANSPORT-001's rule decides which is which: converting a page's transport sends the bill to the other host, so a bare `fetch` whose edge branch does not exist turns a request that silently went nowhere into a 404 - better, and not a fix. Checked one by one against the function production resolves the prefix to: `auto-supply-replenishment/analyze-all`, `contract-renewal/analyze-all` and both `validate/*` branches exist, so those four are `apiRequest` now; `/api/auth/check-disposable-email` (no edge directory at all), `integrations/oauth/init` (no oauth branch), `leads/import-eda` (Express-only by design), `platform-crm/.../bulk/assign` and `admin/tenants` (POST covers ORG_TABLES, not tenants) do not. **`docs/raw-api-fetch-baseline.json` carries a `reasons` map now and the guard REFUSES an entry without one**, the `docs/edge-rbac-triage.json` treatment: a flat list reads the same whether a call site was examined and found unportable or whether nobody looked, which is exactly how nine broken ones sat here being quoted as harmless. The writer preserves both the note and the reasons on `--update-baseline` (round 82) and drops a reason whose call site is gone, so the map cannot rot into pre-forgiveness. Two conversions also dropped an `x-tenant-id` built from `localStorage.getItem('currentTenantId')` - AUDIT-001's caller-chosen tenant - which `apiRequest` plus `resolveTenantId`'s app_metadata-first order makes unnecessary and unsafe.

MOUNT ORDER PICKS A WINNER ACROSS ROUTERS, AND THE PROOF IS TO RUN EXPRESS (round 128). `/api/integrations` has THREE Express files and two are mounted, one line apart: `routes-integrations.ts` at `routes-registry:386` registers `GET /api/integrations/:id`, and `integrations/routes.ts` at `:387` registers `GET /api/integrations/marketplace`. Express matches in registration order, so the marketplace catalogue is served by the `:id` handler with `id = 'marketplace'`; `POST /oauth/init` survives only because the shadowing handler is a GET. **Proven by mounting both in that order and issuing the two requests**, not by reading - the same method round 117 used for `req.path` under a mount prefix, and `check:route-shadowing` reports nothing here because the collision is across routers rather than within one. Nothing in `client/src` calls `/marketplace`, so it is latent. The third file, `routes-integrations-real.ts`, is 320 lines with **no importer anywhere** and sits in `docs/server-orphans-baseline.json` - a name that says "real" on the one of the three nobody mounted.

A COMMENT NAMING A DEFECT OUTLIVES THE FIX (round 128). `routes-registry.ts` carried "The real calendar surface is CalendarProvider, which uses a different prefix entirely (`/api/integrations/calendar/*`) that neither backend serves" - true when written, and PA-052 had since repointed that component at `/api/meetings/calendar/*`. It read as a live finding and cost a detour. When you fix something a comment elsewhere describes, grep for the path you just retired; this file already records the same class for story notes and ratchet annotations, and a mount-table comment is no different.

AN UNAUTHENTICATED CALLBACK TOOK ITS TENANT FROM THE CALLER'S OWN STRING, AND ITS ONE CHECK COULD NEVER PASS (round 129). `GET /api/integrations/:provider/callback` carries no JWT by construction - an OAuth redirect cannot - and its whole validation was `const [tenantId, providerId] = state.split('-')` followed by `if (provider !== providerId)`, under a comment reading "you might want to implement more robust state validation". Three defects, each sufficient. **It never compared the state to the one it issued**: `POST /oauth/init` wrote `req.session.oauthState` and nothing ever read it, so the one thing a state parameter exists for was not done, and the check present compares two fields the caller controls against each other. **The tenant came out of a query parameter** and went straight into the code that stores the returned OAuth tokens - SEC-EDGE-001's "a caller-supplied id is an authorization decision wearing a filter's clothes", with no authentication in front of it at all. **And it could not succeed anyway**: a tenant id is a uuid, so `split('-')[1]` is its second hyphen group - four hex characters - never `google-calendar` or `salesforce`. Verified by running it across all five configured providers rather than reasoning, and every one gives `provider !== providerId`, so every genuine callback redirected with `error=invalid_state`. **The feature had never completed a connection, and the tautological check was the only thing keeping the unauthenticated write unreachable** - which is the uncomfortable half: the bug was the control.

`shared/oauth-state.ts` ENCODES FOUR RULES AND THE FIRST IS THE ONE THE OLD SHAPE COULD NOT SATISFY. **The state carries no information** - 32 random bytes, with tenant, user and provider read from the record stored beside it, because a state you can parse is a state an attacker can write. It is **compared in constant time**, since a compare that returns on the first differing byte leaks how much of a guess was right. It is **single use**, cleared before the write, so a callback replayed from a browser history entry or a proxy log is refused rather than storing a second connection. And it **expires** in ten minutes - longer than any consent screen, shorter than a walk away from the desk. The edge function answers **501 `OAUTH_STATE_IS_SESSION_BOUND`** on both halves rather than 404ing, the `/quickbooks/connect` precedent, placed ABOVE the trailing 405 or it would never run; and IntegrationHub's Connect button is `apiRequest` now, so production shows that refusal instead of parsing the SPA shell into a syntax error the catch swallowed into `console.error`.

CHECK THE BACKLOG'S OWN GATES BEFORE PICKING ROUND N+1's STORY (round 129). Seventeen open priority-1 stories, and reading them rather than the list found that the two that looked most tractable - LEGAL-006 and LEGAL-009 - are **blocked on a credentialed human action and say so in their own notes** (`npm run storage:audit` and `scripts/report-recording-consent-coverage.ts` both need `SUPABASE_SERVICE_ROLE_KEY`), while COP-E02, E03, E04, M03 and WF-S-01 all wait on COP-B00, whose unblocking step is one production run of `crm:reconcile`. That is five P1s behind one credentialed action, honestly recorded - so the tractable work is the defect worklists, not the story list. **A story blocked on a credential is not a story to re-implement**, which is what LEGAL-006's note says in as many words; the wasted move is re-doing the engineering half to feel productive.
